And on a LaTeX book, `--latex-only` say, it wrote the default page and none of the book's colors, which `convert.py` passes in its environment (`BOOK_LATEX_LAYOUT` and `BOOK_LATEX_COLORS`), and LaTeX stops on a formula naming a color nothing defines (OpenIntro's). `latex_book` reads them as `convert.py` does when they aren't passed, from the masters `latex.main` chooses: `latexsource.chosen_masters`, which `convert.py`'s `latex_masters` now uses too, its messages the same, and `latexsource.book_page`, which `prepare` uses, each color statement once, as `convert.py` passes them. A book that isn't LaTeX has no master, as `convert.py` finds none, and gets the default page. The PDF suite's preamble book is built again by hand, from the folder above it with `-d` relative; the LaTeX suite's customized book, OpenIntro's shapes, with `--latex-only` and none of `convert.py`'s environment, keeps its 11pt type, its margins, and a color of its own; the checks fail under four mutations, one of them a binder taken for a master, which the LaTeX suite's binder check catches through the code the two now share.

46. **The page for WCAG 1.4.8 (AAA) counts spacing from the type's size.** Rob, of the two readings (item 40): "It sounds like 1.5 times the type's size is defensible, so go with that." W3C's technique C21, one of the criterion's sufficient techniques, which W3C says meet it, measures space-and-a-half from the type's size (CSS's `line-height: 150%`), where W3C's explanation of the criterion measures it from single spacing, "the default spacing for the font", 1.2 times the type's size in LaTeX; only the criterion's own words are normative, and they don't say. The page now: 13.5-point type as before; lines 1.5 times the type's size apart, the default's own (`linestretch: 1.25`); paragraphs 2.5 times it apart, top to top (`\setlength{\parskip}{1em plus 2pt}`); and footnotes' and captions' lines 1.5 times their type's size (`\SetSinglespace{1.25}`). *Introduction to Sociology 3e* is 1,208 pages on it, about 24 percent longer than the default's 975, where the other reading's 1,408 are 44 percent, each built from the same pages, unpacked with item 41's boxes (2026-10-21); the configuration page gives that reading's three lines. The PDF suite's AAA book: lines 1.5 times its 13.45 points apart; the least space between paragraphs, over the pages, within half a point of 2.5 times it, since a full page's gaps are stretched to fill it (`\flushbottom`); and a footnote's and a caption's lines 1.5 times their type's size. Three mutations, each caught, the paragraphs' at 1.2em only once the check allowed half a point either way, where it had allowed three over.

47. **An OpenStax book's PDF is set in its design's face, at 12 points.** Rob, of OpenStax's typefaces (TextbookImprover observations): "Let's follow your plan for adopting the OpenStax typefaces selectively, sticking to 12 pt." The plan: the text faces, which are larger at a given size than Latin Modern and sturdier, and not the heading faces, three of which draw a capital I and a small l alike. `cnx.project`'s `DESIGNS` names each design's face and its styles, as ce-styles' typography settings (`baseFont`) and each style's `book.scss` give them: cosmos IBM Plex Serif (12 styles), carnival IBM Plex Sans (18, *Organic Chemistry* in its design's second face, Plex Serif), and cardboard and corn Noto Sans (16 and 8); `ap-history` and `otto-book`, which have no design there, keep Latin Modern, and a check keeps every style a recipe bakes in a design or in that list. The unpacker writes the face's lines into the book's `pdf-layout.yaml` (`mainfont`), under a comment saying what they do, unless the file names a face or a math font already, so a copy someone edited keeps theirs; the headings, which Pandoc's template sets in the text's face, are its bold. The face is named by its four files (`mainfont: NotoSans`, and in `mainfontoptions` `Extension=.ttf`, `UprightFont=*-Regular`, `BoldFont=*-Bold`, `ItalicFont=*-Italic`, and `BoldItalicFont=*-BoldItalic`), so a file that isn't installed stops the build, which names it: named by its family, Noto Sans was matched by Noto Sans Medium, its bold by ExtraBold, and the build went on without a word, where TeX Live was Debian's, whose `noto` has no Regular, Bold, or Italic, and `fonts-noto-core`, which has them, wasn't installed, as a separate agent's review found with a font database of its own. A Plex Serif book's formulas are in IBM Plex Math (`mathfont: IBMPlexMath-Regular.otf`), whose letters are Plex Serif's. A Plex Sans book's stay in Latin Modern Math, IBM Plex having no math font with Plex Sans's letters, and so do a Noto Sans book's, its line for Noto Sans Math commented out: the Noto Sans Math of Debian's and Ubuntu's `fonts-noto-core` (2.001, in Ubuntu 24.04 through 26.10) has no MATH table, the measurements unicode-math sets a formula by, and given it, unicode-math sets formulas in Latin Modern Math; notofonts' 3.000 has one, and TeX Live has no OpenType Noto Sans Math. It needed two things of the PDF target first. A face a metadata file named got no fallback fonts (`build-pdf.py` gave them only to the families it left at Latin Modern), and Noto Sans lacks the minus sign and most of mathematics' symbols (≤, ≠, ≈, →, ∞, √, ∑, ∫), and IBM Plex ⇒ and the circled letters and digits (ⓐ, ①), so each was left out of the PDF; a named face gets them now, unless the file gives its own (`mainfontfallback` and the rest), and the text's face is set again for babel, whose copy of it Pandoc's template sets the text in when a book has a language (PANDOC-NOTES), and which `\setmainfont` doesn't reach. And a face that isn't installed stops LaTeX, which the run now says, naming the face and where an OpenStax book's come from (TeX Live's `plex` and `noto`, or on Debian and Ubuntu `fonts-noto-core`, which their TeX Live recommends). Measured (2026-10-20), each unpacked and built by `convert.py`, the output check and veraPDF on: *Introduction to Sociology 3e*, whole, is 1,032 pages in Plex Serif and 975 in Latin Modern, built beside it, and nothing is found in either; *Principles of Economics 3e*'s chapter 5 is 39 pages in Plex Serif and Plex Math, and *Chemistry 2e*'s chapter 3 65 in Plex Sans, one display formula 87 points too wide, nothing found in either; and *College Algebra 2e*'s chapter 1 is 136 pages in Noto Sans and 128 in Latin Modern, with 28 lines too wide, the widest by 224 points, where Latin Modern has 14, by 136, and veraPDF fails both on two glyphs of LaTeX's `line10` font, which the cancel package's strokes are drawn with (Open questions). Checked in the CNX suite (a style of each design, named by its files, *Organic Chemistry*'s face, a style with no design, an edited default given the face and one naming its own keeping it, and the converted book's LaTeX setting the face by its files for fontspec and babel, with the fallbacks, and Plex Math for formulas), the PDF suite (the fancy book in Plex Serif, named by its files, its ⇒ drawn), and the LaTeX suite (a missing face named across fontspec's lines); twelve mutations, each caught.

48. **A formula's term struck out is drawn as paths.** Rob, of the `line10` font (Open questions): "Yes, fix the strike-through issue." Pandoc's template loads the cancel package for a formula that names `\cancel`, `\bcancel`, or `\xcancel` (PANDOC-NOTES), which texmath writes for MathML's diagonal strikes, as OpenStax's math books have them, and a deck's template does the same; cancel draws each stroke with picture mode's `\line`, and `\cancelto`'s arrow with `\vector`, glyphs of LaTeX's `line10` font, whose map to Unicode in the PDF has only a space, and veraPDF failed *College Algebra 2e*'s first chapter on two of them (8.4.5.8, in PDF/UA-2 and both WTPDF profiles), in Noto Sans and in Latin Modern alike. pict2e draws picture mode's lines and arrows as paths, with no font. cancel, given pict2e, asks for LaTeX's own commands (`\OriginalPictureCmds`), its comment saying pict2e's have no bounding box, which pict2e's `\line` has now. `latexbuild.CANCEL_STROKES`, which a book's PDF and a deck's read in `PDF_MACROS` and `texremediate.tag` adds to the tagged copy of a LaTeX book that loads cancel, loads pict2e where cancel is loaded, at the end of the preamble (the `begindocument/before` hook, after any pict2e a book or a deck loads with options of its own), and makes `\OriginalPictureCmds` nothing, so cancel draws with pict2e's. pict2e has drawing commands of its own, `\arc`, `\Line`, `\polygon`, `\moveto`, and nineteen more, and loaded so late, as a separate review of the change found when it loaded pict2e for every PDF, it replaced a book's own `\arc`, on which LaTeX then stopped ("Missing number"), and stopped LaTeX on a book's own `\circlearc` and on a `\newcommand` of `\Line` in a book's text ("already defined"; measured on test pages). So those names are set aside before pict2e loads and, at `\begin{document}`, given back as the book had them or taken away, but for `\maxovalrad`, which pict2e's `\oval` reads, and the trig package's `\UseSin` and the rest, which pict2e loads and uses, a book's own of which still stops LaTeX (`\maxovalrad`) or is replaced (`\UseSin`), names a book is unlikely to have; a book that loads pict2e itself keeps all of it; and a PDF with no cancel loads no pict2e at all. A tagged copy made from such a copy, as a `source` target's laid over a book's folder is, gets it once, since its constant twice stops LaTeX ("already defined"). Measured on a page of `\cancel`, `\bcancel`, `\xcancel`, and `\cancelto`, in text, in a script, and displayed, built tagged with and without it and rendered at 300 dpi: each stroke where it was, the pixels that differ (3,739 of 8.4 million) its edges, no `line10` font, and veraPDF's 8.4.5.8 gone; and on pages with a book's own `\arc`, a `\newcommand` of `\Line` in the text, and a book's own pict2e: each the book's. Built again with it, *College Algebra 2e*'s first chapter has its 136 pages in Noto Sans and 128 in Latin Modern, its 24 strokes drawn as paths, each where it was (rendered at 150 dpi, Noto Sans's pages differ only around the strokes), and no `line10` font; veraPDF's three findings are gone from each, and the output check's six others and its lines too wide, 28 and 14, are as they were. Checked in the PDF suite's notes book (a fraction's terms struck out, `\bcancel`, and `\xcancel`: five lines on their page, and no `line10` glyph, tagged or not; and an `\arc` the book defines in its text, its own), the decks suite (a deck with two terms struck out and an `\xcancel`: four lines, no `line10` glyph), and the LaTeX suite (a LaTeX book that loads cancel and defines `\arc` in its preamble: two lines in its PDF from its own LaTeX and from its pages, no `line10` glyph, its `\arc` its own, and the run's report saying so; a tagged copy of a book that strikes a term out, its report, and the copy made again from itself, which builds, its own `\cbezier` its own; a book that loads pict2e itself, its `\cbezier` drawing; and a document with no cancel, no pict2e). Thirteen mutations, each caught: `CANCEL_STROKES` left out of `PDF_MACROS` (by the PDF suite and the decks suite), out of a deck's macros alone, and out of a LaTeX book's copy; cancel still asking for LaTeX's own commands; pict2e's names neither set aside nor given back; a book's own not set aside (by the LaTeX suite, whose book defines `\arc` before `\begin{document}`); pict2e's own of a name the book hadn't left in place; `\cbezier` left off the names; pict2e loaded with no cancel; a book's own pict2e's names set aside too; the copy made from a copy given it again; and each report leaving it out, the tagged copy's and the PDF target's. veraPDF alone wouldn't have caught the first: with the strokes drawn in `line10`, it passed the notes book and the deck, where it failed *College Algebra*'s chapter and the test page on two glyphs each; why it reports some of the font's glyphs and not others wasn't found. A book or a deck that strikes a term out needs TeX's `pict2e`, then, as well as `cancel`, and neither is in TinyTeX's bundle or TeX Live's medium scheme or a smaller one (the bundle of 2026-09 measured; TeX Live's database puts `cancel` in collection-latexextra and `pict2e` in collection-pictures). With the suites' books that strike a term out, the suites need both, which neither the installation page's line nor the suites' lists of what their books load named: Rob's run of the mailbox (2026-10-21) failed 28 checks and stopped the decks suite's case of a deck's PDF, every one on a book or a deck that strikes a term out, as a run here does check for check with both packages moved out of TeX, and two of the LaTeX suite's checks of a book's index skipped, as if `pdftotext` were missing, where the book they read wasn't built. The installation page's first line has both now; the PDF, LaTeX, and decks suites each name either when TeX lacks it, the PDF and decks suites `luacolor` and `lua-ul` too, which their struck-out text loads and TinyTeX lacks, and the unit suite's check that the page installs what the suites name reads the decks suite's list as well; the decks suite's case goes on past a deck that wasn't built, two of its checks that would pass on a deck's empty text failing on one; the LaTeX suite's two checks of a book's index fail where the book wasn't built; and the run's advice, when LaTeX stops on either file, names both, with the command that installs them, where a build that lacks both stops on `cancel.sty`, which the template loads in the preamble, and, once that's installed, on `pict2e.sty`, loaded at `\begin{document}`, a file the book never names (the LaTeX suite's check of it caught three mutations). A separate review of the change found the decks' and the unit check's gaps and the advice. TinyTeX's bundle, brought up as the installation page says (`tlmgr update --self --all`, then the page's lines; LaTeX 2026-06-01 with l3kernel 2026-09-09, newer than this container's 2026-06-18), passes every suite.

49. **What a book in another language needs is written down.** Rob, of a Spanish book's strings and TeX's Spanish (Open questions), asked to make sure "the potential need for additional language support is mentioned in the docs and instructions on adding Spanish given as an example." Two things. TeX's support for the language, for a PDF: babel's file for it, in the language's `babel-` package, without which LuaLaTeX stops with babel's `Unknown option` (measured: a page in Polish, which this TeX Live hasn't, stopped on `Unknown option 'polish'`; a page in Spanish built, `texlive-lang-spanish` installed), and its hyphenation patterns, in its `hyphen-` package, without which babel says so in the log and hyphenates the language by the format's first language's patterns (`\@nopatterns`, `babel.sty`); on Debian and Ubuntu one `texlive-lang-` package has both, `texlive-lang-spanish` Spanish's. `docs/installation.md` says so beside the packages every book needs, with the lines that install Spanish's, and says some languages' packages are named otherwise (Portuguese's are `babel-portuges` and `hyphen-portuguese`); `docs/formats.md` and the project's `language` setting point there. And the port's words: a book in a language other than English or Polish is baked with English ones, which `docs/cnx-input.md` says now, with how Spanish's would come over as Polish's did: its words, cookbook's `lib/locales/es.yml` and the twelve recipes' `locales/es.yml`, beside the Polish ones in `cnx.bake.strings`; its order, CLDR's Spanish (ñ after n), as cookbook sorts by the book's locale with TwitterCldr's collator (`lib/kitchen/patches/i18n.rb`), in a branch of `collation_key` beside the Polish one, its index's groups as they are, ñ's terms under N, since cookbook groups a term by its first letter without the accent in every language but Polish (`I18n.character_to_group`); and its footer, cookbook's "Acceso gratis en openstax.org", in `cnx.project`, which writes the English words for every book. (A Polish book's footer is English too, where cookbook's Polish is "Podręcznik dostępny za darmo na openstax.org", set aside with the Polish books.) When babel lacks a language, the run's advice names it as the book's or a passage's, where it said a passage's, and gives `apt-file search` for Debian and Ubuntu beside `tlmgr`; the LaTeX suite's check of it fails under two mutations, each caught.

50. **A table or a formula too wide for its line is fitted to it, in a PDF.** Rob, of the lines too wide (Open questions): "what is the cleanest way to ensure that everything stays on the 'paper'?", and "can we simply substitute smaller fonts for just the too-wide tables or displayed equations? Maybe try narrowing the spaces first and then going to a smaller font if that's not enough?" So it's done, with LaTeX telling which run over, since a count ahead of it can't: `pdf-target.lua`'s, 70 characters to a line of 10-point type, had set a table whose longest words don't fit a line in `\small` or `\footnotesize`, the first they fit in by the count, which left *Introductory Business Statistics 2e*'s F tables up to 172 points too wide in `\footnotesize`, in 12-point Noto Sans, and set the notes book's table of two long words smaller, though it fits. `pdf-target.lua` numbers each table not in a table's cell, each display formula the writer sets in `\[ \]`, and each formula in text that is an environment, as a worked example's steps are an array, each in a group (`\OERFitTable`, `\OERFitFormula`), and `latexbuild.FIT_MACROS`, in `PDF_MACROS`, measures each as LaTeX sets it: a table by the widths longtable records for its columns (`\LT@save@row`) and, where its columns wrap, by the longest piece TeX can't break of each line of its cells against its column (`post_linebreak_filter`); a formula by what TeX makes of it (`post_mlist_to_hlist_filter`). One too wide is fitted on the next run, as Pandoc runs LaTeX again: its spaces narrowed (a table's columns half an em apart, where they were 12 points; a formula's `\qquad` a quad, an `\mspace` wider than a quad a quad, and an array's columns 0.4 as far apart), and where that's not enough its type set smaller, as much as it takes but to three quarters of its size at least, a table whose columns wrap given columns as wide as its words need. A table or a display formula fitted is centered on the page, its `\LTleft` and `\LTright`, or its `\displayindent` and `\displaywidth`, reaching into both margins, so one still too wide runs into both alike, and one that would come within a quarter inch of the paper's edge even at three quarters is set smaller still, to half its size at least; a formula in text still too wide runs into the margin on its right. A numbered display (`\tag`, as `equation()` writes a book's number), `e` to `pdf-target.lua`, isn't centered: centered, its number was set at the end of a line as wide as the margins allow, past the text's edge; TeX sets the number beside the formula, squeezing its spaces if it must, or on a line of its own. A display under the `fleqn` class option isn't fitted, fleqn setting it in a box as wide as the line, a margin from its left end. On a page of two columns a fit has no margin to run into, since the margin there is the other column, so a formula is set smaller to fit its column, to half its size at least, and what's still too wide runs past it, into the space between the columns or across the other, and is warned of as too wide for its column, not for the paper; a fit made on the second run can leave a formula a few points past its column, a smaller design being wider for its size (below). A book in two columns can't have a table, since longtable stops there, but for a table of the class `float`, which the writer sets as a `tabular` in a `table` float (PANDOC-NOTES), and which isn't fitted: a table is measured by the widths longtable records, and those a floating table's group found were the last longtable's, so that the fit book's floating table, after one too wide for the paper at half its type, was fitted to them and warned of as too wide for the paper, its columns 3 points apart, where they're 12. A display's smaller type keeps the text's line spacing: TeX sets the lines of the paragraph before it as it begins, inside the fit's group, and set with the smaller spacing too they were closer together. A layout table's caption, a paragraph before it, is before the group, in the text's type. A table whose columns wrap, once fitted, isn't hyphenated: in the fit book's table of 22 columns, set at 51 percent, the 6-point design, wider for its size than the 7-point the fit was measured in, left the corner's "Twentytwo" a point wider than its column, and TeX broke it ("Twen-tytwo"), where now it runs a point into the space beside the column. A wrapping table whose spaces between its columns alone fill its line, as a table of 45 columns whose widths are given does on the default page, the writer giving its columns less than no width (PANDOC-NOTES), was left as it was, its numbers printed over one another, and named only among the lines too wide, 48 of its lines; its spaces are narrowed on the first run now, which gives its columns some width, and it's fitted on the second, the fit book's at 61 percent. A wrapping table's columns are worked out with the spaces between them at the size it's set in: counted at the size the run before had, a table set larger on the second run than on the first, as *Introductory Business Statistics 2e*'s F tables are, longtable's first widths under the head across their columns having made them too small, had narrower columns than counted, and in seven of them "denominator" ran 1.6 to 3.4 points past its column (Table A7 at 98.8 percent, where it's at 93.5 now). And a cell's line's longest piece leaves out a box as wide as the line, a head cell's minipage, which had counted every head cell as filling its column. Smaller type isn't proportionally narrower, measured: Latin Modern Roman, the default face, has designs for sizes from 5 to 17 points, each wider for its size than the next larger, 2.2 percent from 12 points to 10, 2.8 from 10 to 9, 3.3 from 9 to 8, and 6.7 from 8 to 7; and unicode-math sets math of 10 points or less, in 12-point type, in its font's forms for subscripts (ssty), 13 percent wider for their size, and of 7 or less in the second level's forms, 38 percent wider, and in 10-point type math of 8 or less in the first. So whatever is too wide is fitted on LaTeX's first run, a formula's spaces narrowed and its type set smaller at once, and on the second each fit is measured again and fitted again where it's off its line by more than 4 percent of it, too wide or too narrow, more than a design's step, or, for a formula in text or a numbered display, which nothing centers, too wide at all. The notes book's table of numbers, in 10-point type, fitted at 86 percent (8.57 points, the 9-point design), is 1.2 points too wide, centered; fitted again, it was at 84 percent (the 8-point design) and 2.7 too wide. And longtable gets some first runs' widths wrong: where a table's head is one row, a cell across columns, it gives the last of those columns the cell's whole width (the F tables' "Degrees of freedom in the numerator", Table A1's last column 621 points), so that they were 816 points too wide on the first run and 254 on the second, which the second run's fit sets right. Nothing is fitted after the second run: fitted on the third as well, *College Algebra 2e*'s first chapter ended its fourth with "Label(s) may have changed", the pages of LaTeX's last run not those of the run its labels, the contents' page numbers among them, were read from. `pdf-target.lua`'s count leaves a table of 25 columns or more in the columns the writer gives one without widths: it had shared out a line with no room left in it, and the writer gave a table of 45 columns of numbers columns of less than no width, its numbers printed over one another (PANDOC-NOTES). The build says what it fitted, by narrowing and in smaller type, names what's still wider than its line, centered, and warns of what's too wide for the paper even in smaller type, or for its column on a page of two, with the smallest size it's in (`latexbuild.fit_report`); a formula still too wide, but a display centered within the margins, is among the lines too wide as well, LaTeX's overfull boxes. A deck's PDF, which `pdf-target.lua` and `PDF_MACROS` build too, fits a slide's table or formula the same way, where the decks suite's slide of a table of twelve columns ran 147 points off it and its formula 234, built with `main`'s code; and the `latex` target's LaTeX carries the marks, which `latexsource.fit_marks` takes out when it's read back as a LaTeX source, since Pandoc's reader expanded them as the preamble defines them and stopped ("unexpected ()"). The `.aux` file counts LaTeX's runs once something is fitted, and as the second from the second on, so that it stops changing: counted as the run, and with nothing fitted too, it changed on every run, so that latexmk stopped the `latex` target's build at its most runs, five, and a deck's build warned that its cross-references were still changing, as each commit's suites, run on the series before delivery, found. And it keeps a digest of each of the book's own `.tex` files LaTeX reads (by a path from its folder, not TeX's own): the main file, each it reads lines of once the fitting's macros are in, a chapter or a file read with TeX's own `\input`, and each read with `\input{}` before them, as a book's header-includes are, which LaTeX's list of files has: a book edited and built again beside the files of an earlier build, as the `latex` target is, had the earlier fits applied by their numbers to what now had them, and nothing new fitted, the count saying no more runs were to fit. A mark of what was numbered, each one's kind, columns, and line, missed what changed without moving, a formula edited on its own line, which kept its old fit, or a master given wider margins, where a formula that had fit was too wide and wasn't fitted; and a digest of only the files LaTeX's own `\input{}` and `\include` read after the fitting's macros missed one input before them and one read with TeX's own `\input`. Now, before any fit is set, a file whose digest differs has the fitting start again. Measured (2026-10-21 and 22), each book's LaTeX run four times as Pandoc runs it, and built whole by `build-pdf.py`, the output check and veraPDF after: *College Algebra 2e*'s first chapter, in Noto Sans, had 28 formulas too wide, the widest by 224 points; 11 fit with their spaces narrowed and 17 in smaller type, two of those, worked examples' steps in text, still too wide at three quarters of their size, by 36 points and by 0.6, so the build names one line too wide where it named 28, and the output check finds what it did (six `pdf-table-header-is-formula`). *Introductory Business Statistics 2e*'s appendix A had 40 lines too wide, the widest by 172 points; its 13 tables too wide are fitted, 5 with their spaces narrowed and 8 in smaller type, Table A1 the smallest, at 83 percent, none of them still wider than the text; no line is too wide, and the output check finds nothing. Checked in the PDF suite's fit book, on the default page (24 checks: a table that narrowed spaces fit, one in smaller type, one left 2 percent too wide by the 9-point design and centered, not fitted again, one centered at three quarters of its type, one set smaller still to stay on the paper, one too wide for the paper at half, its 45 columns as the writer sets them, warned of, a floating table after it in the text's type and spacing, one whose head is a cell across its columns, fitted again on the second run in the text's type and spacing, one of formulas fitted again on the second run, one whose columns wrap, fitted with its spaces narrowed in the text's type, Table A7's second-run fit worked out by `texlua` from its measures, one whose columns' widths are given, a word too long for its column, a layout table, its caption in the text's type, one of 45 columns whose widths are given, narrowed and then fitted, and one of 22 columns, its corner wrapping, kept on the paper with its words whole; display formulas likewise, one in a paragraph whose lines before it keep their spacing, one whose spaces narrowed fit it, one fitted again on the second run, one centered, and a numbered one, its number at its line's end; a worked example's steps fitted again on the second run; nothing fitted on a third run; and the build's report) and its book in two columns under `fleqn` (3 checks: a display left as fleqn sets it, steps set smaller for their column, and the warning of what's too wide for its column), the notes book's two tables, and the untagged book's formula, too wide now for the paper even at half its type and named still; in the decks suite (a slide's table and formula fitted inside its edges, the deck's runs settling, and a deck with nothing to fit settling in two runs, as before); and in the LaTeX suite (the `latex` target's long sum marked, its build by latexmk settling with it fitted, built again after an edit with each formula fitted by its own measure, and after one in place with nothing fitted, and the target read back; and a document of LaTeX's own whose file input before the fitting's macros, and whose file read with TeX's own `\input`, each edited, has the fitting start again); forty mutations, each caught.

51. **A table's notes on its rows are no bands, and leave it no header column.** Rob, of *Prealgebra 2e*'s Table 3.1 (Open questions): "can we detect these cases and give them the right guess up front?" A row one cell across that ends as a sentence does, with a full stop, a question mark, or an exclamation mark, any closing quotation marks or brackets after it, right after a row of the table's cells, in a table that ends with such a row, is a note on the rows above it, not a band over the rows below (`tablecensus.note_rows`). The split doesn't cut the table there, as it doesn't at its foot's rows, and the guess (`explain`) gives a table with a note before its last row no header column, since no cell of a note's row could head it, and a writer cuts a cell across a body's row-head columns (PANDOC-NOTES): `both` is `first-row`; `first-column` is `first-row` where the source marks a header row (`head_rows`: a `<thead>`, a Word table's first row its table look formats, a row marked to repeat) and `none` where nothing does; and `unknown` is `none` where nothing does, but for a table whose header row is below a data row, which stays `unknown`, as one with a marked head does, Word's look making a head of any table. A part's guess, between bands, goes the same way (`guess_table`), and the reason leaves out the clause `table-headers.py` reads as a summary row's, since a summary row's empty label has no column to keep it. A table that opens with such a sentence has no notes, a table of bands (a self-test closed by its total's line, "Add up your scores for your total."), and a line at a table's foot that says where it comes from (`ATTRIBUTION`: "Adapted from …", "Source: …") is no note, nor what makes a table end with one, nor a row a note comes before: a table closed by a note and then its source's line keeps its header column, as it would without the line. Of the 15,935 tables the census was run on, the 12,992 the unpacker writes for the books cloned here and the Word corpus's 2,943, six are guessed otherwise: Table 3.1 (`first-column`, split at its first note) and Table 3.4 (`unknown`, split at its first note), each `none` and whole; their copies in chapter 3's Key Concepts and a table of *Intermediate Algebra 2e*'s section 2.1, all three layout tables; and *Clinical Nursing Skills*' steps in two columns in 8.3, a step across both first and last, `first-row` still and no longer split at its first step. Measured by leaving each out, against the rule as it is, two of its conditions keep tables as they were: a row of cells before the note, 20 (16 of them *Life, Liberty, and the Pursuit of Happiness*' inquiry organizers, sentences under a title), and the table's ending with one, 33 (28 of them worked examples' steps in the algebra books, layout tables); `unknown` made `none` changes 3, Table 3.4, its copy, and *Intermediate Algebra 2e*'s; and the guards for a table that opens with a sentence, an attribution, and a marked header row change none of the 15,935, the cases they're for made up to test the rule, a self-test closed by its total's line or by its source among them. Three tests of a sentence's form, no parentheses round it, three words or more, and not in title case, changed none of the 15,935, so the rule hasn't them. The row Rob declared for Table 3.1, `none`, says what the guess says now. Checked in the census suite (Table 3.1's shape and 3.4's, the steps, a table formatting marks both, a header row Word's table look formats, a header row below a data row, a table no rule recognizes with Word's look, a note within a part between bands, a summary row's empty label, a self-test, plain, cited at its foot, closed by its total's line, and with a header row and its source, a worked example's steps with a sentence across them, an organizer, labels in a table that ends with a note, and a note in the last row alone, with its source's line after it too); thirteen mutations, each caught.

52. **A worked example's steps written as one formula are a table whose reasons wrap.** Rob, of the steps still too wide at three quarters of their size (Open questions): "if we can fix something like the 'step tables' by wrapping the comments/instructions in their column, then that is a better solution for those. Go ahead with that." The emitter makes such a formula a layout table, class `formula-steps`, as the dev-math books' steps are (`steptables.formula_steps`): an `mtable` and nothing else, through what only groups, of rows of cells and nothing else, of two columns or more, but those empty in every row; its first or last column words, each of its cells that holds anything opening with text (an `mtext`, after any space) that holds a word, and one of them two words or more, a word being three letters or more, small after the first, so that a chemical formula's symbols aren't words (NaCl, KBr); math beside them (an `mi`, `mn`, or `mo` outside text); no column between the first and the last with two words in a cell; and not an equation's side written in words beside its relation ("Total damage = …", the economics books' way), a table of values, three columns of math or more, none a relation's, under a column of their names (*Elementary Algebra 2e*'s "Integer | −2 | −1 | 0 …"), or a formula with lines between its rows or columns, a labeled row, or a cell across columns or rows, which the table wouldn't span. Words the source broke over lines, a table of one column each of whose rows opens with text, are one cell's words ("Commutative and associative" over "properties of multiplication", *College Algebra 2e*'s 1.2). Lines labeled in words written as one formula are so too, as *Chemistry 2e* labels a cell's half-reactions ("oxidation half-reaction:"). It's a table where a table can stand, in a paragraph, a list item, or a division, an unnumbered equation's too; not in a table's cell, where a PDF's longtable can't be set, a footnote, a caption, a heading, or a numbered equation. Each cell of math is a formula of its own, in display style where the `mtable` set its cells so (which Pandoc's reader drops, PANDOC-NOTES, so every output sets them in text style, as it set the `mtable`'s), each column aligned as the `mtable` aligned its first cell holding anything (the HTML reader takes a column's alignment from the first row, PANDOC-NOTES), and the space that set it apart from the words beside it, an `mspace` of 1em to 13.5em in *College Algebra 2e*'s first chapter (2em the commonest, 76 times), or an empty text or an operator of a no-break space beside one, left to the table's columns; each cell of words is text, left aligned, its no-break spaces spaces a line can break at, bold or italic where it's set so, its math formulas, and the punctuation an `mo` that ends a run of math is text. A line break right before or after such a table goes, as a solution's lettered parts break the line between the letter and the formula. The run counts the tables. In the 42 of OpenStax's repositories here, 852 in 18 books, lines labeled in words among them (*Chemistry 2e*'s half-reactions, *Calculus Volume 3*'s "Normal vector:"): *Algebra and Trigonometry 2e*'s 197, *College Algebra with Corequisite Support 2e*'s 165, *College Algebra 2e*'s 160, *Precalculus 2e*'s 142, *Elementary Algebra 2e*'s 43, *Intermediate Algebra 2e*'s 35, *Calculus* Volume 1's 30, Volume 2's 29, and Volume 3's 17, *Prealgebra 2e*'s 11, *Chemistry: Atoms First 2e*'s 8 and *Chemistry 2e*'s 7, *Fizyka dla szkół wyższych* Volume 1's 2 and Volume 3's 1, *University Physics* Volume 1's 2 and Volume 3's 1, and *Algebra 1*'s and *Microbiology*'s one each (each book unpacked with the run's count). In a PDF, where LaTeX doesn't lay it out by its own measure (item 56), `pdf-target.lua` sets such a table at its own width where the line has room for it, and where it hasn't, each column of math as wide as its widest formula and a tenth more (`MATH_ROOM`), the words wrapping in what's left of the line, no narrower than their longest word or the widest formula among them and a tenth more, since it doesn't break either; where the formulas and the longest words together are wider than the line, the words are as wide as their longest and the formulas share what's left by their counts, and only steps whose longest words alone fill the line are set as any other table (`steps_widths`). Seven of *College Algebra 2e*'s first chapter's 51 tables of steps share so; set as other tables are, six of those seven would be set in smaller type to fit their formulas to their columns, and shared so, three are. A formula in such a table doesn't break over lines: `Table` sets `\binoppenalty` and `\relpenalty` to 10000 in the table's group, since TeX breaks a formula in text after an operator or a relation and FIT_MACROS measures a column by the longest piece TeX can't break, so that a formula wider than its column is fitted, not broken, as without them one of the steps of Example 6 (e), in section 1.1, would be. Pandoc's writer draws a rule above such a table and one below (`\toprule`, `\bottomrule`), as it does every table, where the formula had none. It counts a formula's width with `formula_width`, closer than `formula_length`, which counts a fraction's two parts one after the other and space around every operator, a script's and a formula's first: measured against LaTeX, each of the 301 step formulas in *College Algebra 2e*'s first chapter set in a box in Latin Modern Math at 12 points, `formula_length` counted them at 1.76 times their width (the median; 2.38 at the ninetieth percentile, and 4.09 the most, fractions), `formula_width` at 1.23 (1.27 at the ninetieth percentile, none under 0.93), an operator or a relation a command names, as `\leq`, as wide as `=`, but a dot, a function's name, as `\sin`, by its letters and one more for the space TeX sets beside it, where it had counted it one character, as `formula_length` does (`\sin x \cos x \tan x \cot x \sin y`, 129 points wide as LaTeX sets it in Latin Modern at 12 points, 22 of the filter's characters, 70 to a line of 10-point type, was counted 10, and is 25 now), but no space before an opening delimiter, where TeX sets none (`\ln(x)`, 4.4 characters wide, was counted 6, and is 5), though one before `\left(`, which makes a group TeX sets apart (`\log\left( x \right)` is 2 points wider than `\log(x)` at 12 points); limits set under something, as `\underset` and `\lim\limits` set them, as wide as the wider of the two, where it had counted them beside it, and a relation so set, as `\overset{?}{=}`, as a relation, which amsmath makes it; and an escaped symbol, as `\{`, and a text's spaces counted too, but not a space in `\mathrm`, where TeX sets none. With `formula_length`'s count, the chapter's steps that fit their line were set across it, their words wrapped, and a formula broke over its column's lines. The tenth more is the book's face's: Pandoc's template scales Latin Modern Math to the height of the text's small letters (`Scale=MatchLowercase`), and Noto Sans's are 24 percent taller than Latin Modern's (each of the 301 measured in both), so `formula_width` counts them at 0.90 of their width in the book's PDF, the median of the 205 that aren't an equals sign alone (0.81 at the tenth percentile, 0.75 the least; 0.99 with the 96 that are, which the count takes about right). Without it, 10 of the chapter's 51 tables of steps would have a formula wider than its column, 9 of them set smaller to fit it; with it, 4 have, 3 of them set smaller, at 92, 93, and 96 percent of their size, which are wider than the line even in columns as wide as LaTeX measures their widest formula or word. Of the parts tried for it, 1 to 1.3, a tenth is the least that leaves the fewest too wide for their columns, modeled from LaTeX's widths of each formula and word, which model each table as LaTeX measures it. *College Algebra 2e*'s first chapter, built with `main` and with this, in Noto Sans: with `main`, 28 formulas fitted, 11 by narrowing their spaces and 17 in smaller type, two still too wide at three quarters of their size, by 36 points and by 0.6, on 136 pages; with this, 6 fitted, 1 by narrowing its spaces and 5 in smaller type: 4 tables of steps, one with its spaces narrowed (Example 9 (d), the steps 36 points too wide) and three at 92, 93, and 96 percent of their size, and 2 formulas, one at 80 percent and the one 0.6 points too wide (Example 12 (e)) at three quarters, still 0.6 points too wide, on 139 pages. Seven of the chapter's 20 tables of steps set across the line would fit it at their own width, their words on one line, as LaTeX measures their cells: the filter counts a line as 70 characters (`LINE`), the book class's at 10 points. In the chapters whose steps hold functions' names and limits, modeled the same way, no more tables have a formula or a word wider than its column than with a count that spaced a function's name before a parenthesis and set limits beside it, and fewer in *Calculus*: in *Precalculus 2e*'s chapters 4, 7, 8, and 12, 52 of 99 tables of steps are set across the line, 5 of them so, 1 too wide however shared, where 54 were and 5 had; in *Calculus Volume 1*'s chapters 2 to 4, 22 of 28, 4 so, 2 of them too wide however shared, where 6 had; and in *Calculus Volume 2*'s chapters 3 and 5, 28 of 29, 14 so, 5 too wide however shared, where 15 had. Against their width in Noto Sans, their formulas are counted at 0.94 in *Precalculus 2e*'s, 0.90 in Volume 1's, and 0.85 in Volume 2's, the median of the cells of math that aren't an equals sign alone, where the first chapter's are at 0.90 (0.99 with the 96 of its 301 that are, which the count takes about right), so that only *Calculus Volume 2*'s are counted narrower, those with an integral's sign or ∞ in them (105 of its 170 cells of math) the narrowest, at 0.82: ∞, for one, is a character as counted, and 2.03 as Latin Modern Math sets it. These figures for the other books are measured again (2026-10-22, late): the first model's LaTeX ended its paragraph at a blank line in a formula holding a matrix's rows, as texmath writes them, mismeasuring 9 of *Calculus Volume 2*'s 161 formulas, by up to 3.5 times; and it measured a reason's formula as its TeX, in text, which made 4 tables set at their own width look wider than the line. A formula's line breaks are read as spaces now, as TeX reads them, and a cell of words is measured as the writer sets it. On a narrow screen, `page.css` sets a step's cells one after another on a line, wrapping where they don't fit, an empty cell no gap, where a layout table's cells stack, each on a line of its own, the relation alone between its sides (the convert suite measures it in Chromium, 360 pixels wide). Checked in the CNX suite (the shapes made tables and those left formulas, each rule's case one only it decides, their cells, alignments, and words, the space beside the words, the line breaks beside a table, and the places a table can't stand), the convert suite (a step's cells on a line on a narrow screen), and the PDF suite (in the notes book, steps longer than a line, their formulas whole, their words wrapped, and their columns of math a tenth wider than their widest formula as counted, steps that fit set at their own width, and that count's escaped braces, text's spaces, functions' names, and limits; and in the fit book, steps whose formulas and longest word the filter counts wider than a line, which fit it, in the text's type, steps whose longest word leaves their formulas too little of the line, set smaller with their formulas whole, steps of `\leq`, which fit in the text's type, a reason's formula given room for it whole, its column a tenth wider than the formula as counted and the formulas sharing what's left, and steps whose longest word alone is longer than a line set as any other table); sixty mutations, each caught. Not done, each an offer: `formula_width` for every table's formulas, which `formula_length` counts for now; a step table without the rules above and below it; and display style kept in the outputs, a cell marked for it, which the PDF filter and `page.css` (MathML's `math-style`) could read. Of the three, as Rob answered (2026-10-23): the rules taken off (item 58); `formula_width` tried beside LaTeX's measure of every table without widths, and the measure taken (item 61); and display style measured before he decides (Open questions). Done since, by LaTeX's measure (item 56): a table that fits at its own width so set, and its columns of math as wide as LaTeX measures their formulas, which leaves `formula_width`'s calibration on *Calculus*'s formulas, offered here too, needless where LuaTeX sets the PDF, every table of steps in the four books having the one column of words LaTeX lays out. **Two ways to find the line**, modeled for Rob (2026-10-22, late: "Explain further why you're measuring and the implications of one way of measuring vs. the other"), from LaTeX's widths of each formula, cell, and word in the book's preamble and the filter's own decisions and shares, run in `pandoc lua` on each book's pages, on the 51 tables of steps in *College Algebra 2e*'s first chapter, the 99 in *Precalculus 2e*'s four chapters above, the 28 in *Calculus Volume 1*'s three, and the 29 in Volume 2's two. As the filter is: 7, 32, 6, and 7 of them set across the line though they'd fit at their own width, and 4, 5, 4, and 14 with a formula or a word wider than its column, set smaller. With the page's line read from its settings (469.75 points, 79.4 of the filter's characters, less a list's indents, 30 points for one list) and the page's gap between columns (2.03 characters, where the filter counts 3): 0, 7, 0, and 2 across though they'd fit, but 9, 6, 15, and 22 set smaller, since the line counted short, with the gaps between columns counted wide, has given each column of a table set across it 8 to 20 percent more than its count, by the table's columns and whether it's in a list, which covered the formulas' undercount, and `MATH_ROOM` was tuned with it. *College Algebra 2e*'s first chapter built with the page's line alone (`LINE` 79.427): 9 tables of steps set in smaller type, and the 2 formulas fitted with 70 too, where with 70 it's 3 tables of steps in smaller type and a fourth with its spaces narrowed; the model says 10 tables. With LaTeX choosing by its own measure, the filter's shares kept: none across though it would fit, and 4, 5, 4, and 12 set smaller, none that isn't now. With LaTeX choosing and setting each column of math as wide as it measures the column's widest formula: only what's wider than the line however its columns are shared set smaller, 3, 1, 2, and 4. Rob chose LaTeX's measure, its columns of math as wide as it measures them (item 56). **A reason's formula that leaves the math too little** (2026-10-22, late, found while modeling the two ways): where the formulas and the longest words together are wider than the line, the words were given their longest, a reason's formula among them, and the formulas what was left. *Precalculus 2e*'s Example 7 in section 12.2 has a reason holding a formula about as wide as its column of math's widest, the two together 1.3 times the line as LaTeX measures them. Shared that way, its formulas got less than a third of their counts, and the table was set at half its size, 353 points wider than the text, off the paper's left edge (`main` sets the steps as one formula at three quarters of its size, 48 points into the right margin). Where giving the words their longest would leave the formulas less than three quarters of their counts (`FIT_FLOOR`, the least part of its size the fit sets a table in to fit its line), every formula, a reason's too, now gets the same part of its count, and a column of words stays no narrower than its longest word, so that the fit sets them smaller alike. The example is at three quarters of its size, 2 points wider than the text, centered (chapter 12 built with `main`, the series as first delivered, and this: the example at three quarters, half, and three quarters of its size); by LaTeX's measure (item 56), at three quarters, 3.5 points wider, its reason's formula given its width, where the counts' column for it was narrower and TeX squeezed its spaces. A column of words is held at its longest word because a word given less than its width would be broken at a hyphen, where a formula is fitted: given the same part as the formulas, the fit book's 36-letter word was hyphenated. A word is as the filter counts the words of any table's cell (`cell_size`), which counts a piece of a formula between the places TeX could break it as one, though a formula in steps doesn't break. Built with a column held at its words alone, *Calculus Volume 2*'s steps in 3.3, at three quarters of their size, were 108 points wider than the text, where they're 36 as the filter counts words and were 43 before the rule: their reason's formula, counted 9 percent narrower than LaTeX sets it, set their size, where their column of math's widest is counted 23 percent wider. Of every table of steps in the four books (158 in *College Algebra 2e*, 142 in *Precalculus 2e*, 30 in *Calculus Volume 1*, and 29 in Volume 2), modeled as above, the rule changes 5: the example; Volume 2's in 3.3; Volume 1's in 3.6, at three quarters of its size, 26 points wider than the text before and 17 now; the hyperbola's in *Precalculus 2e*'s 10.2, at three quarters of its size, 67 points wider than the text before and 56 now; and the same steps in *College Algebra 2e*'s 8.2, modeled alike. As built, all but the last, each is narrower than before, none in smaller type. A review of the rebased series (2026-10-22, late) found that the rule compared the formulas' part of their counts given `MATH_ROOM` with `FIT_FLOOR`, not their part of their counts, so that it shared alike steps whose formulas would get up to 0.825 of their counts, two tables of the four books': one of *Precalculus 2e*'s in 12.4, which it set at 93 percent of its size, where it's at 94.5, and one of *Calculus Volume 1*'s in 3.9, not built, which the model set at 87 percent so and sets at 86 now, as before the rule; and that steps whose reason's formula alone fills the line were set as other tables are, the reasons' formulas counted among the words. A second review found the hold's count of a formula's pieces pinned by no check, its words alone giving the fit book the same LaTeX, and steps whose reason's formula alone fills the line set at their own width where their cells' length, a function's name a character, fits it, none such in the four books. Each is as described now, and checked. Checked in the PDF suite's fit book: steps whose reason's formula leaves their formulas less than three quarters of their counts share the line in proportion, in the text's type; a little more (0.774 of their counts), the reason given its formula's room, and a little less (0.746), shared alike; steps whose reason's longest is a 36-letter word, and steps whose reason holds a formula and a word wider than the formula's share, hold the reason's column at the word; steps whose reason holds a fraction longer, counted piece by piece as a cell's words are, than its words hold the reason's column at that count; and steps whose reason's formula alone is longer than a line share it in proportion, their cells longer too or, a function's name a character, shorter. The unit suite checks that the filter's `FIT_FLOOR` is the build's. Fifteen mutations of the rule, each caught by a check of its own.

53. **A floating table's tags are where the text has it.** Rob: "double-check that when a table is floated it still gets written into the tag tree as close to its position in the text as possible. IIRC, the default behavior in LaTeX is for everything to get moved to the end of the document, and we are overriding the default to keep things in the section or chapter where they belong." It is LaTeX's default (`latex-lab-testphase-float.sty`, 0.81n: each float's structure kept in a container of its kind, which goes at the end of the document or where `float/flush` puts it), and the PDF target overrode it for figures, which `pdf.figures: in_place` places H, so they don't float, and `section` flushed at each section; but not for a table of the class `float`, which Pandoc's writer sets in a `table` float under every placement (PANDOC-NOTES). Measured on a book with one between two paragraphs of its first chapter: under `in_place`, the default, and `float`, its tags were in a `tables` container after the second chapter's, the document's last, and under `section` after its section, in its chapter. The PDF target now sets `float/here` (`latexbuild.FLOATS_HERE`), as a LaTeX book's tagged copy already did (`texremediate.FLOATS_HERE`, built on it now), and the table's tags are between the two paragraphs under each placement, a figure's where the text has it too; where each is printed doesn't change, and veraPDF passes. `section`'s flushing went, needless with it. Checked by the PDF suite's fit book, its floating table's tags between the paragraphs around it, which without it were after the book's last table, and by what each placement gives LaTeX; one mutation, caught.

54. **A formula's lines in a table's cell are measured as the formula's.** Found by a mutation of item 52's check of a reason's formula, which passed with the reason's column as narrow as its longest word: `FIT_MACROS` measures a table whose columns wrap by the longest piece TeX can't break of each line of its cells (`longest`), a formula's spaces part of the piece from the node that opens the formula to the one that closes it, as TeX breaks a formula only at a penalty, after an operator or a relation; but TeX drops a math node at a line's start, as it drops glue after a break (TeXbook, chapter 14), so a line that opens in a formula, broken before it, has the formula's closing node and not its opening one, and `longest` took the formula's spaces for the spaces between words. A formula TeX can't break, too wide for its column, was measured by its widest piece between two of its spaces and not fitted: on that mutation's build, the reason's formula, on a line of its own, was 14.4 points wider than its column (LaTeX's overfull box) and ran into the margin, its line measured at 0.10 of its column; it's 1.32 now. A line whose first math node closes a formula is taken as math from its start. The second review found a line with neither node measured by its spaces too: the middle of a formula over three lines, or the first line of one that opens a line, its opening node dropped, and breaks after an operator (`\sin x \cos x \tan x \cot x + 1`, 29 points past its column, unfitted); carrying the state from one line to the next wouldn't have caught it, since the line before ends outside the formula. A line with no math node is taken as the formula's where it ends at a penalty, as TeX breaks a formula, and the next line opens in one; a line of words that ends at a space before a formula that opens the next line stays words. One that ends at a penalty set in the text, as `\allowbreak` and `\linebreak` set one, is taken for the formula's and measured whole, which can't make a table too wide but can make one fitted anyway smaller than it needs (the review measured such a line at 0.96 of its column, in a box 100 points wide); Pandoc's writer sets none in a cell, whose line breaks end a paragraph of a `minipage`. Taking only the penalties TeX sets after an operator or a relation (LuaTeX's `noadpenalty`) would leave a formula its author broke at a penalty measured by its spaces, and unfitted where it's too wide, which is worse. In a book's PDF such a line ends with a node closing a text direction after the penalty, before the right skip, which babel's `bidi=basic` puts there and which the review's test, without babel, didn't have: the rule as the review wrote it, passing over glue alone, never found the penalty in a PDF the target builds, and it passes over that node too now. Checked in the PDF suite's fit book: a table whose columns' widths are given, a formula TeX can't break (`\sin x \cos x \tan x \cot x`, no operator or relation between its parts) too wide for its column opening a line of its cell, which ran 20 points past its column, into the margin, unfitted, and is set smaller now, ending within the 4 percent of its column a fit may leave it over; one whose formula opens a line and breaks after an operator (`\sec x \csc x \sin x \cos x + 1`), which ran 27 points past its column and is set smaller now; and a table whose columns' widths are more than its line together, a line of words in a cell before one a formula opens, which keeps its type, its columns narrowed to fit, and which, the line of words taken as the formula's, would be set in type three quarters its size; four mutations, each caught.

55. **A formula's empty row is kept in a PDF.** Found building *Calculus Volume 2*'s chapter 3 for item 52 and offered; Rob: "Also go ahead with the blank-line fix." texmath writes a one-column `mtable` whose last row is empty with an empty line before its end, and an `mtable` of one empty row as an empty line (a wider `mtable`'s empty last row is its `&`s; PANDOC-NOTES), as OpenStax writes an evaluation bar's limits in *Calculus* (an `mtable` under the bar and one over it, a row of each empty), a synthetic division's divisor in *Precalculus 2e*, and an empty row or two as space beside a matrix or a derivation there and in *College Algebra 2e*. TeX reads the empty line as a paragraph's end, and LaTeX's tagging, which reads a display formula's body as an argument, stopped at it ("Paragraph ended before \environment equation* was complete"), where LaTeX without tagging, and a formula in text, don't (measured in a bare `article` with TeX Live 2026). Where LaTeX read on, the row was lost, since a `\\` before an alignment's end makes no row: in a formula in text, as `main` sets one, a bar's upper limit sat 9.5 points above the formula's line and its lower one 28.5 below. `pdf-target.lua` writes the empty line as an empty group, `{}`, which makes the row (`Math`): a bar's limits are 27.6 points above its formula's line and 28.5 below, and a synthetic division's divisor is on its first row, where read as a line end it was between the first two (*Precalculus 2e*'s 3.6, built alone both ways). The draft read the empty line as a line end, which built the chapters but lost the row; the review found it (Round 15 in PROJECT-NOTES). In the four books: *Calculus Volume 2*'s 3 display formulas in chapter 3, 9 in text in its tables of steps there, and 1 in text in chapter 7; *Precalculus 2e*'s 19 display formulas, in chapters 1, 3, 7, 8, and 9 and the answer keys of chapters 7 and 9; *College Algebra 2e*'s 7, in chapter 7 and its answer key; *Calculus Volume 1*'s none; every empty line before a `matrix`'s or an `array`'s end. *Precalculus 2e*'s chapter 7 stops with `main`, at its first (90 s), and builds with this, as Volume 2's chapter 3 does. The PDF suite's new page holds an evaluation bar's limits as texmath writes them, in a display formula and in text; its checks, that the page builds, its LaTeX's four matrices holding no empty line, and that each bar's upper limit is as far above its formula's line as its lower one is below (within 3 points), fail without the change, LaTeX stopping, and with the empty line read as a line end, the limits 9.5 and 28.5 points from the line. Found by the way: the Markdown writer writes the empty line inside `$$…$$`, where the Markdown reader ends the paragraph, so such a formula in a `markdown` target reads back as raw TeX and text (Open questions).

56. **A worked example's steps laid out by LaTeX's measure.** Rob, of the two ways to find the line (item 52, 2026-10-22): "Yes, let's let LaTeX measure and size the columns." Where LuaTeX sets the PDF, as the PDF target and the `latex` target's build do, a table of steps with one column of words, as the unpacker writes them (every one of the four books' 361), is laid out by what LaTeX measures of it, not by the filter's counts: `pdf-target.lua` opens its group with `\OERFitStepsTable` (`steps_spec`), giving its columns' alignments and the counts' part of the line for its words, and FIT_MACROS sets the longtable's columns in place of the writer's (a one-shot `\LT@array` in the group, `oerfit.lt_array`): each column of formulas `l`, `c`, or `r`, as wide as its widest formula, and the column of words `p`, as wide as the run before decided, or on LaTeX's first run the counts' part. Each paragraph of the words is measured as LaTeX sets it, before its lines are broken (`pre_linebreak_filter`): how wide it is as one line, and its longest piece TeX can't break, a formula whole (none breaks in the steps), a fill-in blank no more than three underscores, as it's drawn shorter to fit its line (item 33), and a box as wide as the column, the minipage the writer sets a cell's lines in where they're broken where it says, not at all, its own paragraphs being measured; the formulas by the widths longtable records. On the first run each table is decided (`steps_next`): at its own width, the words on one line, where that fits the line; otherwise across it, the words as wide as the line leaves them, where their longest piece fits; otherwise with its spaces narrowed; otherwise in smaller type, as another table is, three quarters of its size the least to fit the line and half to keep a quarter inch inside the paper's edges (`smaller`, now shared with a table whose columns don't wrap), the words as wide as the line leaves them at that size, or their longest piece. On the second run, steps still wider than their line are decided again from that run's measures, as math set at 10 points or less in its forms for subscripts can leave them (the fit book's Smallsteps: 0.801 of its size on the first run, its math 12 percent wider for its size on the second, then three quarters). A word a smaller design makes wider than its column runs past it, not broken (`\hyphenpenalty` 10000, as in a wrapping table). The counts still give the words their part of the line on LaTeX's first run, and lay out the steps a TeX without Lua sets, those with no column of words or more than one, and those the writer floats, a `tabular`, not a longtable. A longtable whose own specification has another number of columns than `\OERFitStepsTable` says, as an edit of the `latex` target's LaTeX can leave it, is set as written, unmeasured (`oerfit.spec_columns`), where LaTeX would otherwise stop on it. Built in Noto Sans with item 55 alone (`main` stops on two of the chapters) and with this, on the model's chapters, *College Algebra 2e*'s first, *Precalculus 2e*'s 4, 7, 8, and 12, *Calculus Volume 1*'s 2 to 4, and Volume 2's 3 and 5 (51, 99, 28, and 29 tables of steps): the counts set 10, 21, 6, and 7 across the line that fit it at their own width, and the measure none, 41, 79, 12, and 10 at their own width; the counts fitted 4, 5, 4, and 14, their spaces narrowed or their type set smaller, and the measure 0, 1, 2, and 6, where Round 14's model said 3, 1, 2, and 4 (in *College Algebra 2e*'s chapter, only its 2 formulas are fitted, where the counts fitted them and 4 tables of steps); and the counts left three wider than the text at three quarters of their size, *Calculus Volume 1*'s in 3.6 by 17 points, Volume 2's in 3.3 by 36, and *Precalculus 2e*'s Example 7 in 12.2 by 1.6, where the measure fits the first two to the line, at 86 and 80 percent of their size, and leaves the example 3.5 points wider, centered, its reason's formula given its width, where the counts' column for it was narrower and TeX squeezed the formula's spaces to nothing. Six of the other seven the measure sets smaller come out a little wider than the line, by 0.01 to 0.49 points, their math a little wider for its size than at full size (0.15 percent in Volume 2's at 93 percent), within the half point the second run leaves as it is (`TOLERANCE`); the build names none. Each chapter took LaTeX four runs both ways. **Found on the way**: a cell's formula holding an alignment, which `braced_math` puts in braces for LaTeX's tagging, was taken for words (`lone_formula`), so its column was one of words and the table one with two (20 of *College Algebra 2e*'s 160 tables of steps and 38 of *Precalculus 2e*'s 142, OpenStax spacing a step's formula with an empty matrix's `&`), which the counts shared as words and the measure would have left to them; the braces are seen through now. And the `latex` target's LaTeX, read back as a source, has `\OERFitStepsTable`'s group taken out, as `\OERFitTable`'s is (`latexsource.FIT_MARK`), which the LaTeX suite's round trip checks with a table of steps in its book. A review (a separate agent's, of the series as first made) found item 55's draft losing a formula's empty row; checks named for the counts that read the PDF, which the measure lays out, so that none could fail on the counts, `\leq` counted as a symbol passing them all; no check of the columns' alignments; an edit of the `latex` target's LaTeX giving a table of steps' longtable another number of columns than `\OERFitStepsTable` says, which LaTeX stopped on ("Extra alignment tab has been changed to \cr"); the floated steps' check reading another table's group; and figures in comments written when the counts laid out every PDF: each fixed, a fix of code with a check that fails without it. Checked in the PDF suite's fit book, by LaTeX's measure: steps the counts set across the line that fit at their own width, set so, centered, their reason on one line (Ownwidth); steps a little too wide for that set across the line, their spaces as they were (Nearwidth), and steps too wide at their own width, each column of formulas as wide as its widest (Mathwidth); a reason's longest word wider than its count, fitted with spaces narrowed (Wordwide); steps too wide even so set smaller, measured again on the second run, their word whole (Smallsteps), and steps too wide for the paper a quarter inch inside its edges (Paperwide); a reason's fill-in blank (Blankreason), its lines broken where it says (Breakreason), and a formula (Mathpiece) and a matrix (Matrixreason) in it, each measured as above; a formula spaced with an empty matrix taken for a formula (Bracedsteps); a column's cells of different widths aligned as the steps align them (Alignone); a longtable with a column more than its group says, set as written (Editedspec); item 52's steps, which LaTeX finds fit in the text's type, their formulas whole; and steps with two columns of words (Twowords) and floated steps (Floatsteps), left to the counts; and the counts read from the LaTeX, each column's share of the line, item 52's checks, a relation TeX names counted as an equals sign is among them (Leqreason). Thirty-three mutations of the measure and the filter, each caught by a check of its own, run on the final code, six of them of the review's fixes: the alignments' letters dropped at the filter and at the macro, `\leq` counted as a symbol, the edited table's fallback taken out, the table it leaves unmeasured decided, and floated steps opened as another table.

57. **A fill-in blank too long for a table's cell no longer sets the table in smaller type.** Found by the Instrumental Analysis session in the review of its round 7's rebase, and written as `blank-fit.mbox` in its round 8, for another session to take up (PROJECT-NOTES, A LibreTexts book from a web archive); Rob, giving it to this one (2026-10-23): "Is this still an outstanding issue that needs fixing? If so, evaluate the proposed patch and, if it's needed, incorporate it into the new mailbox." `oer.blanks` (item 33) draws a blank shorter once LaTeX has broken its paragraph into lines, in `post_linebreak_filter`, whose functions LaTeX runs last registered first (a reverselist in `ltluatex.lua`), and `PDF_MACROS` wrote `FIT_MACROS` after the blank's Lua, so the fit's measure of a cell's lines (`oer.fit.cells`) ran first, and took a blank at its full length for a piece TeX can't break. Measured in the PDF suite's fit book with c8fb928's measure: a table whose columns' widths are given, a blank of 30 underscores in one of its five (Blankcell), at 87 percent of its size; one whose columns' widths are more than its line together, a blank of 36 underscores in one of two (Overblank, the review's case), at 75; and the mailbox's own case, steps whose reason holds 30 underscores, at 84. So it's still there, but not for steps: item 56 measures a worked example's steps before their lines are made, a blank as three underscores at most, so the mailbox's steps are in the text's type without its change, and its check couldn't fail. The mailbox's change, `FIT_MACROS` written before the blank's block so that the blank's function runs first, isn't taken. It fixes Blankcell, but a blank drawn shorter first ends where its column does, and the fit took it as a piece as wide as its column, which a table being narrowed is set smaller for: Overblank at 83 percent (found by this round's review, an agent's that hadn't seen the work). And on item 56 it moved `FIT_MACROS` ahead of `\newattribute\OERBlankAttribute`, which the steps' measure read where the macros are read: nil, which `node.has_attribute` reads as attribute 0, which luaotfload sets to 0 for every node (`luaotfload-init.lua`; 81 of 81 glyphs on a tagged test page), so every run of underscores would count as a blank, right for a blank only by accident, every check passing. Instead the fit's measure of a cell's lines counts a blank as three underscores at most, as the steps' measure does, the shortest `oer.blanks` draws it (`longest`), and the fit's function still runs first (written after it, the blank's function would put kerns between a blank's underscores, which the count doesn't skip); and both measures take no run of underscores for a blank where the blank's attribute isn't there (`blank and node.has_attribute(n, blank)`), as where the LaTeX suite reads `FIT_MACROS` alone. Checked in the fit book, each failing without its part of the change: Blankcell and Overblank in their type, each blank drawn shorter to its column, which fail with a blank taken whole, at 87 and 75 percent, Overblank also with the blank's function run first, at 83, whether or not the count is there; and steps whose reason holds a run of underscores underlined, which isn't a blank and which nothing draws shorter, measured with the run whole, set at 89 percent within the line (Underreason), which fails with the steps' measure taking any run for a blank, the steps across the line in the text's type, the run 62.8 points past it. A table's cell holding such a run has no check of its own: fitted for it, the run ends 3.2 points past its column (built with 12 to 30 underscores), within the 4 percent a fit may leave a table off, and the build names it as a line too wide. In OpenStax's sources, 1,705 table cells hold a run of four underscores or more (and 125 cells of math, where a run isn't marked), counted by the book that prints them, a module in each of its books: *Statistics* 548, *Introducción a la estadística* 525, *Introductory Statistics 2e* 169, *Algebra 1* 125, *College Algebra with Corequisite Support 2e* 77, every one in a section of corequisite skills, which OpenStax's recipe for *College Algebra 2e*, *Algebra and Trigonometry 2e*, and *Precalculus 2e* takes out, *Prealgebra 2e* 68, *Organizational Behavior* 51, *Introductory Business Statistics 2e* and its Spanish edition 38 each, *Elementary* and *Intermediate Algebra 2e* 22 each, *Writing Guide with Handbook* 20, and two books one each; whether a blank is too long for its column turns on the face and the column. *College Algebra with Corequisite Support 2e*'s chapter 7, built both ways in its design's face, Noto Sans, has 11 tables with blanks of 8 to 40 underscores, each of which fits its column, nothing drawn shorter, the fits the same both ways; and *Writing Guide with Handbook*'s 10.5, built alone in its design's face, IBM Plex Serif, has a table for drafting a position argument, of one column, whose eight blanks are seven of 68 to 77 underscores, all but one longer than the column, and one of 8: the series before the change, in `main`'s order, set it at 88 percent of its size, its blanks drawn whole, and with the change it's in the text's type, six of its blanks drawn shorter to end at the column's edge. With the change, that table's first blank, after "Name", is on a line of its own: in a cell whose lines are ragged, TeX breaks before a blank too long for what's left of its line, and the blank is drawn shorter on the next, where at 88 percent the two had shared a line. Offered: a no-break space before a marked blank, so that it's drawn shorter on the line of the words before it. Open, from the second review (an agent's, of this change, reading the code): `oer.blanks` draws a blank shorter only where the run of underscores itself reaches past its line's end, so where text is joined to its end, as in `(____)`, `____%`, or `____.`, a blank that ends just inside its column leaves the text running past it, by up to the text's width less what microtype hangs into the margin (in Latin Modern at 12 points about 1 point for a full stop, 3.2 for a parenthesis, and 9.3 for a percent sign), and the count, which takes such a blank as three underscores, no longer sets the table smaller for it, where `main`'s whole measure did; testing the run's whole piece, to the next glue, against the line's end would close it (offered). And a kern between two underscores would end a blank's count, which no face the review checked has (Latin Modern, New Computer Modern Sans, IBM Plex Serif, Noto Sans, and DejaVu Sans), and would take the blank whole, as `main` does. Open also: the widths `pdf-target.lua` gives a table's columns count a blank whole, as a word TeX can't break (`widen_to_words`), so a long blank's column can take the line from the next column's longest word: the review's table of two columns given half the line each, a word of 26 letters in one and a blank of 60 underscores in the other, is set at 92 percent (69 with c8fb928's measure); and, as the mailbox's notes have them, a table whose columns don't wrap has no lines in a cell to draw a blank shorter on, and is fitted for it at full length; microtype lets a blank drawn shorter overhang its column by about 1.75 points at each end in Latin Modern (0.7 in IBM Plex Serif); and the counts (`steps_widths`) still give a blank its whole length, as LaTeX's first run lays out the steps and a TeX without Lua does, which draws no blank shorter. Each done since (items 60 and 61), as Rob asked: the whole piece tested, the margin's kerns set to nothing, the counts taking a blank as three underscores in a word, and a table whose columns had no widths laid out by LaTeX's measure; and the blank kept on its label's line another way than the offer's.

58. **A worked example's steps have no rules above and below them.** Rob, of item 52's offer (2026-10-23): "Yes, remove the rules for the tables of steps, assuming that's done in the CNX unpacker." Only the unpacker writes a table of steps, class `formula-steps`, and the targets draw that class without them: `pdf-target.lua` sets booktabs' `\toprule` and `\bottomrule` to nothing in the table's group (`STEPS_OPEN`), the `\noalign{}` after each left alone (steps have no head, so no `\midrule`); and `page.css` takes the rules off a table of steps' body, which Pandoc's stylesheets, a page's (`styles.html`) and an EPUB's (`epub.css`), draw above and below every table's body (`tbody`, a pixel wide), so the pages and the EPUB had them too. A Word file had none: Pandoc's reference document rules only under a table's first row, and only where it's a head. OpenStax sets the steps as a formula, with no rules. Checked in the PDF suite's fit book (no artifact rule within 20 points of the steps' two lines, where a table of data has two by its row), failing with either rule's redefinition taken out, and in the convert suite in Chromium (the steps' body's top and bottom borders 0 pixels wide, a table of data's on the same page 1), failing without `page.css`'s rule.

59. **A formula's empty row in the `markdown` and `asciidoc` targets.** Rob, of item 55's offer: "Go ahead with that fix, assuming it doesn't result in empty tags in the PDF target." `markdown-source.lua`'s `Math` writes texmath's empty line before a matrix's end as `{}`, the row, as `pdf-target.lua` does for LaTeX (item 55), and drops any other empty line in a formula. The Markdown reader ended a paragraph at the empty line, so a `markdown` target's page read back with an evaluation bar as the text `$|_{`, raw TeX, and `}$`; and the `asciidoc` target, which writes a formula in text on one line, wrote `\\ \end{matrix}`, a row texmath reads but LaTeX doesn't, and kept the empty line in a display. Checked in the convert suite with an evaluation bar in text and one in a display, both read back with their empty rows, each of its three checks failing with `main`'s `markdown-source.lua`. It doesn't touch the PDF target. Empty tags there: since item 55 the PDF target writes the row as `{}` too, and LaTeX's tagging makes the row's cell an `mtd` structure element with nothing in it, as the source's MathML has it (`<mtd></mtd>`): built as a PDF, the convert suite's page of two evaluation bars has six `mtd` elements, two of them empty, one for each bar's empty row, and 16 empty `mspace` elements, the formulas' spaces, the same with `main`'s code and with the change. ISO 32000-2 lets a structure element be empty (14.8.4.2, in a note: "Structure elements can be empty"), and veraPDF's PDF/UA-2 profile passes the PDF.

60. **A fill-in blank: text joined to it, its label's line, the margin, and a table's widths.** Rob (2026-10-23): "Go ahead and fix the other problems you've identified", item 57's open ones. `oer.blanks` (item 33) draws a blank shorter where its line is too wide and its whole piece, to the next glue outside a formula, reaches past the line's end, text joined to it included (`(____)`, `____%`, `____$x$`): in the fit book, a blank of 15 underscores joined to a number of 20 digits, in a column of half the line (Joincell), whose number ran 8.8 points past the line on `main`.
