an outer item's paragraph holding a note after its list went into the list's last item, with 3.13, where the old code had it right, the note making list paragraphs of the item's paragraphs before it, which the reader folds into the item just before, the nested one (not once a deeper item has opened now); a tight definition list after a table or a rule an item held, which the reader gives back as more blocks than the item held, the definition in the item's style, left its last block out of the item, and the list split, with 3.13 as with 3.12.1 (what's left before the list begins again goes back in the item whose list goes on); sibling items that each open with an anchor before a list, as *Introductory Business Statistics 2e*'s solution to Example 2.17 has them, fused into one list of six, the writer numbering neither item (an item that opens with anchors before a list gets a paragraph of them for its number now, with either Pandoc); a note that ends with a list left that list's level open to the reader, which took the item's next paragraph, a list paragraph keeping its indent, for the note's list's, as it did with the old code (a list paragraph has no indent now, which only Pandoc reads); and a table's caption whose words aren't the text of `w:tblCaption`, a formula or a line break in it, stayed Compact, the caption twice (every paragraph between a table's mark and the table is its caption now). And some the old code had too: a formula alone in a `Plain`, in a tight item, ended the list with either Pandoc (`_display_math_para` and `_split_display` take a `Plain` now, and a span holding only a formula, as the writer's `isDisplayMath` does); an outer item's paragraph after an inner item's held table, and held blocks at two levels, came back outside the list or in the wrong item, which the counting below mends; and a caption of two paragraphs and a figure in a div with a credit, limits the docs state now. It found the pairing and the count of figures otherwise the reader's in every case it built, `table_captions` safe where its words matched, a file the old code wrote read right by the new, and on the earlier pages the new code never worse than the old but where these were. Then 68 mutations of the code so fixed: 66 caught. Of the two that got past, one took out `table_captions`' guard for a table's mark with no table in its range, which no mark the target writes has, the writer writing the table inside its mark; a check has one now. The other put nested items' blocks back in document order rather than the deepest first and changed nothing on any of the nested pages, and finding out why found three ways a list in an item still came apart, with 3.12.1 as with 3.13: a table an outer item holds after its list, where it holds one before it too, went into the inner list's last item, put back with the outer item's blocks and then moved again by its own mark; an inner item's table after a list that item holds went into that list, the reading walking from a part of the outer list the reader had begun at the top one list too deep; and an outer item's paragraph after an inner list whose last item ends with a table and a paragraph was left out of the item, the count folding it into that item where the reader can't. The target counts what the reader gives back after something an item holds as the reader groups list items now (`flatToBullets'`, PANDOC-NOTES): a list the item holds is the parts the reader splits it into, and each of the item's paragraphs after a table a block of its own; the reading puts the marks back in document order, the outermost first, so an inner item's block is found in its outer item after the list it belongs to, and leaves one already back in its item where it is. Trying an item that opens with a table found that neither writer numbers it, so it's lost in Word, the file numbering the next item in its place; the target gives it an empty paragraph first, as the writer gives an item that opens with a list, and so one that opens with a rule or a figure of two pictures (PANDOC-NOTES). Then 78 mutations of the code as it stands, the 68 but the two these changes left with nothing to change, and twelve of the count, the order, the block left in its item, and the item's number, each run on the checks that should catch it with the Pandoc it concerns: all caught.

81. **Another user's sidecar warned of where only whose it is keeps it as it is.** Rob (2026-10-27), having pushed `pandoc-3.13.mbox` and the notes (225c807): "I do think we want to warn in case #2 where a sidecar isn't being rewritten when it otherwise would be because of file ownership." Case #2 was item 78's second judgment call: the run never rewrites another user's sidecar, even one it may write, since the new file would be the runner's. **The change** (5176302): another user's sidecar that nothing but whose it is keeps the run from rewriting, as nothing would were it the runner's, is a warning now whatever it holds, one of ASCII alone too, which had been a note, and the warning says it *isn't* given the mark, or *isn't* rewritten as UTF-8, rather than that it can't be (`sidecars._theirs`, whose reason carries a third item saying so, and `_because`). That's so whether the user may write it ("it belongs to another user, root, and though the user running this may write it, a rewrite would take it from them") or only its owner may, as the usual mode, 0644, lets its owner alone write it; and of one in a folder with the sticky bit that isn't the user's, as `/tmp` has, where only a file's owner or the folder's may replace it, which the warning says. One the run couldn't rewrite were it the user's (read-only, another name linked to it, or in a folder that can't be written) is said to be that and theirs, in a note when it holds nothing outside ASCII, as one's own is. What to do: replace it with a copy of one's own made with `cp -p`, which keeps its mode, offered only where the user can put a file in the sidecar's name's place (`_replaceable`: its folder writable, and in one with the sticky bit, the name the user's or the folder); "and make the copy writable" for a read-only one; and ask its owner, named, to save it as UTF-8 with the mark, which needs no rewrite. A link says whose file it leads to, and a copy can take a link's place where the link is the user's, whatever folder its file is in. The docs give `mv -f`, without which `mv` asks before it replaces a file the user may not write, and how `ls -ld` shows the sticky bit, a `t` or a `T`. A run as root that couldn't give a rewritten sidecar its owner, as root in a user namespace (a rootless container) can't give a file a user outside it has, had made the sidecar the runner's, saying only that the mark was added: the new file's owner is checked before it's moved into place now, and a sidecar whose owner couldn't be kept is left as it is and said to be another user's (`OwnerLost`), its owner named as the namespace sees it (`nobody`). Root there that may not write a file of a user outside the namespace, which it sees as the kernel's overflow uid, nobody, said in a note that the file couldn't be written "though it has a write bit (marked immutable, say)" and to make it writable: it's another user's now, warned of (`_overflow_uid`), found mutating the code below. Root's own file that root can't write is still said to be marked immutable, say, and so is another user's outside a namespace, as a network share mapping root to nobody would have it (not tried). Not looked for, as the code says: a file the kernel won't let be replaced for another reason, append-only or mounted on its own as a container mounts one, is taken for one that could be, so the rewrite fails and says why. **Checks** (`run-unit-tests.py`): each case with the user patched, as a run that isn't root makes it, and for real under `setpriv` where root runs the suite, as uid 65534: root's files in folders with the sticky bit and without, the user's own among them; a copy made with `cp -p` and moved with `mv -f`, as the run advises, taking root's file's place in a plain folder and in the user's own with the sticky bit, given the mark by the next run, and refused in root's; the user's own file in root's folder with the sticky bit given the mark; links both ways, and the user's own link in root's folder with the sticky bit, which the user may replace there; and, with `unshare -r`, a run as root in a user namespace leaving root's file root's, the one it may write and the one it may not. **A separate review** of the first version (0bca118) found, each fixed with a check that fails without the fix: root's 0644 file, which only root may write, got the note and "can't be", not the warning, as if something besides whose it is kept it; a link's sticky folder was judged by its file's folder, not the link's, which is the name a copy would replace; a sticky folder the user owns lets the user replace anyone's file in it, which the advice denied; the user's own file in someone else's sticky folder was refused the mark; and the namespace's lost owner, above. Three of its mutations of the code got past the checks, which catch them now. The docs said a sticky folder shows `t`, where one without execute for others shows `T`, didn't say a sticky folder that's the user's is no bar, and gave `mv` without `-f`. Found trying the namespace, not by the review: the advice named the owner as the namespace sees it, "ask nobody to save it", which reads as asking no one; it says "ask its owner, nobody, …" now. Then 25 mutations of the round's sidecar code, each run on the unit suite's sidecar checks: 24 caught, two of them by checks added for them: the user's own link in root's sticky folder, which a mutant judging by the folder's owner alone had got past, and the namespace's file root may not write, which looking at another that got past found said to be marked immutable (above); the one that isn't takes a file the lost owner's warning describes as one the user may write, which the run has just found it is before trying to rewrite it. Left for Rob: the review's other way, writing another user's writable sidecar in place, keeping its owner, as `shortdoi.py` does when asked to write one, where the run warns and leaves it; it would change a colleague's shared file with no word but a note, and was the alternative to case #2 that Rob's answer didn't choose. **Tried** as uid 999 on a one-page book whose sidecars root owns: in the book's own folder, root's `table-captions.csv` (UTF-8 without the mark) and `bare-links.csv` (ASCII alone), which anyone may write, and its `image-alt.csv` in Windows-1252 and `languages.csv` (ASCII alone), which only root may write, were each warned of, said not to be given the mark or rewritten, where `main` noted the two of ASCII alone and said the others couldn't be; in a root's folder with the sticky bit, each was said to be in a folder where only a file's owner, or the folder's owner, may replace it, the advice to ask root. The user's own sidecar was given the mark in both, and the page read each as written.

82. **A book in Spanish baked with cookbook's Spanish words.** Rob (2026-10-27): "Unless there's something else holding us up, let's port the Spanish strings (and any other language strings used in the CNX repos that we haven't ported)." **Which languages**: of the 78 books in the 46 repositories cloned here, 61 are in English, 11 in Spanish, and 6 in Polish, and the approved-books list (openstax/content-manager-approved-books at 93a3a9a) names twelve more repositories, eleven by English titles and one holding the same two Spanish statistics books; cookbook (v2.33.0, f5b488f) has words in those three languages alone, 53 locale files in English, 14 in Spanish (one its tests'), and 6 in Polish. Polish's were ported already; Spanish's weren't, so a Spanish book was baked with English words and an English footer, as item 49 wrote down. **The change** (e942d17): `COMMON_ES`, cookbook's `lib/locales/es.yml`, and `BY_STYLE_ES`, the `locales/es.yml` of each of the twelve recipes that has one (anatomy, calculus, chemistry, computer-science, finance, history, microbiology, political-science, precalculus, precalculus-coreq, statistics, u-physics), each word as cookbook has it, for the keys the port's English has, merged over the English for a book in Spanish by `for_style`, as the Polish are. "problem", the heading the statistics recipe gives an example's problem, has no Spanish in cookbook, so OpenStax's own *Introducción a la estadística* shows "Translation missing: es.problem" there (seen on openstax.org); the port writes "Problema". Glossaries and indexes are sorted by CLDR's Spanish collation, ñ a letter after n (`&N<ñ`, twitter_cldr's tailoring for `es`, which cookbook sorts with), a decomposed ñ as the composed one (`OWN_LETTERS` in `directions.py`, which holds the Polish letters too); an index lists a term that opens with ñ under N, as cookbook does. And the footer `conversion.yaml` gives an OpenStax book is in the book's language, cookbook's `folio.access_for_free` ("Acceso gratis en openstax.org", "Podręcznik dostępny za darmo na openstax.org"), with the period the license page's English has: a Polish book's had been English. **Measured**: each of the eleven Spanish books bakes with no word a recipe looks up left in English, by a script that compares each word a book looks up with the English for its key (`r24/es/inventory.py`), and the same holds for the six Polish books; each Spanish book unpacks with its footer in Spanish. Compared with cookbook's files, the port's Spanish and Polish are the same word for word, but Problema; cookbook's words for what the port doesn't do (link descriptions, the table of contents' title, an iframe's link text, `folio.preface`) aren't ported, in any language. Left in English, each TextbookImprover's own or Pandoc's, which `cnx-input.md` lists: the heading over a page's notes ("Notes", `notes.title`, which a book's `conversion.yaml` can give in its language), a note's link back as a screen reader names it ("Back to reference 1"), a PDF's blank as a screen reader reads it ("blank"), the description `project.yaml` gives an OpenStax book, which an EPUB's metadata carries ("Licensed Creative Commons Attribution License 4.0", the license's name as the collection gives it), the menu and pager `menu: on` gives a page ("Contents", "Previous: ", "Next: "), and Pandoc's names for the title page among an EPUB's landmarks ("Title Page") and, with a cover image, for the cover ("Cover", PANDOC-NOTES); the last two found by the review (item 84). **Checks** (`run-cnx-tests.py`): a book in Spanish baked as statistics (its captions and the links to them, an example's Problema and Solución, its notes, the end-of-chapter pages' titles and names, the glossary's order, the footer); the port's Spanish and Polish words against cookbook's with `CNX_COOKBOOK` set, none differing, Problema aside, and each of cookbook's that the port looks up ported, a key the English has or one of a Polish recipe's own, and, without it, each Spanish word for a key the English has; the collation's Spanish order, a decomposed ñ, and the root order beside it; and the Polish book's footer. Each fails on `main` but the index's group, which `main` has right already, and ten mutants of the words (a word dropped or changed, a recipe dropped, a key added, in each language) are each caught.

83. **Words beside a long fill-in blank keep their line where they fit it.** Rob (2026-10-27): "Also go ahead and fix the issue with counting widths for the fill-in blanks." The open line from item 57 in What's left was stale in part: items 60 and 61 had a table's counted widths take a blank as three underscores in its longest word, and a table without widths laid out by LaTeX's measure, the blank drawn shorter in its column. What was left, found before fixing: a table without widths fits its line by each column's widest paragraph set as one line, and the measure counted a blank whole in that, so a long blank took the line from the words beside it, which wrapped where they had room, though in LuaLaTeX the blank is drawn as long as its column leaves room for. **The change** (9497295): where the cells fit the line with each blank at its least, each column is as wide as it needs to be so and the columns of blanks share the rest by how much longer their blanks are whole (`steps_next` in `latexbuild.FIT_MACROS`, a worked example's steps' reasons too, which the same measure lays out). A blank's least is three underscores, or, where words share its line, before it or joined to it as far as a space TeX may break at, a no-break space not one, two thirds of the line: the blank macros draw a long blank at two thirds of its line while TeX breaks a paragraph and ask TeX to end the line after it, so a column less than three times as wide as the blank's label puts the blank on the line after the label. The first version, which took a blank's least as three underscores whatever its label, did that: "Signature:" alone on its line beside words on one, the blank below it. A paragraph's measure is a function of its own now, `oerfit.paragraph`, which the PDF suite runs by `texlua`. Where the words don't fit the line beside the blanks at their least, the line is shared as before, the blank counted whole, and so it is by the widths `pdf-target.lua` counts for a table's first run, which a table with a cell across columns keeps, LaTeX measuring none, and a TeX without Lua sets, where a blank is drawn whole (item 84). **Measured** on the 182 tables without widths that hold a blank in the 14 CNX books with any, their tables in a book of their own (`r24/bc`), built with `main`'s code and with this, and with the first version, which laid every one of them out as this does: 2 are laid out differently, each with its words on fewer lines and its blanks shorter, on the lines they were on (*Algebra 1*'s 5.6.2, "Volume of Coral (cubic centimeters)" on one line, not two; *College Algebra with Corequisite Support 2e*'s 7.7, two steps' words each on one line, not two); the rest as they were, but for where one breaks across pages. **Checks** (the PDF suite's fit book): a table of seven words beside "Signature:" and a blank of 66 underscores, its words on one line where they had been on two, and one of a name and a role beside a blank of 58, each on one where each had been on two, each blank drawn shorter to end at its column's edge; a third whose words fit beside "Labelsig:" and a blank of three underscores but not beside one of two thirds of a column three times the label's width, the blank on the label's line and the words wrapping; and `oerfit.paragraph`'s least of eight paragraphs, on nodes a character wide: a label's blank, text and a formula joined to one, a no-break space after one, several blanks, and none. The first two fail on `main`, the third where a blank's least is three underscores whatever its label, and each of eight mutants of the measure fails the fourth; of four mutants run on the fit book's PDF, the one that takes a blank's least as more than its line whole got past it, which the fourth check catches. Left: where the words don't fit beside the blanks at their least, a column of blanks still takes its share by its blanks' whole length, so the words may wrap onto more lines than a blank at its least would need (a longer line of words beside "Signature:", tried, three lines where two would do with the blank at two thirds of its column); a column of blanks could be held at its least, as a column at its longest word is, which would shorten the blank too.

84. **What a review of the Spanish words and the blanks found.** A separate agent read both commits as they then were, ran their code on cases of its own (`pandoc` with the filter, the measure's Lua on mock nodes, cookbook's files compared by a script), and built no PDF. It found no defect that blocks either; fixed, each with a check where one could fail: **the counts**: the first version also counted a blank at its least in `pdf-target.lua`'s widths, which a TeX without Lua sets, where a blank is drawn whole, so a long blank ran further past a column now half as wide (the suite's Threecol: 28 percent of the line, where it had 56), and the count disagreed with the measure on a formula joined to a blank, and had code for a cell across columns that no check reached and that gave such a cell less than it needs; the counts are `main`'s again, LaTeX's measure alone taking a blank at its least, and a table with a cell across columns, which LaTeX doesn't measure, is laid out by the counts as before. **A no-break space**: the measure ended a blank's words at any space outside a formula, so "Label: ____~cm" left the blank too little room, though TeX can't break there and the blank macros don't ask it to; a space after a penalty of 10000 joins now (the PDF suite's check of `oerfit.paragraph`, a case no CNX module has). **The docs**: the steps' section of `formats.md` didn't say the rule reaches a worked example's reasons, which the same measure lays out; and `cnx-input.md`'s list of English words left in a Spanish book missed the menu and pager `menu: on` gives a page ("Contents", "Previous: ", "Next: ") and an EPUB's "Cover" landmark with a cover image. **The Spanish commit's message** said each of its checks fails on `main`, where the index's group, ñ under N, is right on `main` already. Checked and found right: every Spanish and Polish word against cookbook v2.33.0's, Problema aside; the keys the five Spanish styles look up (`preface`, `section`, and `lo_label_text` stay English, and none of those recipes looks them up); the collation against CLDR's Spanish; the footers' words; the Lua in `FIT_MACROS` against what TeX can read; a table without blanks unchanged by both the count and the measure; several blanks in a cell; and the 40 underscores in Prealgebra's tables written as math, which neither takes for a blank. Not reached by any of the 17 Spanish and Polish books, so left: the "Glossary" heading of a glossary outside a chapter, the text for an exercise the bank lacks, and an interactive's link text, each English.

85. **A book's PDF is set in IBM Plex, and in Latin Modern, with a warning, where TeX lacks it.** Rob (2026-10-27), of the faces suggested for the default page (`typefaces-research.md`, section 8, in the project): "Let's go with your suggestion of Plex with Plex Math as the new default. Have it fall back to Latin Modern with a warning, if it's not available. We don't need to reproduce all the research in the actual repo, but make sure that the default of Plex, including the reasoning for choosing it, and the fallback behavior are documented into the appropriate place in the docs." **The change** (`build-pdf.py`'s `FAMILIES` and `default_faces`): each family a book's metadata file doesn't name is set in IBM Plex, the text in Plex Serif and its headings in its bold, sans-serif and monospaced type in Plex Sans and Plex Mono, and formulas in IBM Plex Math, whose letters are Plex Serif's, where the file names neither a face for the text nor one for formulas (one that names a face for the text alone gets Latin Modern Math, unicode-math's own, as before); each named by its files, as the unpacker names an OpenStax design's (item 47), and each of the 13 files looked for first, with fontspec's `\IfFontExistsTF`. A field left empty (`mainfont:`) names nothing, as the template reads it. Where TeX lacks any of the files, the families it would have set are set in Latin Modern, as before, with the fallback fonts, and LaTeX writes a line for each file it lacks (`TextbookImprover face: no …`), which the run gives as one warning (`face_warning`), whether LaTeX finished or not: what's set in Latin Modern, the book, or else the text, sans-serif type, monospaced type, or formulas the file doesn't name; the first file lacking and how many more; TeX Live's `plex`, by `tlmgr install plex` for TinyTeX or TeX Live from tug.org and `texlive-fonts-extra` for Debian's or Ubuntu's texlive packages, the split `failure_advice` makes; and that naming Latin Modern's faces in the file keeps them without the warning. The LaTeX a `latex` target writes carries the same test, with LaTeX's own warning for one who builds it. Plex Sans and Plex Mono with Plex Serif, where the suggestion named Serif and Math: the family is one design, and `plex` has all four. Code is in Plex Mono at 0.858 of the text's size (`PLEX_MONO_SCALE`), where a character is as wide as Latin Modern Mono's at the text's size, its small letters a little larger still (0.443 of the size to 0.431): nothing fits or wraps a line of code too long for its line, and at the text's size, Pandoc's template's scale, a line of 76 characters ran off the paper (item 86). Beside a face the file names for the text, Plex Mono is scaled to that face's small letters, as Pandoc's template scales every face. A face the file names for sans-serif type, code, or formulas, and none for the text, is set again after Plex Serif with a scale (`rescaled`), so that it's scaled to Plex Serif's small letters and not to Latin Modern's, which the template scales it by (PANDOC-NOTES). A face a file names stops the build where it isn't installed, as before, so a cosmos book, whose `pdf-layout.yaml` names Plex Serif by its files, stops where the default would give way; the unpacker's comment above a design's face says so. A LaTeX book's PDF from its pages is set in Plex too, on the book's own page; its PDF from its own LaTeX keeps the author's faces, and a deck keeps ltx-talk's. **Docs**: `configuration.md`'s new "The faces", the reasons and the fallback, and its WCAG 1.4.8 section, a subsection of its own now, measured again in Plex; `formats.md`, `cnx-input.md`, `installation.md`, `testing.md`, the CHANGELOG (a Breaking entry, and the numbers of the default page's and the AAA page's entries said to be Latin Modern's), and the two pages' YAML comments. **Measured** on two sections built as the samples were (*Introduction to Sociology 3e*'s 1.3 and *College Algebra 2e*'s, `r25/samples`): on the default page, Plex Serif's full lines average 80.6 characters (77 to 85, the 10th to the 90th percentile), Latin Modern's 87.6 (83 to 92), 33 pages to 31; on the AAA page, 71.5 (67 to 75), Latin Modern's 77.8 (73 to 82), 40 pages to 39; the AAA page in 12.5-point type, 77.2 (73 to 81), 36 pages; and the default page with side margins of 1.1 inches, 78.0 (73 to 82), 34 pages. *Introduction to Sociology 3e*, whole, unpacked and built in Plex Serif and Plex Math: 1,032 pages on the default page, as item 47 measured it, and 1,279 on the AAA page, 24 percent more, as Latin Modern's 1,208 were to its 975. IBM Plex Math has every math alphabet Latin Modern Math has, more script letters (41 of 52 to 18), and all but 17 of Latin Modern Math's 2,045 characters (‽, ₡, a word joiner, and arrows such as ⬅ and ⇳ among them), with 4,382 of its own; Plex Serif lacks the circled digits and letters, which the fallback fonts draw, as they did for Latin Modern, and Plex Mono has no Greek but π. **Checks** (the PDF suite): the default book in Plex Serif, Plex Mono, and Plex Math, with the fallback fonts, the run saying nothing of the faces, its code's characters as wide as Latin Modern Mono's at 12 points and its formulas at the text's size; a copy of it built with Plex Math's file named as no TeX names one, in Latin Modern with the fallback fonts, the warning word for word; the files LaTeX looks for, given the families a file names, an empty field naming none; the warning's words for each; the scale a face set again is given; and the preamble book, which names Plex Math, and Latin Modern Mono with fallbacks of its own, and no face for the text: its formulas at the text's size, and its code a fifth larger, as Plex Serif's small letters are to Latin Modern's. The suite's books whose checks were measured in Latin Modern's widths name its faces (`LATIN_MODERN`: the book, the notes book tagged and untagged, the untagged book, the fit book, and the two-column book): set in Plex, 36 of their checks failed, each on a measure Plex's wider letters change, a blank no longer running past its line, a table "a little too wide" more than a little, and the notes book's untagged copy's on pypdf's text, which runs an untagged PDF's words together in Plex (4.0.2, 5.9.0, and 6.21.2 alike), where pdftotext's and MuPDF's don't; such a PDF draws no spaces, and pypdf finds them by the gaps. With pypdf 4.0.2, Ubuntu 24.04's, three of the untagged book's checks of its text failed so too, which the run with Ubuntu's Python 3.12 found, and that book names Latin Modern as well. The AAA book's check of its paragraphs' spacing, which in Plex took a footnote's first line, as far under the text's last as a paragraph's under the one before, for a paragraph's, measures the text's lines alone. In the default book in Plex every fill-in blank ends within the text's edge. Each of 16 mutations of the faces' code is caught by the default and preamble books' checks: no file looked for, a lacking file not marked, the formulas not in Plex Math, Plex Math beside a face a file names, Latin Modern without the fallback fonts, no warning made, the log's line not found, every family set whatever a file names, the warning's "the book" always, Plex Math's file not looked for, code at the text's size, a math font named alone not set again, an empty field taken for a face, a math font set again without a scale, a face with fallbacks of its own set again without one, and a face's own scale replaced by the template's.

86. **What a review of the faces found.** A separate agent read the change as it then was, against fontspec 2.9g's code, Pandoc 3.13's template and `PDF.hs`, and the fonts' files, and built small books of its own (`r25/rv`, `r25/rv2`). Fixed, each with a check: **code off the paper**: in Plex Mono at the text's size, 0.600 of the size to a character where Latin Modern Mono's is 0.515, 65 characters filled the text's width, where 76 had, and a line of 76 ran off the paper, where it took 88, with no warning of a highlighted line, and at 13.5 points, on the AAA page, one of 67; Plex Mono is set at 0.858 of the text's size now, each character as wide as Latin Modern Mono's (item 85). **A face the template sets, scaled to Latin Modern's small letters**: the template sets a face the file names, with `Scale=MatchLowercase`, before the default's text face, so Plex Math named alone was set at 9.99 points beside the text's 11.96, a sixth smaller, and a sans-serif or monospaced face with fallbacks of its own wasn't set again at all; set again after Plex Serif as the template had set it, each kept the size it had, fontspec giving a face set with the same features the family it made before (PANDOC-NOTES), and each is given a scale now, the last its options give or the template's own, which makes fontspec work it out again: Plex Math at 11.96 points beside 11.96, and Latin Modern Mono at 14.31, where it was 11.96 (the preamble book). **An empty field**: `mainfont:` with nothing after it kept the default's faces out, so the text was lmodern's Latin Modern Roman without the fallback fonts and the formulas weren't in Plex Math, without a word; a field names a face now only where it isn't empty, as the template reads it. **The install advice** gave every reader `sudo apt install texlive-fonts-extra`, which changes nothing for TinyTeX or TeX Live from tug.org, and those on Debian's packages a `tlmgr` that installs nothing there; it's split as `failure_advice` splits its own. **The warning** was given only where LaTeX finished, and said "the book is set in Latin Modern" where a file named Plex Math alone, whose formulas keep it; it's given either way, and says what's set in Latin Modern. **The docs**: the WCAG 1.4.8 section's numbers, `cnx-input.md`'s and `formats.md`'s "the default's face", and the CHANGELOG's other entries' numbers, all Latin Modern's; Latin Modern's Greek, which `formats.md` and a comment said it had none of, where it has the capitals and none of the small letters; and the default page's comment, which didn't say that naming `mainfont` sets formulas in Latin Modern Math. **The suite's check of the warning** wanted `tlmgr` or `sudo tlmgr`, where the advice names `tlmgr` by its path when none is on the path. Checked and found right: `\IfFontExistsTF`'s group and lookup (a name ending `.otf` looked up as the file, as `\setmainfont` and `\setmathfont` look it up, and near misses reported missing); the faces' order and sizes in the default book; babel's `\babelfont`, written only for a named `mainfont`, and Plex Serif kept through a change of language; the warning's way from LaTeX's log, and the LaTeX target's master; and the reasons' numbers: x-heights of 0.516 and 0.431 of the size, the thinnest strokes 2.45 times as thick, contrast of 1.90 to 3.80, Plex Serif's Greek and Cyrillic, Plex Math's Latin letters Plex Serif's, 17 of Latin Modern Math's characters lacking in it, the 13 files in TeX Live 2026, and 5.85 percent more pages. Not checked by it: the nine serif faces, the 16 symbols, the page counts, and UKAAF's 2 mm, which come from the survey (`typefaces-research.md`). Left (Open questions): Latin Modern's measures in the PDF filter's counts and the fit's macros, which the suite's fit and two-column books, pinned to Latin Modern, don't take to Plex; and the fallback built whole only where nothing is named, the other cases checked as their LaTeX and their warnings' words.

87. **Every PDF draws LaTeX's picture mode with pict2e, and `picture_paths: off` keeps LaTeX's own.** Rob (2026-10-27), shown a figure drawn both ways (Round 25's `r25/pict`), asked whether a circle meant to be 40 points would come out at 80; told the other way round, that LaTeX draws a circle asked for at 80 points at 40, its font's largest, and pict2e draws it at 80, as written, so the risk is a picture laid out around what LaTeX drew: "OK, then using pict2e by default sounds like the best choice. If we can add an option to turn that behavior off, that would be good insurance for the failure mode we're worried about." **The change**: `latexbuild.PICTURE_PATHS`, item 48's `CANCEL_STROKES` renamed, loads pict2e for every PDF, a book's, a deck's, and a LaTeX book's tagged copy, where it had loaded it for cancel alone, at `\begin{document}` and with its own drawing commands set aside and given back as before, `\buttcap` among them, which pict2e's `\circle`, `\dot`, and `\oval` call, and which are given pict2e's own in the group each draws in: taken away, as it was while pict2e drew only cancel's lines, it stopped LaTeX at the first circle ("Undefined control sequence"), as the LaTeX suite's new checks found, and kept for them, a document's own was replaced (item 92); `\maxovalrad`, which `\oval` reads, is set aside too, given back where the document had one, and kept where it hadn't; and the trig package's names, which pict2e loads and uses, `\UseSin` and the rest, stay trig's, a document's own replaced, as where cancel loaded pict2e before. `picture_paths` (`on`, the default, or `off`; a render setting, so a target's intermediates are shared) writes `\OERPictureFonts` before it where it's off (`PICTURE_FONTS`), and pict2e is then loaded only where cancel is, for the struck-out term's strokes, as before, and draws any picture in the document too: off is LaTeX's own picture mode only in a document that loads no cancel, which Pandoc's template loads for a formula that strikes a term out, and a LaTeX book may itself (the setting's description had said only the first). `texremediate.tag` adds `PICTURE_PATHS` to a LaTeX book's tagged copy with the setting on, and with it off only where the book loads cancel, after `PICTURE_FONTS`; the run's line for the copy says "its drawings in picture mode drawn with paths by pict2e, not a font's glyphs (picture_paths)" where it has a `picture` environment. A TeX without pict2e now stops every PDF, not only one that strikes a term out, the run's advice naming pict2e and cancel together (`failure_advice`); pict2e is in TeX Live's `collection-pictures`, and TinyTeX installs it with `tlmgr install pict2e`, which the installation page's first `tlmgr` line has. A fallback to LaTeX's own picture mode with a warning, as the faces fall back to Latin Modern (item 85), would keep a book with no picture building without it; not built. **Checks**: the LaTeX suite's struck-out case: a document with no cancel loads pict2e, its circle asked for at 80 points a path, no glyph of `lcircle10`; with `PICTURE_FONTS` it loads none, LaTeX drawing the circle with `lcircle10`'s glyphs; with `PICTURE_FONTS` and cancel, pict2e is loaded for the struck-out term and draws the picture too, one diagonal stroke and no `lcircle10`; a document's own `\buttcap` and `\maxovalrad` kept, its circles, oval, and dot drawn by pict2e all the same, four curves each; and what a tagged copy gets, the macros and the count, with the setting on, off, and off with cancel, and a book's copies as the run makes them with a target's setting each way, the run saying so of the first. The decks suite: a deck's circle, its target's `picture_paths` off, in `LCIRCLE10`'s glyphs. The PDF suite: the untagged book, its target's `picture_paths` off, draws its page's 80-point circle with `LCIRCLE10`, and the preamble book, in the default, with neither `LINE10` nor `LCIRCLE10`, and its circle four curves, where the check had looked only for the fonts' absence, which a picture dropped would pass (item 92). Docs: `formats.md`'s PDF section, `latex.md`, `installation.md`, `testing.md`, the setting's description (`conversion-settings.md`), and the CHANGELOG's Breaking entry. CNX-NOTES had called this Rob's call (Open questions); it's decided.

88. **A table OpenStax's math books rule row by row is drawn so, in every format but Word.** Rob (2026-10-27), of Round 19's open question, a light rule under each of the dev-math books' steps: "yes, add the rules where OpenStax also has them"; asked how far that goes, the dev-math books' steps alone or every table OpenStax rules so: "Everywhere OpenStax rules". **Where OpenStax rules** (ce-styles): the corn design, the books whose style is `additive-manufacturing`, `algebra-1`, `calculus`, `contemporary-math`, `dev-math`, `precalculus`, `precalculus-coreq`, or `statistics` (`cnx.project.DESIGNS`), gives each data cell of a table classed `unstyled` a bottom border, 0.09rem of Grey3, #757474 (`design-settings/corn/parts/_table-settings.scss`, UnnumberedUnstyledTable; a header cell has none, so the head's row has no rule), and the calculus and algebra-1 styles give it Grey1, #F1F1F1, hardly seen, in Key Equations (their `book.scss`, KeyEquations, whose rule reaches only a table classed `unstyled`); a table of another class in that design (BasicTable) has every cell's four borders, a grid, as do the dev-math books' steps OpenStax gives no class; cardboard's unstyled tables are a grid too, and carnival's and cosmos's have none. **The change**: the unpacker gives the class `row-rules` (`steptables.row_rules`, from `Emitter._layout`) to a table classed `unstyled` in a corn book, and `row-rules-faint` beside it to one in Key Equations in a calculus or algebra-1 book (182 tables, the three volumes of *Calculus* and of *Cálculo*; *Algebra 1* has no Key Equations), and `row-rules` to every table of the class `steps` (item 64), all in corn books, whose rules under each row are among a grid's lines where they're of no class (in *Prealgebra 2e*, 6 of its 860 tables of steps; the rest, 854, are classed `unstyled`, and so are 9 of its other tables). `RULED_STYLES` is the corn design's styles, a set of its own, since `cnx.project` imports `emit`, which imports `steptables`; the CNX suite holds the two equal. A page and an EPUB (`page.css`): a rule under each row of the body, 1 pixel of #757474, `light-dark()`'s #9a9a9a in dark mode, which clears 3:1, a line's contrast, in both (the unit suite's check), none under the head, and none of Pandoc's stylesheet's rules above and below a body or above a header cell; a layout table's cells stacked on a narrow screen keep it under each row's; and for `row-rules-faint`, #F1F1F1, #2e2e2e in dark mode, as hardly seen there. A PDF (`pdf-target.lua`'s `ROW_RULES` and `rule_rows`, `PDF_MACROS`' `\OERRowRules`): none of the writer's `\toprule`, `\midrule`, and `\bottomrule`, each defined to nothing in the table's group (the `\noalign{}` after each left alone), and booktabs' `\specialrule` under each row, a `\midrule`'s width (`\lightrulewidth`, 0.6 points in 12-point type) and space above and below (`\aboverulesep`, `\belowrulesep`), in OpenStax's gray (`\CT@arc@`, booktabs' hook for colortbl's color). In a long table, booktabs draws such a rule as a row of its own across the table's columns, not the line, kept on the page of the row above it, and latex-lab's firstaid module marks it an artifact and counts its row off the table's; the rows' tags are the table's own (the PDF suite's rules book). `rule_rows` ends each row of a body it rules with `\OERRowRule` (not a head's or a foot's), after everything its last cell holds, a table in a cell among them, whose own rows are left alone; the rule is put after the `\noalign` longtable opens the next row with (LaTeX's `tbl/row/init` hook, `\group_insert_after:N`, PANDOC-NOTES). A row that a cell spanning rows reaches below isn't ruled, since a rule across the table would cross the cell (the browser rules the row's other cells); such a cell is in 6 of the 3,337 tables these books class `unstyled`, counted book by book, the four Spanish books' among them (2,962 distinct; `r26/scripts/unstyled_spans.py`). A Word file draws such a table as any other: Pandoc's Word writer gives every table one style, and the Word target's marks (`docxtarget.apply_markers`) could carry row borders, not built. **Measured** on *Prealgebra 2e*'s third chapter, whose 110 tables of steps are its only ruled ones (`r26/books/pa3`, `measure26.sh`): 146 pages with the rules, 464 of them on 76 pages, each an artifact, where Round 25's code builds the same pages in 141 (334 seconds and 352). Round 19's measure, a 0.6-point rule with 2 points of space above and below it, gave 144; booktabs' space above and below a `\midrule`, 0.4ex and 0.65ex, is a little more. **Checks**: the CNX suite: in the dev-math book, its steps classed `unstyled` or of no class and its tables of data classed `unstyled`, not one of no class; in the precalculus books, their tables classed `unstyled`, a byline, a worksheet, matching exercises, a list, and a Key Equations table so classed, not one of another class or of none; in the corequisite book, its worked steps, its table of data, and its Key Equations; none in Physics, of another design; a Key Equations table classed `unstyled`, in a calculus or algebra-1 book `row-rules-faint` too, and not one of another class; and `RULED_STYLES` the corn design's; and a page keeps the class. The convert suite, in a browser: steps and a table of data with a head, of the class, a rule under each row of the body, 1 pixel of #757474, none under the head, and none above or below the body or above a header cell; and one of `row-rules-faint` in #F1F1F1. The PDF suite's rules book: a layout table of steps, its last opening with "(", a table of data with a caption and a head, a table with a cell spanning rows, a table in a ruled table's cell, a table not ruled, a table of `row-rules-faint`, and a table of 205 rows: a gray rule under each row of the body and none above it or under its head, the faint table's in #F1F1F1, each an artifact, across the table's columns; the step opening with "(" kept whole, where booktabs would take a rule's following "(" for the columns to trim it to, as Pandoc's writer's own `\noalign{}` keeps its rules from doing (PANDOC-NOTES); no rule under a row a span reaches below; the inner table's rows as before; the table not ruled with the writer's black rules; 205 rules and none under the head on any of the long table's pages, across the end of longtable's first chunk; each table's rows its own; and veraPDF finds nothing. Docs: `cnx-input.md`, `html.md`, `formats.md`'s PDF section, `testing.md`, and the CHANGELOG.

89. **A formula in running text that its page sets in display style is so where `math.display_style_in_text` says, off by default.** Rob (2026-10-27), of Round 19's open question: "I think our default should be not to use display style in running text, but we could add a configuration option to honor it. I assume this is a more general question than simply for the CNX reader, at least in theory." It is: Pandoc's HTML reader drops MathML's display style, an `mstyle` with `displaystyle="true"` or a `math` element's own (PANDOC-NOTES), on any HTML page, so the setting is the run's, not the unpacker's. **The change**: `lib/mathstyle.py` holds what the unpacker's steps had (item 63's `_style_set` and `_displayed`, now `style_set` and `displayed`, which `cnx.steptables` calls), and `marked_in_text`, which `convert.py` runs on every HTML page before Pandoc reads it, after its repairs: a formula in running text or a table's cell, not `display="block"` and not marked already (a step's `displaystyle` span, or one of its own from an earlier run), that sets in display style something display style draws otherwise (a fraction, a large operator, an operator's limits under and over it) goes in a span of class `displaystyle-text`, the markup edited as text a formula at a time, each found as an HTML parser finds it (`_Formulas`), not one in a comment, a script, or an attribute's value, the rest as it was; and so does a target's variant of a page (`read_variants`). Marking at reading, whatever the setting, keeps one reading for every target, since the reading stage's environment has no target's settings; `math-repair.lua`, a filter-stage filter, then sets each marked formula in display style after its repairs, so that its TeX is the same to `math-repaired.csv` and `math-keep.csv` whatever the setting, `\displaystyle` opening it, where the target's `math.display_style_in_text` (`MATH_DISPLAY_STYLE_IN_TEXT`, a filter setting, so targets that differ get intermediates of their own) is true, as `html-source.lua` sets a formula of the class `displaystyle`, which a page's and an EPUB's MathML then carry as an `mstyle` (`target-blocks.lua`) and a Word file doesn't, and either way takes the span away where it holds nothing else. The run says how many it kept, on how many pages, counted in the pages each target that sets it reads, its variants among them, and by which targets where not every one. OpenStax's CNXML sets 6,768 formulas in running text and 473 in a table's cell in display style where it changes them, 6,066 of the first in the calculus books, in English and in Spanish (`r26/scripts/display_census.py`, by `mathstyle.in_display`); Round 19's census (`r18/scripts/inlinedisp.py`), which counted a formula in an equation as in running text, had found 4,723 with that round's code, as the open question below has it; counted its way with today's, it's 9,207 (`display_census2.py`). **Measured** on *Calculus Volume 2*'s third chapter (`r26/books/cv3`, `measure26.sh`), a target with the setting beside one without: 551 formulas kept in display style, on 10 of its 12 pages, as the run says, and its PDF 132 pages, where it's 121 without (the two built in 902 seconds, the pages read once). **Checks**: the convert suite's display-style page, with a paragraph holding a formula so set: kept in display style, its TeX opening with `\displaystyle`, by a target with the setting, and in text style, no span left, by one without; and the unit suite's (`check_display_style_in_text`): a fraction, a sum's limits, and a formula whose `math` element says so marked; a letter, an accent, a fraction in a script, one display style is turned off for again inside, a display formula, and one marked already not; and the rest of the page as it was. Docs: `html.md`, `cnx-input.md`, the setting's description, and the CHANGELOG.

90. **A step's display style is the whole formula's, written down.** Rob (2026-10-27), of Round 19's open question, display style kept to the part of a step's formula OpenStax set so: "Let's just document this and leave it alone." `cnx-input.md` says so where it describes a step's display style: once Pandoc has read a formula, the part can't be told from the rest, so the whole formula is in display style, an integrand beside an integral sign OpenStax set so alone a little larger than OpenStax sets it, and keeping display style to the part would take the unpacker writing the formula's TeX itself, which it doesn't. Nothing else changed.

91. **Code is set at the text's size, and a block of code too wide for its line is fitted to it.** Rob (2026-10-27), of the open question item 85 left, code in Plex Mono at 0.858 of the text's size or at the text's with a line of code fitted to its line, having pushed `plex-default.mbox`: "I think it's worth also setting the code at the text's size and fitting lines the way tables are." **The change**: `build-pdf.py`'s `PLEX_MONO_SCALE` is gone, and Plex Mono is scaled as Pandoc's template scales every face but the text's, to the text face's small letters (`Scale=MatchLowercase`), which beside Plex Serif, whose small letters are as tall (516 units of 1,000 in each), is the text's size: 65 characters fill the default page's line at 12 points. `pdf-target.lua` numbers each block of code not in a table and sets it between `\OERFitCode` and `\OERFitCodeEnd`, a group, a footnote's `\OERCodeFile` (`footnote-code.lua`) too; `FIT_MACROS` measures each of its lines as LaTeX sets it, fancyvrb's (highlighted code, and code in a footnote) by the box its characters are set in, through `\FancyVerbFormatLine`, which the group wraps and unpacks as it was, and LaTeX's verbatim's (code in no language), a paragraph a line, by its line less the glue at its edges (`oer.fit.cells`), not what the output routine sets while the group is open across a page's break; and it fits the block as it fits a formula in text (kind `c`): its type set smaller, as much as its longest line takes, to three quarters of its size at least, not centered, so that a line still too long runs on into the margin on its right, and smaller still, to half at least, where it would come within a quarter inch of the paper's edge there, the right margin's width whatever the left's, fitted again on the second run where it's still too wide (Latin Modern Mono's 8-point design, used from 8.5 points down, is 3 percent wider for its size than its 12-point one), and nothing narrowed, since its spaces are characters. A footnote's code is fitted to the footnote's line, a footnote's in a table's cell too, and a quotation's to the quotation's; code in a table's cell isn't fitted itself, its table is, a line of code measured as a piece TeX can't break. LaTeX's verbatim no longer breaks a line after a hyphen, as ragged right let it. The build's fit report counts blocks of code with the tables and formulas, names a block still wider than the text, by how far it runs past the text's edge, running into the margin (`fit_report`), and warns of one too wide for the paper; `overfull_lines` leaves a block's lines to it, LaTeX reporting a line of its verbatim as a line of text too wide and fancyvrb's not at all, and knows them by the lines each block prints in LaTeX's output as it begins and ends (`CODE_MARKS`), since LaTeX names a footnote's line of code by the line of the file it's read from. The latex target's LaTeX keeps the marks, and `latexsource.fit_marks` takes them out when it's read back, a line at a time, since LaTeX's verbatim holds braces that don't pair. A deck's PDF fits a slide's code the same way. **Measured**: OpenStax's books have 667 blocks of code as the unpacker takes them (`r26/scripts/code_census2.py`, its newline elements line breaks and the indentation its lines share taken off, as the review counted them from the unpacked books; the first census had counted lines a newline element joined as one, item 92), 119 with a line longer than 65 characters (74 in *Principles of Data Science*, 41 in *Introduction to Python Programming*), 46 longer than 86, which run into the margin at three quarters of their size, 32 longer than 97, set smaller still, and 7 longer than 145, too long for the paper even at half, 5 of them in *Principles of Data Science* (up to 749 characters, a paragraph written as a string) and 2 in *Introduction to Python Programming*: wrapping a line would keep those on the paper, which nothing does. **Checks**: the PDF suite's code book, in Plex and in Latin Modern: a block whose lines fit at the text's size; a highlighted line too long and one in no language set smaller to fit, the block's other lines with them; a line in a list's item that fits the text's width fitted to the item's line; a line in no language too long even at three quarters set at three quarters, running into the margin, named as code and not as a line of text too wide; one that would come within a quarter inch of the paper's edge set smaller to end that far inside it, in Latin Modern Mono too, whose smallest design is wider for its size; one too long for the paper even at half set at half and warned of; a footnote's fitted to the footnote's line; each line's text as written; and veraPDF finding nothing; and the cases of item 92: a line with hyphens fitted whole, a quotation's line, a definition that opens with code, code in a table's cell, in no language in one table and highlighted in another, a footnote's in one, a footnote's line still too wide and a footnote's words too wide, a one-sided page with a wider right margin, a numbered display on it too, a list's line in an untagged PDF too, where LaTeX's own verbatim indents it by its left skip, and a list's code under a footer across pages. And the two-column book's line of code too wide for its column even at three quarters of its size, in Latin Modern Mono, fitted again on the second run; the default book's code at the text's size, the unit suite's reports (`check_code_fit_report`), the decks suite's slide of code too wide, fitted, smaller than a slide's code that fits, and a section's footnote's code at its size, and the LaTeX suite's latex target marking each block, a footnote's too, and reading back to the same pages. Docs: `formats.md`'s PDF section and its latex target's, `configuration.md`'s "The faces", `slides.md`, `latex.md`, `testing.md`, and the CHANGELOG, the Plex entry's sentence on code's size changed.

92. **What the reviews of Round 26 found.** Two separate agents read the change: the first the pict2e, rules, and display-style commits, against Pandoc 3.13's writer, longtable 4.27, booktabs, latex-lab's firstaid and block modules, pict2e and trig, and ce-styles' corn design, building small books with each edge case of the row rules it could think of (a table of the class `float`, an empty last cell, a cell across columns, a foot, a caption, a table in a cell, tables of exactly 200 rows with and without a head, page breaks), each ruled right, tagged and untagged, and veraPDF finding nothing; the second the code commit. **Fixed**, each with a check: `marked_in_text` found a formula by a pattern that didn't know a comment, a script, or an attribute's value, so the words `<math>` in a comment or an `alt` ran its span from inside them to the next formula's end, taking a French span's last words out of it, and losing an image's alt text and a formula, on any page, the setting on or off, since the marking runs on every page: each formula is found as an HTML parser finds it now (`_Formulas`); a formula in display style that math repair changed got `\displaystyle` before it was repaired, so a `math-keep.csv` row copied from a target without the setting kept nothing in one with it: the setting is applied after the repairs; a target's variant of an HTML page wasn't marked: it is, and the run counts what each target reads; pict2e for every PDF replaced a document's own `\buttcap`, which the first version kept from the names set aside since pict2e's `\circle` and `\oval` call it, and a document's own `\maxovalrad` stopped pict2e loading: `\buttcap` is set aside with the rest, pict2e's three commands given its own, and `\maxovalrad` set aside and given back; the preamble book's check of its circle looked only for the fonts' absence, which a picture dropped passes: it counts the circle's four curves (`pdftext.curves`); OpenStax rules a calculus book's Key Equations in Grey1, #F1F1F1, and only a table classed `unstyled`, not in Grey3 whatever its class (`row-rules-faint`); it draws no rule under an unstyled table's head, which the first version drew in every format; the count of tables with a cell spanning rows had left out the design's four Spanish books (6 of 3,337, not of 3,133); and `picture_paths`' description said a struck-out term where for a LaTeX book it's loading cancel. Found while fixing those: a ruled table in the latex target's LaTeX, read back as a source, gained an empty paragraph, the reader expanding `\OERRowRules` as the preamble defines it; `latexsource.repair_text` drops both commands now. **Left**: a `\maxovalrad` made in a document's text, and a command of one of the trig package's names, which pict2e loads, stop LaTeX or are replaced by trig's, as where cancel loaded pict2e before (documented). And outside the change: `lib/mathjax.py` takes a MathJax rendering followed by a `<script type="math/mml">` for one whose TeX follows it and drops the rendering, but Pandoc reads only `math/tex` scripts, so the formula is lost (`mathjax.rebuilt` on such a page keeps the script alone, which `html-raw.lua` drops); not fixed. The second agent, of the code commit, built a small book or deck for each case it thought of, a page's footer, a deck's section, a definition, a quotation, a table's cell, and found thirteen things. **Fixed**, each with a check: a definition that opens with code lost its term's line, the writer's `~` before such a block (`\item[term] ~`, its #4662) gone with the mark first, and LaTeX set the term in the code's first line, or the next list's: the definition opens with a no-break space again, which reads back as the writer's own does; reading the `latex` target back stopped on a footnote that opens with code ("unexpected ()"), whose mark the writer sets after `\footnote{`, where the pattern took only a mark on a line of its own (`latexsource.FIT_CODE_MARK`); a page's footer, which the output routine sets while a block's group is open across a page's break, was measured as the block's lines, and a list's code set at 90 percent under a long footer: nothing the output routine sets is measured (`status.output_active`), a wrapping table's cells across a page's break having had the same flaw; a deck's section's footnotes, which deck-pdf.lua runs pdf-target.lua on apart from the deck, numbered their fits from 1, as the deck does, so a section's code took the deck's first fit and was set at 69 percent though it fit: each section's are numbered from a number of their own (`OER_FIT_FIRST`); latex-lab's verbatim, whose right skip is the paragraph's, which ragged right stretches, broke a line of code too long after a hyphen, and the measure took each piece for a line: verbatim doesn't break after one now (`\exhyphenpenalty`), as LaTeX's own, whose right skip doesn't stretch, never did; a footnote's line of code in no language still too wide was named twice, as code and as a line of text, LaTeX naming it by the line of the file it's read from, and a footnote's own words too wide, whose last block is code, not at all, LaTeX naming them by the line the footnote's text ends on, in the block's lines: the build knows code's lines by what each block prints in LaTeX's output as it begins and ends (`CODE_MARKS`); a footnote's code in a table's cell, which the writer sets after the table, wasn't fitted, and is; code in a table's cell, whose table the docs said was fitted for it, wasn't, the cell measured by the pieces between its spaces, and a line of highlighted code not at all: a line of code is a piece TeX can't break, as words a no-break space joins now are, and a highlighted line is measured through `\FancyVerbFormatLine` for the innermost fit; how far code, a formula in text, or a numbered display may run into the margin was set by the narrower of the page's margins, a left one narrower than the right among them: it's the right one's (`\__oer_fit_right_margin:`), the narrower of two on a two-sided page; a quotation's code, fitted to the quotation's line and inside the text's, was said to run into the margin: a block is named by how far it runs past the text's edge; the decks suite's code passed fitted or not, its line fitting the slide at the text's size: it's longer, and checked against a slide's code that fits; the census of OpenStax's code had counted lines a newline element joined as one and kept the indentation the unpacker takes off: 667 blocks, 119 with a line longer than 65 characters, 46 than 86, and 7 than 145, where it said 670, 153, 74, and 16, which the docs, the CHANGELOG, and item 91 had (`code_census2.py`, the review's count from the unpacked books); and the PDF suite's line in a list, said to fit the text's width, didn't. Found by the full run of every suite on the change: the PDF suite's checks of the fit report's two warnings had their old wording, and its two runs of FIT_MACROS' Lua under `texlua` lacked `node.subtypes`, which the code's measure reads. **Mutations**: 70 of the change (`r26/scripts/mutate26.py`), each made in a copy of the tree, the checks that should catch it run there, and each caught; five weren't at first, each caught once a check was added: counting the glue at a line of verbatim's edges, which latex-lab's verbatim, indenting a list's line with a paragraph shape, leaves empty, once the PDF suite's margins book was built untagged too, where LaTeX's own verbatim indents the line with its left skip; a line of code in a table's cell measured as one piece, a highlighted line measured at all, and its width given to its table, once the code book's two kinds of code had a table each, either kind having fitted both; and a block of code still too wide fitted again on the second run, once the two-column book had a line of code, which Latin Modern Mono's 8-point design leaves a little too wide for its column, within 4 percent of it, where nothing else fits it again.

## Pandoc facts

This file's Pandoc facts are all in PANDOC-NOTES now (a8acfe7, 2026-10-14), each measured again there with 3.12.1 and saying which parts were, so read them there. Three of mine didn't hold, and measured again here with 3.12.1 (2026-10-14), they don't:

- **A prefixed `<m:math>` is read as MathML.** I had it read as no formula at all. The reader drops every tag's prefix first (`stripPrefixes`), and `<m:math>` holding `<m:mi>` reads as a formula, inline and display, its namespace declared on it, on `<html>`, or nowhere. What I measured before isn't known. The unpacker writes MathML unprefixed all the same.
- **lxml.html reads a self-closing `<mspace/>` as closed.** I had it nesting the rest of the paragraph inside. With lxml 5.2.1 (libxml2 2.12.6) and 6.1.3 (2.14.6), `lxml.html.fromstring`, `document_fromstring`, and `lxml.etree.HTML` close it, and `none`, `mprescripts`, `malignmark`, and the other elements tried. What I saw before isn't known either. `util/cnx-compare-word.py` still reads a page as XML, since that's how it finds a formula, by its namespace; its comment no longer gives the wrong reason.
- **A `<pre>`'s and its `<code>`'s classes aren't merged**: `pre`'s win where both have one, so `<pre class="x"><code class="language-python">` is a code block of class `x` alone. OpenStax names a program's language as a class of its code element (`python`, `r`), which the unpacker puts on the `<pre>` too, so no block of the books loses it.

One more, for PANDOC-NOTES, whose entry on `<pre>` says 3.9 is said to read one without a `<code>` as a `CodeBlock`, not measured. Measured with 3.9, TI's minimum (its release binary, 2026-10-14): a `<pre>` without a `<code>` is a `CodeBlock` (`plain` and an indented `two` as one block of two lines, where 3.12.1 makes a paragraph of no-break spaces and a `LineBreak`), and a `<pre>` with any attribute of its own takes none of its `<code>`'s: `<pre id="p2"><code class="python">` is a code block with the id and no class, so no language, where 3.12.1 gives it both, and `<pre class="x" id="p1"><code class="y" data-n="3">` loses `data-n` too. Read so in 3.9's `pCodeBlock` (the code's attributes are read only when the `pre` has none). The unpacker's blocks keep their language either way, their class being on the `<pre>`.

And one more, for PANDOC-NOTES: **the HTML writer writes a table's `summary` as `data-summary`.** `toAttrs` (`Writers/HTML.hs`, 3.12.1) prefixes `data-` to an attribute HTML5 doesn't have, and `summary` is in `html4Attributes` alone (`XML.hs`); it looks for a repeat by the name it read, so a table with both `summary` and `data-summary` gets `data-summary` twice, which isn't valid HTML. Read and measured, 2026-10-15. So an HTML source's summary reaches the page where a screen reader doesn't read it, as enki writes OpenStax's on purpose (open questions, the step tables).

From converting the CNX books with `main`'s `convert.py`, validators on. PROJECT-NOTES has them in brief since 2026-10-14 (OpenStax's books from their source, through the checks); the detail is here.

- **`[html-raw] … iframe x1`** is the closing `</iframe>` fragment being counted, the frame itself made an embed. Tested as such (`"iframe x1" in marked.stderr`); misleading to read, but deliberate.

Nutrition: four vnu errors, and four epubcheck errors in its EPUB, each a link from an address the source writes as text with a typo (`https://doi.org:10.1016/…`, a colon for a slash, read as a port), linked as cookbook's `post_bake` links it. - **A table's id a heading took first was renumbered**, until 77e8c20 (Fixes for `main`). `make_ids_unique` in `figures-and-tables.lua` kept an anchor's id over a heading's generated one, since it walks inlines first, but between two blocks the first kept it: an HTML page whose `<h1>Money</h1>` came before `<table id="money">` gave the heading the generated id `money` and the table `money-1`, so a link to the table landed on the heading. Found by a test's page, not a book. Pandoc's HTML and Markdown readers choose a heading's id looking only at earlier headings' (PANDOC-NOTES). - **`notes.*` on a book of a file per page: the HTML and the EPUB disagreed** (`main` at a8acfe7, 2026-10-14), until 1192f59 (Fixes for `main`). The HTML took a page's group to be what the split made of its source (`page_group` in `convert.py`, as `notes.py` says), so a page that is a source of its own, as every unpacked page is, was a group of one; the EPUB took it to be the top-level entry of the book's contents holding the page (`bookassembly.py`'s `groups`). So for a book whose contents group its pages, `numbering: group` and `placement: group` changed the EPUB alone: *Entrepreneurship*'s chapter 1 was numbered 1 to 24 across its pages and gathered on its last, Suggested Resources, in the EPUB, and each page restarted at 1 and kept its own in the HTML. `placement: book`'s Notes page, in the HTML, put each page with notes under its own title, in the order of the files' names (1.1, 1.3, then chapter 1's Introduction, then 10.1), where the EPUB's Notes chapter put each chapter's under the chapter's title, in order. Three Markdown pages show it (zebra and mango in Chapter 1, apple in Chapter 2): the HTML's Notes page listed Apple, Mango, and Zebra, a note 1 each; the EPUB's Chapter 1, notes 1 and 2, then Chapter 2. And the EPUB's rule took a part as the group: with the two chapters in a part, apple's note was 3, so by that rule an OpenStax book in units (*Principles of Marketing*) would be numbered across each unit's chapters (not run), where OpenStax numbers per chapter. `main` took one rule for both in 1192f59, not the one first proposed here (Fixes for `main`), and the unpacker now writes `notes.numbering: group` and a unit's `type: part` (Design decisions).
- **`alttext.py`'s rules on OpenStax's alt text** (4271da7, which judges a book's images by a deck's rules). Every `<media alt>` in the 45 repositories here, 42,286, put through `alttext.problem` (2026-10-14): 44 named, and the rest not. Rightly, 19 "placeholder" in *Introduction to Behavioral Neuroscience*, OpenStax's own, and 11 of punctuation alone (*Physics*' "..." 7 times, *Psychologia*'s ".", "-", and "–"). The file-name rule names 2, both in *Workplace Software and Skills*, screenshots described with the file types they show ("JPEG image (.jpg, current slide)", "A .pdf file titled customers1.mdb"), as PowerPoint's rule has it. The page-title rule names 12, none a page's title: the pipe 5, an augmented matrix's bar (`[1 1 3 | 0]`, *College Algebra 2e* 3 and *Prealgebra 2e* 1) and *Calculus*' "the _ and | parts"; the ellipsis 2, a sequence that goes on ("multiples of 4: 4, 8, 12, 16, 20, ..."); the dash 1, a quotation's attribution ("…THE FIRE OF GENIUS - LINCOLN", *Introduction to Intellectual Property*); and the stock words 4, Word's Stock Images button in two of *Workplace Software and Skills*' screenshots and "isolated on a white background" in two of *Prealgebra 2e*'s. The code's comment has OpenStax's alt text in eight repositories matching none; these are in others. Each is a row for a person to look at, not a change to the book, so it costs a look; the pipe rule could want no digit or bracket after the pipe, and the ellipsis rule no comma or digit before it, which leaves 6 of the 12 (measured on the same alt texts). The slides session took both suggestions further (cc0530b, 2026-10-15): put through `main`'s rules at da4f1f0, the same 42,286 alt texts give the page-title rule none, as that session found in its copy of the books, and the 30 placeholders and 2 file names as before. Its two new rules (8712a58): alt text that is only a credit, a warning, names none of them; and one alt text on different pictures, a note, names 1,742 pictures in 48 books by a count from the sources, each book's pictures told apart by their bytes, most in the math books (*Precalculus 2e* 271, *Algebra and Trigonometry 2e* 223, *College Algebra 2e* and the corequisite book 173 each, *Intermediate Algebra 2e* 149).
