Five books converted with both, the code before (`main` at 3dfae32 with `main-fixes-6.mbox` and the branch as it was) and now (*Introduction to Sociology 3e*, *Anatomy and Physiology 2e*, *Introduction to Business 2e*, *Introduction to Python Programming*, and *Mikroekonomia – Podstawy*), give the same body on every page and in every EPUB chapter file but Sociology's chapter 4 references (above), and only the stylesheets differ, with the highlighting's colors (154afe7).

- **A table marked as layout, from any HTML source** (`main` at a494d8b, 2026-10-15). A page of four two-column step tables, one `role="presentation"`, one `role="none"` (ARIA's other name for it), one with an `aria-label`, and one unmarked, converted to HTML: the census skips the first two and has one row for the other two, which are alike. The output check skips the first alone, so the `role="none"` table is a `table-without-headers-or-caption`, as are the other two, an `aria-label` not counting as a name. `figures-and-tables.lua` treats all four as data tables: each is in `table-captions-missing.csv`, warned of as having no header row and no declaration, and wrapped in a focusable scroll region; so would be the derivations DCIC's site profile marks layout (not run here, DCIC not being here). The filter counts a layout table in its page's position keys (here `steps#table-1`), so a change that stopped counting it would move the key of every table after it on the page, and the sidecar rows keyed on them. And no sidecar can say a table is layout: `table-headers.csv` takes `first-row`, `first-column`, `both`, and `none` (and keeps `manual` and `list`), so a Word source's layout table, of which Word records nothing (troubleshooting), can't be one. Item 20 under Fixes for `main` settles each of these.
- **A table with no column widths runs off a PDF's page** (`main` at 7a4e90e, 2026-10-16). Pandoc writes a table whose columns have no widths, and each of whose cells is one paragraph with no line break or is empty, as a longtable of `l`, `c`, or `r` columns (`isSimpleTable` and `colDescriptors`, Writers/LaTeX/Table.hs, 3.12), which don't wrap; any other table without widths gets `p` columns of equal width, which do. *Prealgebra 2e*'s Table 3.2, a worked example's steps, prints "The temperature warmed up 12 degrees from 7 degrees below zero." past the right edge of the page, before the layout-table change and after it. So would any such table with a long cell, from a source that gives no widths. Fixed for `main`, item 24.
- **A PDF numbers a data table that has an id and no caption** (`main` at d21f490, 2026-10-15). Pandoc's LaTeX writer holds such a table's id in an empty caption, `\caption{}\label{…}` (Writers/LaTeX/Table.hs, 3.12), which LaTeX numbers: a page of two data tables, the first with an id and no caption and the second with the caption "Costs by year", gives "Table 1" alone above the first and "Table 2: Costs by year" above the second. A table classed `unnumbered` gets no empty caption, and no `\label`, so its id goes nowhere in the PDF. In the dev-math books, one data table each in *Prealgebra 2e* and *Elementary Algebra 2e* has an id, no caption, and no class `unnumbered`, each in an answer on the answer key's page for a chapter; none of their tables classed `unnumbered` is a link's target (the 109 links to a table in the three books go to captioned data tables). Item 20 makes a layout table's id an anchor before it, and item 23 a data table's without a caption, which LaTeX numbers no longer.
- **A row one cell across a table with a header column loses its span** (`main` at 7a4e90e with `layout-tables.mbox`, 2026-10-16). *Prealgebra 2e*'s Table 3.1, guessed `first-column` and split at its bands, ends each part with a note one cell across it ("When the signs are different, some counters would make neutral pairs; subtract to see how many are left."), which the page writes as a `<th scope="row">` beside an empty `<td>`: Pandoc's table model lays a body row's header cells within its body's row-head columns, and every writer cuts a cell that runs past them, the cells after it moving left, as a table of two rows shows in HTML, LaTeX, Word, and Markdown alike (PANDOC-NOTES, the HTML writer); the same row in a body of its own with no row heads keeps its span in each. Not fixed: where such a row goes, a body of its own or the table's foot, is the roadmap's open question for a trailing summary row's empty header cell, which it says to settle once for both (PROJECT-NOTES). Since item 51, the guess gives a table with such notes before its last row no header column, so Table 3.1's notes keep their span.
- **A fill-in blank of underscores at a line's end runs into a PDF's margin** (*Introduction to Sociology 3e*, whole, with `cnx-open.mbox`, 2026-10-19). Two questions of its chapter 19 section quiz end in a blank written as a run of underscores ("is an example of ________________") whose line runs past the text's right edge, by 24.3 and 5.7 points, inside the page: TeX can't break the run, and the `\emergencystretch` of 3em Pandoc's template sets can't make room for it on a line of few spaces. The other chapters' quizzes have the same blanks and fit. The build's warning said 20 lines on the quiz, and I passed that on as the quiz's; the other 18 are the contents' page numbers (next), which a run of LaTeX on the quiz alone, and LaTeX's own output for the book, showed. Fixed for `main`, item 33, as Rob chose. The rest of the run: the 243 pages and the EPUB with the validators in 226 s, the output check, epubcheck, and the Nu checker finding nothing; the PDF, 953 pages and 51 MB, in 1,359 s, veraPDF finding nothing; 87 of OpenStax's alt texts over 120 characters, 776 bare links, and Table 11.1, two columns of ten rows, a data table with neither a header nor a declaration, its guess `unknown`.
- **The contents' wide page numbers run into the margin** (*Introduction to Sociology 3e*, whole, 2026-10-19). The book class leaves a page number in the contents 1.55em (`\@pnumwidth`, 15.5 points), and in Latin Modern a roman number such as xviii or xxviii, or any of four figures, is wider. The preface's sections, after the contents' own pages, are on pages xxvii to xxxiii, 15.8 to 24.2 points wide, so 18 of the contents' lines stand 3.1 to 8.7 points past the text's right edge, and four, on page xxx, 0.3. The build counted them among its lines of text, on no page, so its warning named 20 lines on the quiz's page (fixed, item 34). Widening the contents' column for page numbers, and the margin entries keep from it, would settle it, for every book; not done (Open questions). Most of it was the contents' depth: they went a level deeper than `pdf.toc_depth` says (item 35), and with that fixed, Sociology's preface begins on page xi and no line of its contents is too wide.
- **OpenStax's page against the PDF target's** (2026-10-19; Rob: "how much do our versions of the PDFs differ from the PDFs being produced by OpenStax in terms of width, height, etc?"). OpenStax's PDF is Prince's: enki's PDF step (`dockerfiles/steps/step-pdf.bash`, at 85b78c2) has MathJax draw each formula as SVG, then runs Prince 16.2 with `--tagged-pdf --pdf-profile PDF/UA-1` over the baked book and its style's stylesheet from ce-styles (v2.22.2, a592354, the revision enki's last update of it names). Each of the 55 stylesheets that has rules (`styles/output/*-pdf.css`; AP History's is empty) sets margins of 1in left and right and 0.8in top and bottom, the running heads and folios in them, and "Access for free at openstax.org" at the foot of each left-hand page; only two of the Polish styles (nursing and physics) set a size, A4, so the others' is Prince's own, Letter: the one OpenStax PDF in the repositories here, a chapter of *Principles of Economics* from 2015 (`media/CONNEX150033_Ch24.pdf`, Prince 9), is 612 by 792 points, its text 468 points wide between margins of 72. So OpenStax's text is 6.5 by 9.4 inches. Its type is the root's: 12px (9 points) on lines 1.5rem apart (13.5 points) in IBM Plex Serif (12 styles: economics, sociology, psychology, the histories, philosophy, and others) or Noto Sans (16: marketing and the business books among them); 12px on 1.4rem (12.6 points) in IBM Plex Sans (17, the sciences and nursing among them); and 11px (8.25 points) on 1.4rem (11.55 points) in Noto Sans (8: the math books' styles, statistics among them, and additive manufacturing); organic chemistry's is IBM Plex Serif on 1.4rem, and a stylesheet named physics, which no book's style directory makes, Alegreya. The PDF target's page is the book class's at 10 points: Letter, Latin Modern on 12-point lines, the text 345 by 550 points (4.8 by 7.6 inches) placed for two-sided printing, 1.5 inches inside, 2.2 outside, and 1.7 at the top; OpenStax's text block holds about two-thirds more. Measured on *Introduction to Sociology 3e*, whose chapters 1 to 20 OpenStax's PDF sets on 618 pages (its contents, read through a web fetch, put chapter 1 on page 7 and chapter 21 on page 625): the target's PDF sets them on 858, 953 pages in all, 76 characters to a full line (66 as first reported, a count that left out a justified line's spaces, which the PDF holds as pieces of their own; corrected 2026-10-19, late); built in OpenStax's page with `pdf.metadata` alone (`geometry`, `linestretch: 1.125`, and IBM Plex Serif scaled to 9 points), on 634, 711 pages in all, 111 characters a line. *Introductory Business Statistics 2e*: 872 pages, 105 lines too wide, on 23 pages (85 in tables, 6 display formulas, 8 lines of text, and 6 in the contents; the widest a line of text, 123 points too wide); in OpenStax's page (Noto Sans at 8.25 points on 11.55), 597 pages, 52, on 4 pages, all in tables, the widest in the appendix's statistical tables (the line of text 123 points too wide in the target's page fits). A size between: OpenStax's margins with the book class's own 11 points (13.6-point lines), 95 characters a line (83 as first reported, the same count's), Sociology's preface and chapter 19 in 43 pages, where the target's page takes 57 and OpenStax's page, built here, 42. The contents' page numbers overran their column in OpenStax's page too (17 lines, built before item 35), since it's 1.55em whatever the page's width. And the footer: openstax.org asks of a copy of one of its books "Access for free at" and the book's address "on every physical page" in print and "on every digital page view" otherwise (each book's attribution box), which the unpacker's `footer` says it gives "every page of every target"; only the HTML pages have it, since neither the PDF nor the EPUB writes a target's footer (none in Sociology's PDF, nor in its EPUB's 297 files). Every format has it since item 36.
- **OpenStax's own PDFs of the four books** (2026-10-19, late; Rob attached *Introduction to Sociology 3e*, *Principles of Economics 3e*, *Principles of Marketing*, and *Introductory Business Statistics 2e*, made 2026-04-20 to 27). Each is Prince 16.2's, PDF 1.7, on Letter, claiming PDF/UA-1, and, measured with PyMuPDF, laid out as the stylesheets say: the text 468 points wide (6.5 inches) between margins of 72, 9-point type on 13.5-point lines (8.25 on 11.55 in *Business Statistics*), set ragged right, its paragraphs apart and not indented, the running heads and folios in 7.5-point bold sans in the top margin (IBM Plex Sans, or Noto Sans in *Marketing*, or 6.88-point Roboto Slab in *Business Statistics*), and "Access for free at openstax.org" in the same type at the foot of the left-hand pages alone: of pages 11 to 60 of each book, all 25 even pages carry it and no odd one does. A full line holds 109 characters, spaces counted (the median of the lines that reach the margin, in *Sociology*'s chapters); the PDF target's page holds 76, and the same text at 12 points in OpenStax's margins 89. The contents give each entry's page number right after it, with no leader or column. veraPDF 1.30's PDF/UA-1 profile finds none of the four compliant: 7 or 8 of its rules fail each, 41 to 60 checks, content neither tagged nor an artifact (17 to 37 checks), link annotations with no description and annotations outside the structure (7 to 10 each), structure types mapped to no standard one (2), an optional content configuration with no name (1), a row of the wrong length or a TH with no scope (1 or 2), and in *Sociology* the catalog's MarkInfo saying the file isn't tagged (`Marked` false), in *Business Statistics* a font's CIDSet. The license page, CC BY-NC-SA 4.0 in all four now, asks of a copy redistributed in a digital format that it "retain on every page the following attribution: 'Access for free at openstax.org.'", of a printed copy the same "on every physical page", and of part of the book the same "in every digital format page view (including but not limited to PDF and HTML) and on every physical printed page": the words, not a link. A book's page on openstax.org gives another form in its attribution box, "Access for free at" and the address of the book's first page, "on every physical page" and "on every digital page view", again with no link asked for, and adds that the book "may not be used in the training of large language models or otherwise be ingested into large language models or generative AI offerings without OpenStax's prior written permission", which the PDFs' license pages don't say.
- **How OpenStax styles its boxes and headings** (2026-10-20, for Rob's question: "Is there more we can do to copy the formatting of the OpenStax PDF? I assume these settings are given somewhere in their conversion chain? … boxes like "Big Picture", "Sociology in the Real World", and "Social Policy and Debate" have the generic box type set much closer to the specific box topic title. (I also wonder whether these are both headings and, if so, whether the hierarchy is correct.) Similarly, they have different colors for some headings."). OpenStax's PDFs are Prince's rendering of its ce-styles stylesheets (LGPL-2.1; v2.22.2, read, not copied), a design (*Sociology*'s is cosmos) and each book's settings (`styles/books/sociology/book.scss`), compiled to `styles/output/sociology-pdf.css`, whose root is 12px, so 1rem is 9 points. *Sociology* has four kinds of box, in two styles. Sociology in the Real World (37) and Social Policy and Debate (12): the label, the note's kind, in uppercase Archivo Black at 10.8 points beside an icon, over a rule across the box (#0074BC or #1D8565), the title under it in Archivo, and a rule closing the box. Big Picture (20) and Sociological Research (10): the label white on a band across the box (#C31427 or #38568A), right-aligned, the title and the text on a tint (#FFE5E5 or #EFF3FA). The label's baseline is 21.5 points above the title's in the first style and 27.8 in the second, 1.6 and 2.1 of their 13.5-point lines; ours are two bold headings 33 to 35 points apart, about 1.9 of our 18-point lines, with no rule, band, or tint, so nothing shows where a box ends. **Both are headings in their PDF and in ours.** In theirs the label is an H3 and the title an H4 wherever the box stands, so a box in a section headed H4 ("Criticism", "Max Weber") has its label a level above its section's heading, and one in an H5 ("Institutional Ethnography") goes H5, H3, H4; nothing in the structure holds the box, which ends where the next heading begins; and the label, uppercase by its stylesheet, has no ActualText. In ours the label is a heading a level below the section's and the title one below that (`cnx.emit`), in the pages, the EPUB, and the PDF alike, so the levels nest. **Colors**: a *Sociology* section's title and its headings down to H4 are #C31427 (6.09:1 on white, AA but not AAA's 7:1 for text that size), its "Learning Objectives" and the chapter outline's title #8B693A (5.02:1), a figure's label #8B1504 (9.52:1), and links #027EB5 (4.51:1), #026591 on the tints; each design has its own. The type is IBM Plex Serif for the text, IBM Plex Sans for boxes, captions, and tables, and Archivo for headings, all under the SIL Open Font License. And the PDF's catalog says it isn't tagged (`/Marked false`) though its metadata claims PDF/UA-1, and a figure's caption is four paragraphs, no Caption.
- **OpenStax's typefaces, measured for legibility** (2026-10-20; Rob: "I need more information to know whether copying the OpenStax typeface choices would be more or less accessible. We've already chosen accessibility of over fidelity to their design in, e.g., the font size. We should choose accessibility here, too. But maybe that means different things for different books, depending on the typeface?"). A separate agent's survey of the research, checked where it mattered, is in the project (`typefaces-research.md`): print size is the best-supported factor in legibility, measured by x-height (Legge and Bigelow 2011; UKAAF's 2024 guidance gives 2 mm for 12-point clear print); at equal x-height, typefaces differ little, serif or sans, and fonts designed for low vision or dyslexia haven't beaten good ordinary ones; ordinary weights do alike, and the one low-vision study that varied the contrast between thick strokes and thin found low contrast best; and readers differ, the font they prefer not the one they read fastest (Wallace et al. 2022). OpenStax's four designs (ce-styles' `styles/design-settings/*/_typography.scss`) set their text in IBM Plex Serif (cosmos, 12 styles, the social sciences and the humanities), IBM Plex Sans (carnival, 15, mostly the sciences and nursing, *Organic Chemistry* in Plex Serif), or Noto Sans (cardboard, 16, mostly business and computing; corn, 8, mostly math), their headings in Archivo, Mulish, Roboto Condensed, and Roboto Slab, one to each, and their formulas in STIX. Measured on the fonts' files: Latin Modern Roman's x-height is 0.431 of the type's size, 1.82 mm at 12 points, under UKAAF's 2 mm; Plex Serif's and Plex Sans's 0.516 and Noto Sans's 0.536, 2.18 and 2.27 mm, a fifth larger. Latin Modern's thick strokes are 3.8 times its thin ones, Plex Serif's about 1.9, the sans faces' 1.2, and its hairlines a third of a pixel at 12 points and 100% zoom. The three text faces tell I, l, and 1 apart, as Latin Modern does; Archivo, Mulish, and Roboto Condensed draw I and l as the same bar, and Roboto Slab doesn't. *Sociology*'s section 1.3 at 12 points on the default page: Latin Modern 12 pages and 89.0 characters to a full line, Plex Serif 13 and 81.9, Plex Sans 12 and 86.1, Noto Sans 13 and 81.9. So copying OpenStax's text faces would give larger and sturdier type than Latin Modern, whichever the design, and copying three of its four heading faces would lose I and l told apart: it's the typeface that decides, not the book. Matching math fonts exist for Plex Serif (IBM Plex Math, in TeX Live) and Noto Sans (Noto Sans Math, which TeX Live has only for pdfLaTeX, and whose OpenType version in Debian's and Ubuntu's `fonts-noto-core` has no MATH table, item 47), not for Plex Sans. A font a metadata file named got no fallback fonts (`build-pdf.py` gave them only to the families it didn't name), which copying needed first. Rob chose the text faces at 12 points (item 47).
- **The widest lines found in an OpenStax book's PDF, of text and of a table** (2026-10-21; Rob, of *College Algebra 2e*'s 28 lines in Noto Sans: "give me the worst examples of what it looks like when the lines are too wide. Give me an example with text and an example with a table."). Chosen from the books measured above, *College Algebra 2e*'s first chapter for text and *Introductory Business Statistics 2e*'s statistical tables for a table, each built with `main` at bec2e48 and its pages widened to show what runs off them. *College Algebra 2e*'s first chapter, 136 pages in Noto Sans and 128 in Latin Modern: 28 lines too wide (4 display formulas, 24 lines of text) and 14 (1 and 13), the widest by 224 and 136 points as TeX measures a line, its box against the text's width, every one a formula's, and no line of prose and no table. The "lines of text" are worked examples' steps, which OpenStax writes as one formula in a paragraph, a table of each step and its reason, the reason after a space in the formula (in the chapter's pages, 60 spaces of 4em or more, the widest five of `<mspace width="13.5em"/>`, in section 1.2's (f)), and which TeX can't break, so a reason too long for the line runs off it. The widest, section 1.2's (d), "Commutative and associative laws of multiplication", after a space of 3em, has its last letter 218 points past the text's right edge in Noto Sans and 130 in Latin Modern, past the paper's edge in both. In Noto Sans a formula is larger: Pandoc's template matches every face's small letters to the text's (PANDOC-NOTES), so beside Noto Sans, whose small letters are taller than Latin Modern's, Latin Modern Math and the em of a formula's spaces are 24% larger (in the PDF, Latin Modern Math at 14.87 points beside the text's 11.96), and of the 88 points more that (d) runs over by in Noto Sans, 65.5 are its formula's and its space's, which start its reason further right, and 22.6 its reason's, longer in Noto Sans. Tables: *Introductory Business Statistics 2e*'s appendix A, 22 pages in Noto Sans and 21 in Latin Modern, 40 and 36 lines of its tables too wide, the widest by 172 points in both, Table A5 (F critical values), thirteen columns at the same places in either face, its last four past the text's edge and the last two wholly past the paper's.

## Design decisions

- **Pages are named as OpenStax names them** (its Word files and web addresses), checked against the Word files of the corpus's six books: 1,379 of 1,386 names match, and the other 7 are the answer key pages of *Principles of Data Science*, empty here without its exercises. The rules: section `3-1-…` from its numbered title; introduction `{n}-{title}`; end-of-chapter page `{n}-{title}`; answer key page `chapter-{n}`; appendix `{letter}-{title}`; a unit's own page `{unit}-{title}`. Slugs: lowercase, apostrophes dropped, accents stripped, `®` as `r`, everything else a hyphen.
- **Notes are `div`s**, titled by heading. `<aside>` gains nothing: Pandoc reads it as a `Div` with class `aside`, and writes that back.
- **A page's title is its first `h1`**, the introduction's included (OpenStax puts it after the opening figure and outline); **a table's caption is a `<caption>`** at its top; **footnotes are Pandoc's** (`role="doc-noteref"`, a `role="doc-endnotes"` section), numbered through each chapter, as OpenStax numbers them, by `notes.numbering: group` in `conversion.yaml`, each page keeping its own at its end; a unit is `type: part` in `project.yaml`, so each of its chapters numbers on its own, and a page at the top level, a preface or an appendix, is a group of its own (`main`'s `notes.groups`, 1192f59). Measured, HTML and EPUB, on *Entrepreneurship* (its 15 chapters with notes, chapter 1's four pages with them numbered 1 to 4, 5 to 20, and 21 to 24) and *Concepts of Biology* (11 chapters with notes in six units, Chapter 7 and Chapter 8 of Unit 2 each from 1): every chapter numbered from 1 through its pages, and the EPUB's numbers the pages'.
- **The word a caption's own label opens with is the book's**, written into `conversion.yaml`'s `captions.figure_prefixes` and `captions.table_prefixes` where it isn't TextbookImprover's `Figure` or `Table`: *Principles of Management*'s and *Introduction to Business*'s "Exhibit", a Polish book's "Ilustracja" and "Tabela", from the style's strings. A PDF then sets each such caption with its own label and none of LaTeX's (item 18). Converted with and without it, *Principles of Management*'s and *Makroekonomia - Podstawy*'s pages are the same; the Polish book's 16 tables whose caption is "Tabela" and a number alone, with no summary to describe them, are reported in `table-captions-missing.csv`, as an English book's are, where they weren't before.
- **A table's summary is its drafted description** in `table-captions.csv`, marked drafted by OpenStax: under its label where the caption is the label alone, and under TI's positional key (`chapter-12#table-5`) where there's no caption at all, as for the unnumbered tables in Economics' answers. The unpacker counts a page's tables as `figures-and-tables.lua`'s `number_tables` does (document order, a table before those in its cells, a table of pictures skipped). Economics 3e: 23 such tables, and after converting their pages each description was on the table whose own summary it is. A summary that says nothing isn't drafted: OpenStax's stand-ins, "No Summary" (575 in the repositories), "Assessment data" (124, with and without its period), "Assessment information" (34), "summary" (21), and "no alt text" (13), and what alt text's checks call a placeholder, punctuation alone (671): 1,438 of the 6,214 summaries in the 41 repositories (June 2026), which is what the review counted. And 166 more, found listing the summaries by how often each is written: *Principles of Finance*'s "Some cells are left blank for visual design purposes." (75), which says why a cell is empty and not what the table holds, the chemistry books' "key equations table" (51), "Table" and the like (26, "Table...." among them), "table x.x" (5), "No-Summary" (5), and a Polish template's "The table is called...Opis tabeli" (4): 1,604 in all. Summaries that say little but something are drafted ("empty x and y table", 9 in the algebra books). *Life, Liberty, and the Pursuit of Happiness*'s 434 summaries are all such (416 "No Summary", 17 ".", one "--"), so its boxes are no longer captioned "No Summary"; the run counts them. Nor is a layout table's, which asks for none.
- **A worked example's steps are a layout table** (`role="presentation"`) in the dev-math books, *Prealgebra 2e*, *Elementary Algebra 2e*, and *Intermediate Algebra 2e*, as the review of the proposal recommended (`claude/layout-tables-review.md`, 2026-10-15): a table OpenStax classes `unstyled`, with no head and no caption but the number baking gives it, as OpenStax's own rule has it (enki's `cnxml-to-html5.xsl`, the label aside), unless TextbookImprover's header guess reads a header row in it. Of the 2,541 `unstyled` tables, 5 have a head; of the other 2,536, 10 are *Prealgebra 2e*'s numbered ones ("Table 3.2", the source's `unstyled` without `unnumbered`), steps all the same, and one has an empty `<caption/>`, which says nothing. The guess reads a header row in 7: five tables of data (*Elementary Algebra 2e*'s "Binomial Squares | Product of Conjugates" and its classification of polynomials, *Prealgebra 2e*'s two comparisons of the specific and the general in 9.7 and its pictures across the top in 11.1), which stay data tables, and two of steps it misreads (*Prealgebra 2e*'s 11.4 and *Intermediate Algebra 2e*'s 6.5, whose first row is "Step 1. Read the problem." beside an empty cell), which do too, each reported (`table-kept-as-data`) for a person to settle with a row of `table-headers.csv`. So 2,529 are layout tables (854, 840, and 835), the number the review expected by a different route: it counted the empty caption as a caption, and didn't have the second misread. The guess is the one `convert.py` makes because it's made of what Pandoc gives `convert.py`'s: `cnx.steptables` reads a cell as `tablecensus.view_from_pandoc` reads Pandoc's AST after `html-source.lua` (a short row padded, an empty column dropped, a picture's text its description), and a formula as its TeX, which `cnx.mathtex` writes as texmath does, ported. A first reading that took a formula's text as it stands misjudged three tables, the fraction 21/100 reading as the number 21100 among them. Measured: every one of the 115,122 formulas in seven books (the three, *College Algebra 2e*, *Calculus Volume 1*, *University Physics Volume 2*, *Chemistry 2e*) is Pandoc 3.12.1's TeX to the character, and every one of those books' 3,674 tables gets the guess `convert.py` makes, 3,011 of them the dev-math books'. Other books' tables that only lay things out are the next item.
- **Other books' tables that only lay things out are layout tables too**, as the review judged them: a table OpenStax classes `unstyled`, with no head and no caption but its number, in *College Algebra with Corequisite Support 2e* (precalculus-coreq) and *Physics* (hs-physics), named with the dev-math style in `LAYOUT_STYLES` (`cnx.steptables`), outside a chapter's Key Equations (the next item); and a table of one cell, so classed, in any book (of any class since 2026-10-18, below); each unless the header guess reads a header row in it. Measured on every book with such tables (2026-10-15). The corequisite book has 125: 32 in Key Equations; of the other 93, 4 have a head (a table of study hours, a worksheet of statements, and two tables of values, one of them inside a table beside a blank graph), and 89 are layout tables, steps in worked examples' solutions (79), corequisite skills (5), Try Its (4), and a problem (1), none of which the guess reads a header row in. *Physics* has 137: 62 in Key Equations, and 75 grids of a section's key terms (74 classed `section-key-terms`, and 1.2's without the class). Tables of one cell: 259 bylines in *Life, Liberty, and the Pursuit of Happiness* ("Written by: Alan Taylor, University of Virginia"), the time a section takes in *College Success 2e* (61), *College Success Concise* (36), and *Preparing for College Success* (40) ("Estimated completion time: 22 minutes."), and a formula alone in the Key Equations of *Chemistry 2e* and *Chemistry: Atoms First 2e* (17 each). Of the 401 tables of one cell in the repositories' sources, 389 are classed `unstyled`; the other 12 have a caption, or no class (the *Writing Guide*'s peer-review forms among them), and were left as they were until 2026-10-18 (below). Unpacked before and after, those eight books' pages differ in the role on those 594 tables and nothing else, and `table-captions.csv` loses the descriptions drafted for them from their summaries, which say what the table holds ("Estimated completion time: 22 minutes.", "The list of Key Terms includes: atom, …", Chemistry's "key equations table") or a worked example's formulas in words. Converted whole (HTML and EPUB, validators on, 2026-10-15), each of the eight books has those layout tables (259, 61, 36, 40, 75, 89, 17, and 17) without a scroll region and every data table with one, epubcheck finds nothing, and the Nu checker only what it did (private-use characters, composing characters, texmath's `\quad`). The *College Success* books report nothing but shared alt text (16 in *College Success 2e*). What the output check reported of tables then (before the next item) was what no summary hides: *Life, Liberty*'s inquiry organizers (34 tables on 17 pages, classed `unnumbered`, not `unstyled`), the corequisite book's 52 (35 classed `os-coreq-element`, 13 of Key Equations, 4 others), the chemistry books' Key Equations of more than one cell (26 each, and *Atoms First*'s table of the signs of ∆H° and ∆S°), and one of *Physics*' Key Equations, each as a table with neither headers nor caption, on its page and in the EPUB; a summary that said nothing had been their caption ("No Summary", "summary", "key equations table"). *Physics*' 20.3 grid of three key terms, which was reported so, is a layout table now, and the corequisite book's header cells holding only a formula fall from 542 to 532. A Key Equations table stayed a data table, which the review said could go either way, until the next item.
- **A chapter's Key Equations are layout tables, in any book** (2026-10-16). Rob, of the open question: "I agree with layout for the key equations." Such a table is a formula's name beside the formula, which OpenStax's own HTML marks a layout table; as a data table, each wanted a header column declared and a caption, and a summary that said nothing had been its caption ("key equations table"). `cnx.steptables` takes a table OpenStax classes `unstyled`, with no head and no caption but its number, in a chapter's Key Equations (OpenStax's section `key-equations`, which baking moves to the chapter's end; *Fizyka*'s "Najważniejsze wzory"), unless the header guess reads a header row in it. Unpacked (2026-10-16), the 16 books with Key Equations, the three volumes of *Fizyka* among them, have 480 tables there, 478 of them classed `unstyled`, the question's count, which named 13 books where there are 16; 475 are layout tables now, 441 of them newly, the chemistry books' 34 of one cell having been layout tables already. Three stay data tables, each reported (`table-kept-as-data`): the same table of the rules of exponents in *College Algebra 2e*, *Algebra and Trigonometry 2e*, and the corequisite book, whose first row is its title, which the guess reads as a header row (`both`). Two are classed `unnumbered`, not `unstyled` (*University Physics* Volume 2's chapter 13 and Volume 3's chapter 10), which the rule left data tables until it took a Key Equations table of any class (2026-10-18, below). A layout table's summary isn't drafted, since it needs no caption, so the summaries the Key Equations tables had drafted (148 that said something, *University Physics*' and *Fizyka*'s describing the table as a picture) aren't; each is still in `cnx-table-summaries.csv`, which lists every summary.
- **A table that holds only what's laid out is a layout table, in any book; so is a dev-math book's table of steps written without the class** (2026-10-18). Rob, of the examples in `unstyled-tables.html`: of a box of one cell, "a one cell table is obviously just layout"; of a form, "I would call this a layout table. If there are other tables with only underlines in the cells, I think we can always safely call that layout"; of pictures in a grid, "I think we'd have to go with layout for anything that only has pictures, unless headers are actually declared"; of a matching exercise, "layout; agree with a specific rule to match this, if there's no collateral damage", and of one the guess gives a header column, "layout"; and of a Key Equations table classed `unnumbered` and a worked example's steps with no class, "the first column is arguably headers, but layout table is not a bad guess if changing the rule to classify it would result in bad guesses elsewhere". `cnx.steptables`' `layout_reason` takes a table with no head (no `<thead>`, no `th`) for what it holds, in any book, of any class, with any caption, and whatever the header guess reads: one cell; lines to fill in and nothing else (underscores, the other cells empty); pictures, each with a label of four words at most; and two lists to match, two columns of three rows or more, each column's items opening with marks of one series (a blank to fill, numbers, capitals, small letters, or roman numerals, a lone i, v, or x read with its column), the two series different. For where it stands, the guess allowing as before: a Key Equations table of any class; and in a dev-math book, a table of another class with no label of OpenStax's, as OpenStax's own HTML has it (enki marks no table with a label a layout table). Unpacked (2026-10-18), the 67 books' 12,211 tables have 181 more layout tables, 3,745 in all, and none fewer. 110 are the dev-math books' steps and boxes of properties written without the class (*Elementary Algebra 2e* 103, *Prealgebra 2e* 6, *Intermediate Algebra 2e* 1), 93 the guess reads no header in and 17 a header column; 34 are matching exercises (*Microbiology* 13, *Principles of Financial Accounting* 10, *Principles of Managerial Accounting* 8, *Biology 2e* 2, the same one twice, and *Algebra 1* 1), each looked at and each a matching exercise; 12 are tables of one cell (the *Writing Guide*'s templates and peer-review form, 8, and 4 captioned pictures, which the filter makes figures); 12 are pictures side by side (*Algebra 1* 10, *Contemporary Mathematics* 2); 11 are the statistics labs' recording sheets, blanks in every cell (*Statistics* 10, *Introductory Statistics 2e* 1, 3 of them titled); and 2 are the Key Equations tables classed `unnumbered`. Of the 453 tables of one cell, all but 2 with a head (*Entrepreneurship*'s) are layout tables now. The dev-math rule first took a table of any class, which made 22 tables with a label layout tables too, 12 of them tables of values or of data (*Prealgebra 2e*'s Table 2.9, the multiples of 3 beside their digits' sums; *Elementary Algebra 2e*'s days of the week beside a stock's changes, twice; two classifications of polynomials; a data set; a sequence's values; a function's domain and range, twice; two classifications of complex numbers; and a comparison of two kinds of inequality), and reported 80 more as kept data, tables of values with a header row; so it takes a table with no label, and the 102 with one stay data tables, unreported. Two it takes are kept data and reported, the guess reading a header row in them: *Prealgebra 2e*'s Table 5.8, the properties of equality in a box, and *Elementary Algebra 2e*'s Table 1.30, three percents written as decimals step by step, side by side. Left as they are, by Rob's verdicts: the tables of values and the dated transactions the guess reads right; the corequisite book's tables of other classes, its tables of values and forms, which a rule for any class there would make layout tables wrongly; *Contemporary Mathematics*' grids of numerals ("layout, though data table / list is also reasonable"); and a data set ("data table or list is right here"). The rating scales are left as they are too (Open questions), and the inquiry organizers have a rule of their own (the next item).
- **An inquiry organizer's key heads its rows with its emoji, and the organizer is laid out** (2026-10-19). Rob, of the explanation that the key's first row is no header row: "Oh, so this type of table should actually have the headers only in the first column? If we are making the guess in the unpacker, then let's put in a specific rule for it. If it's in the converter, then leave it alone." *Life, Liberty, and the Pursuit of Happiness* marks each kind of page with an emoji in its title (🔎 on 175 pages, ✒️ 156, 📝 75, 📍 36, 💬 30, and 📖, 📎, and ✏️ 16 each), so the key's emoji is what each of its rows explains, and its first column the rows' headers. The header guess is the converter's (`tablecensus.py`), which stays as it was; the unpacker writes the header cells itself, as it writes a head OpenStax gives: on a page of the book's style (ap-history) titled "Inquiry Organizer", which is all that marks one, a table each of whose rows opens with a symbol alone beside words (`symbol_key`) has its first column's cells written `<th scope="row">` (`head_rows`), and the page's other table, the chapter's question, objectives, and supporting questions beside their resources, is a layout table whatever the guess reads (`layout_reason`'s "page"). Unpacked, the book has 16 keys headed and 16 organizers laid out, 275 layout tables with its 259 bylines, and no other table changes: 3,761 layout tables in the 67 books. Converted, a key is a data table with a header column, `first-column` declared in `table-headers-new.csv`, and asks a person for its description in `table-captions-missing.csv`, as any data table without a caption does: 16 alike, since each key's summary says nothing ("." or "No Summary").
- **A summary that reads its table out is drafted as any other is, and named** (2026-10-16; the rule widened 2026-10-17). Rob, of the open question: "If we can reliably identify the summaries that read a table cell by cell, then it might make sense to add them to the sidecar with their current content so it can easily be changed. I'm leery of deleting them entirely by default." Such a summary ("Two rows and five columns. The first row is labeled, “x”, and the second is labeled, “g(x)”. The values of x are 2, 4, 6, and 8…"), drafted as a caption, is read before the table it repeats. It's in `table-captions.csv` with its words as they are, marked as OpenStax's, and the report names it (`table-summary-reads-out`, its key and its first twelve words), for a person to put what the table shows in its place; `cnx-table-summaries.csv` marks it in a new column, Reads out. `reads_out` (`cnx.project`) names a summary that reads its table's rows or columns as ordered pairs, a row's or a cell's words ("Top left cell reads: Essential Knowledge", "Row 2 has “Big Bang” and 10 to the 68"), its shape or a column's name and then a run of its values (four numbers or three quoted words), a header's numbers ("are the numbers: 0.10, 0.20, and 0.30"), or a function's values ("f(1)=6, f(2)=8, f(3)=3, and f(4)=1"), in English or Polish; and one that repeats its table's own cells in words of its own ("Washington 67, J. Adams 90, Jefferson 83…", `repeats`, given each table's body cells by the emitter): the words of six cells, or five that are half the table's, from two columns or more, compared whatever their case, dashes, quotation marks, or spacing, a letter alone not counted and a number inside another not taken for a cell's. Of the 4,610 summaries that say something in 41 of OpenStax's repositories (June 2026), it names 1,941, 1,283 of them on a table with no title, whose summary is drafted: 1,609 by their words and 332 more by the cells they repeat (the CNXML's cells, measured). Read: 70 of those its first words name, and all 29 the numbered rows, the headers' numbers, and the functions' values add, and every one reads its table out; and 50 of the 332, of which 49 do, the other reading out a stem-and-leaf plot's stems. Below those thresholds it named too much: at three or four cells, 6 of the 25 it would add were descriptions, figure grids' labels among them ("A 2x2 grid displaying four graphs labeled Graph A, Graph B, Graph C, and Graph D"); at five of sixteen, one whose digits were the table's ("Values of algebraic expressions (x, x^2, x^2+5, x^2-4)…"); and from one column, a list of the rows' names. In the 16 books with Key Equations, unpacked, it names 951 of the 1,020 summaries drafted (894 by the first words: 30 of those were read, and 25 of the 57 the rest add, and every one reads its table out). Of the 69 it doesn't name, all read, 41 read their tables out in ways it doesn't know ("“Pythagorean Identities” with three cells. First: sin(theta)^2 + cos(theta)^2 = 1…", "For (x,f(x)) we have the values (2, 4) and (5, 12)", house values of "37700" where the cell has "$37,700"), and 28 say what the table is ("Chart of year vs membership fees", "empty x and y table"). It leaves out a summary that names the columns and not their cells ("This table presents the year in the first column and percent of workers paid hourly rates in the second column"), or lists one column's cells, rightly. A summary it misses is drafted as before, not named.
- **OpenStax's label for a table (`aria-label`) isn't written**, as the review recommended, and each is in the report (`table-label-left-out`, saying whether the table is a layout table). 1,685 tables have one, 1,683 of them in the dev-math books; 125 words at the median, 747 at most, each describing its table as a picture. With one, a browser ignores a layout table's role (ARIA: a global attribute keeps the role from applying), and a screen reader reads it on entering a data table, before the caption; PDF, Word, and Markdown can't carry one, so the formats would disagree. A table's description is its caption, which every format carries.
- **An address written as text is a link to itself**, as cookbook's `post_bake` makes one after every recipe (302 in Economics 3e's references; OpenStax's Word file has 303 hyperlinks there), so `convert.py` reports them as bare links as it does the Word file's. A Connexions archive book's are left as text, and so is an address a typo has made invalid (a port that isn't a number, a backslash, no host, a host ending in a number that isn't an IPv4 address, as `https://doi.10.1111/…` is, a period for `org/`), which `post_bake` links: the link goes nowhere, and an EPUB holding it fails epubcheck. What such an address holds that a link's can't is escaped in the link, the text left as written: a `%` that starts no escape (*Lifespan Development*'s `#:~:text=In%`, an address a line break cut), `{`, `}`, `|`, `^`, `` ` ``, `[`, and `]` (*Principles of Accounting*'s `#{%22issue_id%22:390208,…}`), and a `#` after the one that opens the fragment (*Principles of Marketing*'s `…year=1994##document-2239…`). And what cookbook's pattern takes in after an address that isn't part of it is text after the link: a closing quotation mark and the punctuation before it (“https://docs.google.com.”, five in *Workplace Software and Skills*, others in the *Writing Guide*, *Introduction to Intellectual Property*, and *Population Health for Nurses*), and a mark that shows nothing (a left-to-right mark after a lecture's address in the statistics bundle); and an address whose host has an empty or impossible name (“http://.”, the *Writing Guide*'s advice to leave it out) isn't linked. Of the 14,881 links the port makes of addresses written as text in the repositories here, 15 change, in 13 texts: 12 end where their address does, and 3 that went nowhere aren't made.
- **Links get the text baking gives them** ("Figure 3.2") and no `aria-label`. A link to a module of another book of the same repository goes to that book's page on openstax.org (the other book is baked to learn the page's name).
- **Inline lists are separated and lettered** as the archive showed them (CNXML's defaults: `;` between items, `.` after an enumerated mark); OpenStax's Word files run them together. **A labeled item's label is text** before the item (`____` in a statistics checklist), where OpenStax's assembly keeps it in an attribute for their stylesheet to show. **Links to learning objectives get a space after each** (*Principles of Accounting*'s "LO 8.2 LO 8.4 Visit…"), which OpenStax's markup runs together and their stylesheet spaces.
- **A missing picture's description stands in its place**, since a page naming a missing file stops `convert.py`.
- **Local frames** (Python's code runners, 81 MB, Wiley's code, "not subject to the CC BY license" per their README) become links to the page of the online book.
- **Code is a block where OpenStax's assembly makes one**: anywhere but in a paragraph or a list (nebuchadnezzar's `cnxml-to-html5.xsl`), or in a title, a caption, a term, and the like, unless it says `display="block"`; code of class `inline-code` is inline anywhere, as their stylesheet shows it (`pre.inline-code { display: inline }`, for a table's cells). A block is `<pre><code>`, losing the blank line it opens with, the indentation it closes with, and the indentation all its lines share (the markup's); a newline element followed by a line break is one line break (Python writes both for a program's output, and in a `<pre>` a browser shows two). `line-numbering` adds Pandoc's `numberLines`, since the text cites line numbers. A block holding bold or italics (Python's example input, 14 of them; Finance's `=SLOPE(`*y*`-data range, …)`) is a `<p>` of inline code with `<br>`s, its indentation no-break spaces, since a code block would drop the bold. A colored span in code (Data Science's `green-code`, Finance's `red-code`) is left to be dropped: color alone.
- **The glossary and index sort** is CLDR's root collation as twitter_cldr (cookbook's collator) gives it: checked against twitter_cldr itself on all 12,934 terms of the eight repositories here, identical order. Text is sorted as it stands, spaces included, so a term written with a leading line break sorts first, as OpenStax's does. Index entries merge across capitals exactly as cookbook's `item_for` does (Ruby's `capitalize` lowercases the rest, so "Gross domestic product (GDP)" met first doesn't merge with "gross domestic product (GDP)"); the one change is that whitespace is evened out before matching.
- **Section titles are `h3` (depth 1) and `h4`, other titles `div[data-type=title]`**, as OpenStax's assembly writes them, so the recipes' selectors find what theirs do; `emit.py` sets the levels pages are written with.
- **A list whose items are lettered ⓐ, ⓑ (class `circled`) is bulleted**, the letters kept: OpenStax's stylesheet hides its numbers, and numbered it would read "1. ⓐ". A labeled-item list of the same items is bulleted already. Bullets were the choice that keeps a list a list for a screen reader; Pandoc can't write a list without its markers.
- **What cookbook writes for screen readers alone isn't written** (BakeScreenreaderSpans: "underline" and "end underline" around each underlined letter of *SohCahToa*), since a page without OpenStax's stylesheet would show it; the underline itself is kept. **Folios aren't written** either (BakeFolio's running heads for print, "1.13.4 • Creating a Table from an Equation" on each page, which OpenStax's stylesheet hides on the web).
- **A term a chapter's Key Terms has twice is one term with both meanings**, a dt and two dds, the second term's ids kept at the head of its meaning. Cookbook lists both definitions, each its own dl (two sections define "pancreas" in *Concepts of Biology*'s chapter 16); the Key Terms are one dl here, which shouldn't name a term twice.
- **An index's groups are cookbook's**: a term is listed under its first letter as Ruby's `I18n.transliterate` spells it in ASCII, its accent off, capitalized as Ruby capitalizes (Æ under "Ae"), and a character with no such spelling, a digit, a sign, or a letter of another script, under Symbols: *Biology 2e*'s "α-helix", which an accent-stripping port had put under a group of its own, "Α".
- **An unnumbered table keeps its title and caption** in *Astronomy 2e* and *College Physics*, whose recipes number every table (BakeNumberedTable.v1), counting the unnumbered ones as OpenStax's books do, and take an unnumbered one's caption away, putting nothing back (*Astronomy 2e*'s "Table A").
- **The *Writing Guide*'s retitled pages and sections keep their headings as their sections have them.** Cookbook's BakeCustomSections titles a Trailblazer page by its kind ("Trailblazer"), its own title an h3 under it, and puts an h3 naming a section's kind ("Living by Their Own Words") at the head of the section, its own title made an h4. Here both are paragraphs, the kind's just before its section, whose title stays its heading: as headings, one put a level between a page's title and its sections, the other a section's title under a heading that isn't its section's. A retitled page is named by its own title (`1-2-social-media-trailblazer-selena-gomez`), as OpenStax's contents, made before the retitling, name it. The icons OpenStax's stylesheet draws beside the handbook's annotations, with their names for screen readers alone, aren't written.
- **A Polish book's links name their targets in the case the sentence wants.** The source marks a link's case (`cmlnle:case="genitive"`, which the unpacker keeps as `data-case`), and the Polish recipes label what a link can name in all seven cases ("Ilustracji 1.2", "na Ilustracji 1.2"); a link that names none shows the nominative, as a label in the text does. The strings are cookbook's `pl.yml`, the English ones where a Polish one is missing (cookbook writes "translation missing").
- **A Polish book's terms are in three indexes**, of subjects, of names (`cxlxt:index="name"`), and of words in other languages (`foreign`, each with its language), listed by the form the source gives for the index (`cmlnle:reference`, "wrażenia zmysłowe" for "wrażeń zmysłowych"; a name's `cxlxt:name`, "Darwin, Karol"); grouped by that form's first character as it stands, Ł a group of its own; and sorted, with the glossaries, by CLDR's Polish collation (ą after a, ł after l, ź then ż after z), which is twitter_cldr's, cookbook's collator, for Polish.
- **A Polish meaning that is one paragraph keeps its markup.** Cookbook's `has_para` keeps the first paragraph's text alone, its italics and formulas lost, any later paragraph too; here the paragraph's content is the meaning's, and its id is kept. A list item that is one paragraph (BakeListsWithPara) is its content as cookbook makes it, the paragraph's id kept as well.
- ***Marketing - Podstawy*'s references are footnotes of class reference**, numbered through each chapter, each a superscript number linking to its entry and the entry a paragraph opening with the number, linking back ("1."), on the chapter's references page (BakeFootnotes.v1 with its selector and BakeChapterReferences.v4); its other footnotes are Pandoc's notes, numbered as the notes settings say, where cookbook numbers them in Roman. A chapter with no references gets no references page; cookbook makes it empty.
- **A note whose kind the locale doesn't name has no label**, where cookbook writes "unknown title for note with classes [...]": the Polish nursing sample's special considerations, safety alerts, and trending today, which its strings lack.
- **A reaction's mechanism is captioned as any figure is** (*Organic Chemistry*'s class `mechanism-figure`): cookbook sets its label, number, and title above the mechanism and its caption beside it, for print; here it's the figure's caption, after the figure as every one is. And **Chemistry Matters' page closes up its dash** ("Chemistry Matters—Organic Foods: Risk versus Benefit", UseSectionTitle), as the book's text writes its dashes; OpenStax's page shows a space either side, the line breaks of cookbook's templates.
- **Organic Chemistry's problems open "Problem 1-1"** (`problem_with_prefix`), the words and number one link to the solution, run into the problem's first paragraph as any number is.
- ***Algebra 1*'s units are numbered as its table of contents numbers them** ("Unit 1 Linear Equations"), not as BakeUnitTitle numbers their headings, counting the three unnumbered units too ("Unit 3"). OpenStax's reader shows the contents' numbers, never the headings. Its lessons are its chapters ("Lesson 1.2"), each page numbered from its overview (1.2.0), as the recipe numbers them; the units and lessons that open, support, and close the book, and each unit's overview and projects, go unnumbered, and the lessons after them are numbered as if they weren't there (Kitchen counts an element among those its search found). A unit's own page is named by the unit's number (`1-unit-1-wrap-up`), where it was its place.
- **Learning objectives numbered by their place are a numbered list** (2026-10-17), after Rob's run of *Principles of Marketing*, whose "Learning Outcomes" showed a bullet and a number for each: BakeLearningObjectives.v2 writes each objective's number as text (`span.os-abstract-token`) in a `ul.os-abstract`, whose bullets OpenStax's stylesheet hides. Where the numbers count the objectives, 1, 2, 3 (`count_only_li`: marketing, pl-marketing, and marketing's appendix), the list is an `<ol>`, the numbers its own and the token gone; where they're the section's (`in_chapter`, "1.1.1": calculus, algebra-1, data-science, neuroscience, nursing-external, pl-nursing), it stays bulleted with each number as text, which an `<ol>` can't show. Rob, of the open question (2026-10-18): "Yes, I think we need to leave the learning objectives alone when numbered by section, at least for now." `util/cnx-compare-word.py` reads an `<ol>`'s numbers as words, as the Word files have them.
- **A formula's forced line breaks make its lines the rows of a table of one column** (2026-10-17), after the same run, where *Marketing*'s "Value = Benefits/Price" and "(V = B/P)" stood side by side: the source breaks the line with `<mspace linebreak="newline"/>`, which texmath reads as a space (PANDOC-NOTES) and MathML Core doesn't break at. A row of a formula holding such breaks becomes an `<mtable>` of one column, a line to a row (`mathml.lines_as_rows`), centered in an equation or a formula displayed as a block and at the left elsewhere, which texmath reads as an array and every target sets as lines; a break at the end makes no row.
- **A table baking leaves outside its container is decided as one in it is** (2026-10-19). Rob, of the open question: "Is that still open? If so, let's fix it." The emitter decided layout, and listed a table for the sidecars it drafts, only for a table in OpenStax's container (`os-table`); one outside it took the generic path, never decided, its summary never drafted. `Emitter.table_alone` takes such a table as `div_os_table` takes a contained one. Measured on the 67 books (2026-10-19): 10 of the 12,221 tables they hold stood outside a container (the survey of 2026-10-18 counted the 12,211 the unpacker decided, those in one). Eight have no class, each in an exercise's solution in the answer key, which no recipe numbers or wraps: the same table in three of the economics books (Jonathon's choices of work under a benefit, chapter 15 of *Principles of Economics 3e* and *Microeconomics 3e*, chapter 14 of *Microeconomics for AP Courses 2e*), three more in the AP editions, and one each in *Prealgebra 2e*'s chapter 6 and *Elementary Algebra 2e*'s chapter 4. Two are *College Physics for AP Courses 2e*'s unnumbered tables in its test prep, a style (ap-physics) whose recipe doesn't bake an unnumbered table, the only such tables in the 16 styles' books: the humanities books, whose repositories weren't here and were cloned sparsely (their CNXML alone, 47 MB for the eight), have every table in a container (*U.S. History* 7, *American Government 4e* 13, *Psychology 2e* 38, *Introduction to Anthropology* 3, *Introduction to Political Science* 16, *Introduction to Philosophy 2e* 15, and of other styles, *Lifespan Development* 59 and *World History* 4). All ten are data tables; the five with a summary have it drafted as a description, under their place on their page (`chapter-15#table-2`), and each of the five reads its table out, so is reported (`table-summary-reads-out`). No other decision changes. The CNX suite's book in U.S. History's style has a byline of one cell, laid out, and a table with a summary and a label, drafted and reported; both checks fail without the change.
- **A formula's script of the wrong number of parts is a base and its script** (2026-10-19), found checking item 27's formulas (open questions, 2026-10-18). texmath reads an `msup` or `msub` of two children only (`checkArgs2`), so Pandoc kept a formula holding one of any other number as its text, in a span of class `math`, and nothing said so. *Fizyka dla szkół wyższych* has 20 such superscripts, 10 in each of Volumes 1 and 2, the only scripts of the wrong number in OpenStax's 49 public repositories: 12 of three parts (the degree sign, the C, and the power of °C⁻¹, 10 of them; "2,00", t, and 2 for 2,00t²; 10, 7, and a space after them) and 8 of one (N, P, Q, G, r, a prime, s, "1 m/s"). A script sits on the symbol just before it, so `mathml.repair_scripts` takes the last part for the script and the one before it for the base, sets what's before those before them and a space at the end after them, in an `mrow` where the script stood for an argument (a fraction's numerator), and makes one of one part that part alone; each is reported (`script-repaired`), since the source may have lost a script: *Fizyka*'s unit of angular momentum reads kg⋅m/s, its `msup` holding the s alone, where the English edition has kg·m²/s. The base taken as the part before the script, not all that's before it, gives 2,00t² rather than (2,00t)², and °C⁻¹ as the page shows it. With Pandoc 3.12.1, all 20 formulas read as formulas. The CNX suite's economics book has a page of four such scripts, one in a fraction and one an `msub`; seven mutations, each caught by its two checks.

## Provenance and licenses

- **Ported**: openstax/cookbook (MIT), at f5b488f0ad2d48bb1b49b990dd249e9869d0b256, its recipes and directions and its English and Polish locale strings; the license is in `lib/cnx/LICENSE-cookbook.txt` and each ported file says so.
- **Ported**: texmath (GPL-2.0-or-later, which TextbookImprover's GPL-3.0-or-later can take), 0.13.3.1, its MathML reader and TeX writer and their tables of characters and operators, for the characters and operators OpenStax's books use, in `lib/cnx/mathtex.py`, which says so. Only a formula's TeX is made, to read a table's cells as Pandoc gives them to the header guess.
- **Not used, outputs only**: nebuchadnezzar (`neb`, AGPL) assembled the economics bundle once, for comparing; its stylesheet and code were read to learn the shapes its output has (which cookbook's selectors depend on), not copied. bakery and the cnxml repository (AGPL): not used. enki's `cnxml-to-html5.xsl` (AGPL) was read for its rule for a layout table, which `cnx.steptables` describes; nothing of it is copied.
- **twitter_cldr** (Apache 2.0), cloned from GitHub, run only to check the collation; nothing of it is in the branch. The punctuation order in `collation_key` is CLDR data read off its output. The Polish order is CLDR's Polish tailoring (`&A<ą`, …, `&Z<ź<ż`), checked on the samples' glossaries and indexes, which match.
- **Exercise bank**: the API's shape was read from nebuchadnezzar's tests and confirmed against the live API through a web fetch; this container can't reach `exercises.openstax.org` from the shell.
- **OpenStax's statement on language models**, on a book's page at openstax.org and not in the PDFs' license pages (TextbookImprover observations, OpenStax's own PDFs): Rob, 2026-10-20: "Our use doesn't violate OpenStax's AI clause. We're not training a model."
- **Read, not copied**: openstax/ce-styles (LGPL-2.1), v2.22.2, OpenStax's stylesheets for its PDFs, read for the values its boxes and headings are set with (TextbookImprover observations), and for each design's text face and the styles that use it, which `cnx.project` lists (item 47).
- **The Symbol font's characters** (item 43): the glyph name the font's encoding gives each code, as pdf.js lists it (`SymbolSetEncoding` in `encodings.js`, Apache 2.0, read, not copied), and the character Adobe's mapping of the font gives each (unicode.org's `VENDORS/ADOBE/symbol.txt`); `cnx.emit`'s table writes the characters out, facts of the mapping.

## The exercise bank

- A link `#exercise/NAME` is fetched as `?q=nickname:NAME`, `#ost/api/ex/TAG` as `?q=tag:TAG`, from `https://exercises.openstax.org/api/exercises`, cached raw under `~/.cache/textbookimprover/cnx-exercises/{public|with-token}/{kind}/{code}.json`, eight at a time. `--offline` reads the cache only; `OPENSTAX_EXERCISES_TOKEN` is sent as a bearer token.
- **Formulas in exercises are TeX** (`<span data-math="…"></span>`, Data Science's solutions among them), made MathML with `pandoc -f latex --mathml` at unpack time, all in one call and one by one where that call's count doesn't match; one that fails stays TeX and is reported.
- **Solutions are public for some books and not others**, per item (`solutions_are_public`): Marketing's are (correctness, feedback, a detailed solution); Information Systems' aren't (answers with no correctness, no solutions). OpenStax's Word files have answers for both, so they build with a token.
- How many books depend on it: Information Systems 410 exercises, Python 613, nursing-external 1,156, Data Science 136, Marketing 662, *Principles of Finance 2e* 580, *Business Ethics* 196, *Introduction to Intellectual Property* 185, *Biology for AP® Courses* 2,361, *Organic Chemistry* 1,961, *Life, Liberty, and the Pursuit of Happiness* 1,896, *Physics* 848, *Introduction to Behavioral Neuroscience* 568, *Contemporary Mathematics* 3,073 (nearly all its questions), *Algebra 1* 932 (its practice pages), *Additive Manufacturing Essentials* 122, *Lifespan Development* 872, *World History* 589 and 569, *Introduction to Political Science* 308, *Introduction to Philosophy 2e* 228, the *Writing Guide* 182, *Introduction to Anthropology* 150; Economics, Statistics, Sociology, the other math books, and the other humanities and science books none.
- **Baking them is checked against cookbook's own** on its samples, which have the bank's exercises in them (Marketing 27, Information Systems 30, Data Science 14; below). **Fetched on real data, for *Principles of Marketing***, by Rob with the network (2026-10-17): all 662 of its exercises, and its 311 pages compared with its Word files (`util/cnx-compare-word.py`), all but two alike to 0.98 or more, word for word, section 1.1's to 1.0000: chapter 3's critical thinking exercises (0.9894), where the Word file runs a table's words together ("AttributesConsidered"), and the preface (0.9971), whose mission statement is worded otherwise in the two. Two questions' solutions aren't public (`exercise-solutions-not-public` in the report, MBT_Ch2_Sec3_DQ3 and MBT_Ch3_Sec3_CQ4, both free-response); the rest's are. His cache, copied here, unpacks the book offline (`--offline --exercises-cache`), which is how the fixes his run led to (Found by converting) were checked. The other books that need the bank haven't been run.

## Measured against the Word corpus

`util/cnx-compare-word.py` compares pages with OpenStax's Word files, formulas left out, a table's caption read after the table. Every page name matches but the seven noted above. Pages below 98% alike, and why:

- **Economics 3e**: 10 of 419. Errata since the Word export (a paragraph moved from 30.2 to 30.1, "Hoffman" to "Hoffmann", the preface), words Word runs together across table cells, the video's description Word adds ("Access multimedia content"), and the index's doubled-space entry.
- **Business Statistics 2e**: 13 of 169. Errata, Word's lost sentence after an `mtable` ("Let P′ = the proportion of NYC residents that smoke." is gone from Word's 9-solutions), the inline lists' letters, and 13-key-terms, where Word orders the R and R² terms differently: cookbook sorts a term's raw text, and OpenStax's assembly gives a formula's text twice (it adds a content MathML copy as an annotation) with its source's indentation, so "\n R 2 \n R 2 – Coefficient of Determination" sorts first. Not copied.
- **Sociology 3e**: 31 of 243, errata.
- **Marketing, Information Systems, Data Science**: the pages holding exercises from the bank differ (no data here); the rest match as above (Marketing's references, unit pages, chapter summaries, key terms, and index word for word).

## Measured against cookbook's own samples

openstax/cookbook tests each recipe on a sample book (`spec/recipes_spec/books/<book>/`: OpenStax's assembly of a few chapters, and the recipe's baking of them), most with exercises from the bank already in them. `util/cnx-compare-cookbook.py` bakes each with the port and compares page by page, word for word, leaving out what's different on purpose (the book's metadata, the print link beside a video, footnote numbers, inline list separators, the words for screen readers alone, the folios, cookbook's "unknown title" for a note, and where a link made of an address written as text begins and ends, which only its edges can show). All fifty-six styles match on every page of all sixty-two samples (2,129 pages): Accounting 34, Additive Manufacturing 12, Algebra 1 136, American Government 26, Anatomy 39, Anthropology 11, AP Biology 11, AP History 56, AP Physics 13 (AP Physics 2e 14), Astronomy 26, BCA 29, Biology 32, Business Ethics 31 (Business Law 16, Intellectual Property 28), Calculus 25, Chemistry 26, College Algebra 43 (Precalculus 17, Trigonometry 16, which isn't among cookbook's tests), College Algebra with Corequisite Support 15, College Physics 12, College Physics 2e 27, College Success 11 (High School College Success 8), Computer Science 16, Contemporary Math 16, Data Science 61, Economics 15, Elementary Algebra 18 (Intermediate Algebra 18, Prealgebra 14), English Composition 15, Entrepreneurship 13, Finance 29, History 12, High School Physics 17, Information Systems 23, Introduction to Business 50, Lifespan Development 26, Marketing 36, Microbiology 13, Neuroscience 45, Nursing External 40, Nursing Internal 32, Organic Chemistry 45, Philosophy 18, Political Science 56, Principles of Management 13 (Organizational Behavior 13), Psychology 58, Python 10, Sociology 27, Statistics 39, University Physics 32, World History 17, and the Polish Marketing 271, Microeconomics 28, Nursing 194, Psychology 36, and University Physics 49. Spaces alone between elements are dropped on both sides, as cookbook's tests drop them reading a sample (libxml2's noblanks), and formatting a word runs through (a subscript, italics, a foreign word's span) isn't a break, since cookbook's Polish glossaries keep a meaning's text alone and the port its markup. `CNX_COOKBOOK=<clone>` makes it a case of the CNX suite. What it found:

- **An answer key page whose solutions were all a bank exercise's was dropped** (`AnswerKeyCleaner` ported as looking for an exercise's solution only): *Principles of Data Science*'s answer key would have been empty even with its exercises. Fixed.
- **Cookbook's `post_bake` wasn't ported**: addresses written as text weren't links (above). Now they are.
- **An index's groups** came out wrong for a term opening with a letter of another script: *Biology 2e*'s "α-helix" under a group "Α" of its own, where cookbook lists it under Symbols (Design decisions). Fixed for every style.
- **An index named the page of a chapter's review by the chapter's number alone** ("3" for "3 Kluczowe pojęcia", the Polish *University Physics*' foreign index): cookbook's CompositePageElement#title takes a page's h3 title too, the title of a page in a chapter's review group, where the port took an h1 or h2. Fixed for every style.
- **The nursing-internal sample isn't well formed**, cut short without its closing tags; cookbook's Nokogiri reads it as libxml2 recovers it, and so does the comparison now, saying so.
- **OpenStax's assembly's own shapes**, which the samples are in, are read too: a table caption's text bare beside its title, a link's `[link]` placeholder, a bank question's `data-id` with no `id` (`AddInjectedExerciseId`), the edition in the head's `<title>` (Sociology's "3e" answer key). Ours writes these differently; the ports now take both.

## Measured against OpenStax's assembly

The samples check baking on OpenStax's own reading of a book; `util/cnx-compare-assembly.py` checks the unpacker's reading. Given `<slug>.assembled.xhtml` from OpenStax's `neb assemble` (nebuchadnezzar, AGPL; run here, not part of the branch, and it fetches the bank's exercises, so a book with them needs the network), it bakes that and the unpacker's assembly of the same repository with the same port and compares page by page. *Principles of Economics 3e* (419 pages), *Introduction to Sociology 3e* (243), *Principles of Management* (240), *Organizational Behavior* (234), *Introduction to Business* (267), *Principles of Financial Accounting* (260), *Principles of Managerial Accounting* (206), *Business Law I Essentials 2e* (90), and *Entrepreneurship* (170) read alike on every page. The books with bank exercises (Finance, Business Ethics, Intellectual Property, Workplace Software) weren't compared: `neb` waits on the bank, which this container can't reach. The statistics books differ only in what's deliberate (an inline list's separators and letters; a labeled item's label, `____` in a checklist, which OpenStax keeps in `data-label` for their stylesheet) and in the R and R² key terms' order (above). What it found, fixed:

- **A note's opening words were dropped** when the note holds text before its first element: a reference written as an inline note inside its citation ("Hannaway, J. (1989" lost from "Hannaway, J. (1989). *Managers Managing*…"). 1,033 notes in *Principles of Management*, 426 in *Introduction to Business*, one in the statistics bundle.
- **A table's foot came before its body**, as CALS writes it and HTML doesn't allow: *Principles of Management*'s tables carry their sources in a foot.
- **Introductory Statistics' one content MathML formula** (a "<") was lost; simple content MathML is now converted (Status, above).

## Found by converting

From *Workplace Software and Skills* and *Nutrition for Nurses* converted with validators on, *Introduction to Python Programming* read page by page, and every page of the 26 books checked for empty headings, headings in links, and blocks in paragraphs; fixed:

- **A heading's term lost its id**: the emitter replaced a baked heading's children with their words, so the note title "*Excel* in the Construction Industry", whose term the index links to, left the index's link going nowhere. Baking's spans are now taken away and what they hold kept.
- **Text in decomposed form** (an "Ö" written as O and a combining diaeresis, page 1.3), which vnu warns of. Text is composed (NFC) now, outside formulas.
- **A list's title lost its id**, so the eight links to four of them in *Introductory Statistics 2e*'s labs went nowhere (the report's `link-to-nothing`, its only rows). Kept now.
- **Empty headings**: a section's empty `<title/>` (the nursing books, Python's chapter summaries) or a note's (Finance) was an empty heading, 18 in the 26 books. Now it's no heading, its id kept on an anchor.
- **References gathered by chapter** (nursing-external's BakeReferences.v4) put each chapter's heading inside a link to the chapter, which `html-raw.lua` took apart ("raw tags dropped … a x20"). Now the heading holds the link, as a summary's does.
- **Code standing apart was written inline**, so Pandoc ran each program's lines together: 350 blocks in *Introduction to Python Programming*, 243 in *Principles of Data Science*, 24 more in *Principles of Finance*, 4 in *Foundations of Information Systems*. See the design decision above.

From the math books, every page checked as above and *College Algebra*, *Calculus Volume 1*, *Prealgebra*, *Contemporary Mathematics*, *Statistics*, and *Additive Manufacturing Essentials* converted with validators on; fixed:

- **Unpacking *Contemporary Mathematics* took seven and a half minutes** and *College Algebra* ten before that: libxml2 reads `//` as "every node below each element found so far" and merges those lists with a duplicate check, quadratic in the nodes, so a search like `.//section[…]//div[…]` over a book crawls. The searches' later steps are written `/descendant::div[…]` now, which means the same: nine seconds, and 14 for *Algebra 1*'s 977 pages.
- **Windows characters held as control codes** (U+0096 for "–", U+0092 for "’", 199 in *Statistics*, text pasted from Word and read as Latin-1 somewhere), which vnu rejects (313 errors) and an EPUB, a PDF, or a Word file shows as nothing. Each is the Windows-1252 character it stands for now, as an HTML parser makes a reference to one; the five codes Windows-1252 leaves unused stay.
- **A paragraph's title lost its id**, so the labs' links to their sections ("the Procedure section", 8 in *Statistics*) went nowhere. Kept on the bold title now.
- **A picture's media element lost its id**: a link to the photograph in *Calculus Volume 1*'s chapter opener went nowhere. Kept on the picture now. The link has no text in the source, and shows its target's address, reported as before.
- **A section with an empty title added a level to the outline**, so a heading inside skipped one (*Additive Manufacturing Essentials* 4.1, h2 to h4). Such a section is no level now, as one with no title.
- **Formulas Pandoc showed as TeX** (PANDOC-NOTES): 25 in the books converted.

From the humanities and science books, each unpacked and converted with validators on (TextbookImprover observations, above); fixed:

- **An address written as text held what a link's address can't**: a lone `%` (*Lifespan Development*'s link to an article whose text fragment ends `In%`) and braces (*Principles of Accounting*'s). Escaped in the link now (Design decisions).
- **An address written as text took the closing quotation mark after it**, linking to a host that isn't one (“https://docs.google.com.”), and an address with no host's name was linked (“http://.”, two vnu errors and two of epubcheck's in the *Writing Guide*). Text after the link now, and no link (Design decisions).

## Traps for whoever ports the next recipe

Cookbook writes its markup in templates whose line breaks fall between elements: `<span class="os-title-label">Think It Through</span>`, a line break, `<span class="os-number">1.1</span>`. That line break is text, and shows as a space ("Think It Through 1.1"); a port that appends the two spans shows "Think It Through1.1". The samples can't catch it: cookbook's `scripts/normalize` reads the expected output with `noblanks`, dropping every space between elements, before its tests compare, so `cnx-compare-cookbook.py` counts each element's edge as a break and can't tell. Found so in BakeNumberedNotes.v1's heading and BakeExercisePrefixes's prefix ("TP 1.", as openstax.org shows it); fixed. When porting, look at each template for elements on lines of their own.

A word the locale doesn't have is written out as such: a note of a kind its strings don't name is titled "unknown title for note with classes [...]", and a string missing from a Polish recipe's `pl.yml` would read "translation missing" (cookbook's I18n has no fallback to English). Look at a recipe's note classes against its locale.

And a search with two selectors, Kitchen's `search(a, b)`, returns what the first finds and then what the second does, not the two in the order they stand: *World History* numbers a page's own exercises and then its bank questions, wherever each stands. A port that takes `a, b` as one XPath union gets document order and numbers them differently; the samples catch this one.
