What the first proposal measured still holds for the reasons: the steps' right cells are formulas or pictures of them, none has a header cell, OpenStax's labels describe each as a picture (6635588, 2023, made 1,684 summaries labels), and enki's rule (7b81804, 2025-12-15; 54dc259, 2026-03-03, a summary not counting against it) marks a table with no head, no label, and no caption or title. The variant that reproduces openstax.org exactly (1,167 layout tables, and 1,368 data tables named by their labels) wasn't done, as the review advised: those labels describe pictures that are formulas now.
- **The `unnumbered` and `unstyled` tables are decided** (2026-10-18 and 19, Design decisions), from Rob's verdicts on the examples in `unstyled-tables.html`, which was made from a survey of all 12,211 tables in the 67 books: OpenStax's own HTML makes 1,871 tables layout tables that the unpacker had left data tables, by their structure alone (no `<thead>`, no `aria-label`, and no caption or title); in 1,304 of them the guess finds header cells in the body, tables of values most, which stay data tables. **Tables of values laid out across**, decided as they were: the corequisite book's 3.4 (`x | g(x) | f(g(x)) | …` over one row of values), whose header row the guess misses, and the chemistry books' rate data ("[Q]initial (M) | 0.170 | …" over "Rate | 6.68 × 10−3 | …"), whose rows' headers it misses, came to one question, since the rate data is the shape of a table of values whose header column the guess finds. Rob: "I think the header column is right for the case you describe. For the chemistry table, I read that as having three data cells that gives the rates for the corresponding initial values. But you are probably correct that there's no rule that could tell these shapes apart, so stick with the existing rule." So the guess is as it was, and a row of `table-headers.csv` settles either table. What was measured for the question: the words-over-numbers rule (`text_row_over_numbers`) wants two rows of numbers under the words; relaxed to one, it changes 33 guesses in the 1,871, giving the composition table its header row in four books and a sequence's terms theirs (`a1 | a2 | …`), 18 tables of values laid out across a header row besides their header column ("x | −2 | −4 | −6 | −8" over "h(x) | 1 | 3 | 7 | 11", "Percent grade | 0–56 | …", "Year | '94 | …"), since a number written as text with a minus sign (U+2212), or a range, isn't a number to the guess, and 8 such tables whose numbers are written with an en dash for the minus sign (`x | –4.1 | –4.01 | …`) a header row alone. In the rate data, a value in scientific notation is a number, a formula holding only ×, and a 10 with its exponent a superscript, so the guess reads the text "6.68 \times 10−3", which isn't a number: 20 such tables, 10 in each chemistry book's exercises (*Chemistry 2e*'s chapter 12, *Atoms First*'s chapter 17). `NUMERIC`, the pattern a number must match in the census, takes the minus sign now (2026-10-19, item 29 under Fixes for `main`), which changes one guess of the 12,221 and none in the Word corpus. **The inquiry organizers** are decided (Design decisions). **Rating scales**: 7, all in *Organizational Behavior*'s skills exercises, each statement beside 1 to 5 under "Strongly Disagree … Strongly Agree" or the like, 5 the guess reads no header in and 2 a header row and column; the other books' scales declare a head. Too few for a rule of their own, as Rob put it, so they're left as they are; a row of `table-headers.csv` makes one a layout table. **Tables baking leaves unwrapped** are decided as tables in a container are (2026-10-19, Design decisions).
- **A unit of two letters nothing marks** is still italic letters (item 30 under Fixes for `main`, 2026-10-19). Of the 988 identifiers of two letters in OpenStax's public repositories that are neither set upright nor applied as a function, most are products (`πr` 35, `rω` 35, `ρv` 33, `mc` 31 in *College Physics*), and some are units: *Contemporary Mathematics*' `cm` 65, `dm` 21, `km` 20, `mm` 15, `mL` 12, and `kg` 10, the nursing books' `mg` 39, `mL` 28, `kg` 16, and `lb` 8, and *Mikroekonomia*'s `zł` 7. A list of units would set most of those right, but not without a product written like one, *College Physics*' `w = mg` (m times g, 3 of the 55 `mg`) and `Q = mLf` (3 of the 43 `mL`). Left as it is, Rob's decision (2026-10-19): "Re: two-letter units, let's leave that as it is. I don't think we can find a better compromise right now."
- **The contents' wide page numbers** (TextbookImprover observations). A page number wider than the 1.55em the book class leaves for it in the contents stood past the text's edge: 18 of *Introduction to Sociology 3e*'s contents' lines, by up to 8.7 points, when its front matter's numbers were listed. Since item 35 Sociology's contents fit, and since item 38, which makes books long enough to reach page 1,000, the contents leave 2.5em, four digits' room or a roman xxviii's, which moves every book's contents a little (Rob had been asked and hadn't chosen; done with the default page, said in the reply).
- **OpenStax's page for an OpenStax book's PDF, decided** (2026-10-19, late), and the PDF target's default for every book (2026-10-20, item 38): OpenStax's paper and margins with 12-point type, lines 1.5 apart, ragged right, and the footer on every page of every format (item 36). Reading a layout from a publisher's own PDF is on the roadmap, not done (Rob: "We can note reading format from an existing PDF as a future enhancement and skip it for now, since we don't need it for the CNX project"). Of what WCAG 1.4.8 (AAA) asks of a page, it misses two things, which the configuration page says how to give, at their cost: lines of 80 characters or fewer on average (the default's average 88.4; 13.5-point type, or side margins of 1.35 inches, 1.3 coming to just over 80), and paragraphs set further apart (1em between them), which Rob hasn't been asked about before. Rob (2026-10-20): not the default; a page for a book that's to meet the criterion instead, which item 40 is, counting spacing from single spacing, as W3C's explanation does, footnotes and captions spaced as the text is. Counted from the type's size since, as W3C's technique C21 counts it (item 46): "It sounds like 1.5 times the type's size is defensible, so go with that."
- **OpenStax's notes as boxes, done** (item 41): each headed by its kind and its own title in one heading, as Rob chose. What's left of the look is the label's: in a span of class `os-note-label` now, which a stylesheet could set smaller, or in OpenStax's uppercase sans, and none does.
- **OpenStax's typefaces, decided** (2026-10-20, item 47): an OpenStax book's text in its design's face at 12 points, its headings in the face's bold, as Rob chose. Open: Noto Sans Math for the cardboard and corn books' formulas, once a version with a MATH table is installed, its line in each such book's `pdf-layout.yaml`, commented out. The default page's face for every book, decided too (2026-10-27, item 85): Latin Modern at 12 points being under the x-height UKAAF gives 12-point clear print, Rob asked for a suggestion, IBM Plex Serif with IBM Plex Math was suggested, with XCharter and XCharter Math if pages matter more, each measured and built as samples (`typefaces-research.md`, section 8, in the project), and he chose Plex.
- **The default's faces, IBM Plex, done** (2026-10-27, item 85), as Rob chose, with Plex Sans and Plex Mono beside Plex Serif and Plex Math, and code at the text's size, a block of code too wide for its line set smaller to fit it, as Rob chose (item 91), where it had been at 0.858 of the text's size, each character as wide as Latin Modern Mono's (item 86). For Rob: the AAA page's type, 13.5 points, kept, where in Plex 12.5 points meets the criterion's 80 characters (77 a line, 73 to 81), and a book on it is shorter (the two sections measured, 36 pages, to 40 at 13.5 points and 33 on the default page). Left: the PDF filter's counts of a table's and a formula's widths were calibrated in Latin Modern (`pdf-target.lua`'s `MATH_ROOM`, and its count of a formula, a capital letter one and a half, measured against Latin Modern Math's widths), where Plex Math's digits are 20 percent wider and its operators 11 percent narrower (a separate review's measure), and the PDF suite's fit and two-column books name Latin Modern, so no check measures the counts, or the fit's macros, in Plex; the fallback to Latin Modern is built whole only where a book names no face, the other cases checked as LaTeX and as the warning's words; and pypdf runs an untagged PDF's words together in Plex, where pdftotext and MuPDF don't, which matters to a check that reads one (the notes book's untagged copy and the untagged book name Latin Modern for it).
- **A Spanish book is baked with Spanish words, done** (2026-10-27, item 82, which says what's left in English). As it was found (2026-10-20), Rob: "Producing English and Spanish is enough." The port has cookbook's English and Polish strings (`cnx.bake.strings`) and not its Spanish, which cookbook has for the common strings (`lib/locales/es.yml`) and for twelve recipes, calculus, chemistry, precalculus, statistics, and u-physics among them, the styles of OpenStax's Spanish books; none of cookbook's sample books is Spanish. *Introducción a la estadística* (`osbooks-introduccion-estadistica`, cloned here without its media), unpacked, is 200 pages baked as statistics, and every word the port adds is English: "Figure" 346 times and "Table" 639 in captions and links, "Try It" 212 and "Example" 37, "Solution" 207 and "Problem" 202 as headings, each chapter's pages titled and named "Key Terms", "Chapter Review", "Formula Review", "Practice", "Homework", and "References" (`1-key-terms`), the notes "Using the TI-83, 83+, 84, 84+ Calculator" (70) and "Collaborative Exercise" (17), and the contents' "Chapter 1 Muestreo y datos". Its footer is OpenStax's English words, where cookbook's Spanish folio is "Acceso gratis en openstax.org", and its captions' word would want `captions.figure_prefixes` and `captions.table_prefixes` ("Figura", "Tabla"). Porting `es.yml` as `pl.yml` was ported is the next step, checked against OpenStax's own Spanish pages, there being no sample to check it on; `docs/cnx-input.md` says how (item 49), and it's not done. OpenStax's other Spanish repositories are `osbooks-calculo-bundle`, `osbooks-fisica-universitaria-bundle`, `osbooks-quimica-bundle`, and `osbooks-precalculo`, not cloned here then; they are now, and their nine books bake in Spanish too (item 82).
- **A tight list's spacing in a PDF, decided** (2026-10-21): left as it is, as Rob asked ("leave it as is"). A list of short items Pandoc writes with `\tightlist`, which sets the space between paragraphs to none inside it, so on the default page, which sets paragraphs apart by space, a tight list has no more space above it than a line (18.06 points, baseline to baseline) and a paragraph's below it (25.28), where a loose list has a paragraph's both above and below, and a page or an EPUB sets either as the loose one. On the AAA page, 20.17 and 33.62. All 524 of *Introduction to Sociology 3e*'s lists are tight.
- **A row one cell across a table with a header column, decided** (2026-10-21; TextbookImprover observations). *Prealgebra 2e*'s Table 3.1, two columns of cases, each pair of rows closed by a note one cell across, is guessed `first-column` and split at its first note, so the cases' first column are row headers, the first note, which sums up the rows above it, is the second part's caption, and the last note a row header beside an empty cell, its span cut as Pandoc cuts it (PANDOC-NOTES). Rob: "`none` or `layout` seems reasonable." So the table is declared, not guessed by a rule: its row of `table-headers.csv`, as `table-headers-new.csv` drafts it, with `none` and no `split-at`, `4a0cd976ea59a632fefc315d0b30069d38784837479ba54e7c7f36c613050d60,none,,,,3-2-add-integers.html,Table 3.1 Addition of Positive and Negative Integers,5 + 3 | -5 + (-3),,`. Measured with each (`main` at bec2e48, the page converted alone): one table of seven rows, its two notes across both columns, no header cell, and its caption, `none` a data table in its scroll region and `layout` a layout table (`role="presentation"`); `none` keeps it a table, as OpenStax numbers it, and as the unpacker keeps any table with a caption of its own. The row covers the table's copy in the chapter's Key Concepts too, the same table and so the same key, which the unpacked page marks a layout table (`role="presentation"`): with `none` it's a data table as well, and with `layout` a layout table still (measured, that page converted with each). Guessed so since item 51, `none` and whole, so the row says what the guess says.

Fitted since item 50, their spaces narrowed and their type set smaller, two of that chapter's 28 formulas too wide are still too wide at three quarters of their size, running into the margin on their right, two of section 1.2's, by 36 points and by 0.6. Set as tables whose reasons wrap since item 52, as Rob chose: the steps 36 points too wide, Example 9 (d), are a table in the text's type, its spaces narrowed, and the formula 0.6 points too wide, Example 12 (e), a step's two sides with no words, which isn't made a table, still is. Since item 56, by LaTeX's measure, none of the chapter's 51 tables of steps is fitted: of the 20 set across the line, 10 are at their own width, and the other 10 across it in the text's type, Example 9 (d) with its spaces as they were and the three that were at 92 to 96 percent of their size among them; the formula 0.6 points too wide still is.
- **Display style in a table of steps, measured, then kept** (2026-10-24; Rob, of item 52's offer: "Yes to measuring before deciding"; then "Go ahead and implement the fix for display style", item 63). OpenStax sets some of a worked example's steps in display style, an `mstyle` with `displaystyle="true"` in a cell of the `mtable`, which Pandoc's reader drops (PANDOC-NOTES), so every output sets them in text style, a fraction's parts and an integral's limits small. Measured on *Calculus Volume 2*'s chapter 3, whose 28 tables of steps hold 93 formulas in display style, in 87 of their 213 cells of math, 26 of the tables at least one: built as the chapter is now, and with `\displaystyle` before each of the 93, `main`'s code otherwise. In text style, 10 tables are at their own width and 18 across the line, one with its spaces narrowed and 5 in smaller type, the least at 79.5 percent of its size and the others at 93 to 97; in display style, 9 at their own width and 19 across the line, 2 narrowed and 6 in smaller type, the least at 79.5 percent and the others at 87 to 96. The chapter is 121 pages, not 120, and builds as fast (401 s, 402). *College Algebra 2e* has one such table, in its answer key, and *Precalculus 2e* none; and the unpacker reads display style set on an `mtable` itself as its cells', as *Fizyka dla szkół wyższych*'s three volumes set 132, 64, and 59 `mtable`s and *Algebra 1* 15 (a census of the repositories' CNXML). Keeping it would take the unpacker marking a cell whose MathML is in display style, the PDF filter writing `\displaystyle` before its formula, and the pages' MathML its `mstyle` again, since texmath writes none for `\displaystyle` (PANDOC-NOTES). For Rob to decide.
- **A rule under each of the dev-math books' steps, decided and done** (2026-10-24, item 64). OpenStax's own PDFs draw a gray rule under every row of a table it classes `unstyled`, the dev-math books', *Precalculus 2e*'s, the corequisite book's, the calculus books', and *Algebra 1*'s designs alike (0.09rem, #757474), so a worked example's steps there are ruled off one from the next, where the unpacker's own steps, a formula in OpenStax's source, have no rule. Item 64 draws the dev-math books' with none, as offered and as item 58's are. Offered: a light rule under each row of a table of the class `steps`, OpenStax's way, in a PDF (a rule between the longtable's rows, in gray) and on a page and in an EPUB (each cell's bottom border); Rob's call. Measured (2026-10-25): *Prealgebra 2e*'s third chapter, its 110 tables of steps given a 0.6-point rule in OpenStax's gray under each row, with 2 points of space above and below it (`r19/q/q1rules.py`; OpenStax's is 0.09rem, three quarters of a point at its PDFs' 11-pixel type), is 144 pages, where it's 141 without, and builds as fast (458 s, 498). Shown to Rob with Example 3.32, whose pictures stand between two reasons without rules, in the PDF and on the page (a 1-pixel bottom border on each cell). Rob (2026-10-27): "yes, add the rules where OpenStax also has them", and, asked how far, "Everywhere OpenStax rules": done in item 88, every table OpenStax's math books rule row by row, in every format but Word.
- **Display style in a formula in running text, and in an array, decided and done** (2026-10-24, item 63). OpenStax sets 4,723 of the 276,493 formulas in running text in its public repositories in display style where it changes something (a fraction, a large operator, limits), 3,875 of them in the calculus books, 508 in *University Physics*, and 72 in the dev-math books, and 22 of its 7,443 display formulas, which are in display style anyway; and 471 more that are an `mtable` alone, 35 of them item 63's steps and the rest arrays, whose cells LaTeX sets in text style whatever the formula's style (a census of the CNXML, `r18/scripts/inlinedisp.py`). Every output sets them in text style. The unpacker could mark a formula in running text as it marks a step's, at the cost of a taller line wherever one stands. Measured (2026-10-25) on *Calculus Volume 2*'s chapter 3: 629 of its formulas in running text are marked so where it changes something, and the 547 found in the chapter's LaTeX as written (the rest ending a sentence or written otherwise), set in display style (`r19/q/q23marks.py`), make it 132 pages, where it's 121, the line holding one taller, an integral's sign and a fraction drawn full size. Shown to Rob in the PDF and on a page, section 3.7's "so if … converges, then so does …". Rob's call. Rob (2026-10-27): "I think our default should be not to use display style in running text, but we could add a configuration option to honor it": done in item 89, `math.display_style_in_text`, off by default, for any HTML page. An array's cells stay in text style, as LaTeX sets them whatever the formula's style.
- **A step's display style kept to its part, decided: left as it is** (2026-10-24, item 63). OpenStax sets the integral sign alone in display style in 54 of the 97 parts of *Calculus Volume 2*'s steps so set; once Pandoc has read a formula, the part can't be told, so the whole formula is set so. Keeping it to its part would take the unpacker writing the formula's TeX itself, the part in `{\displaystyle …}` or a lone integral sign as `\mathop{\displaystyle\int}`, which `cnx.mathtex` could (it writes the TeX Pandoc writes, measured on 115,122 formulas), as an annotation the HTML reader reads in the MathML's place, and the page's MathML carried through from the source, since texmath writes none; not built. Measured (2026-10-25) on the same chapter, the LaTeX alone: of the cells of steps marked so, the 84 found in its LaTeX as written, 45 marked on their integral signs alone set so, each sign `\mathop{\displaystyle\int}` and the rest in text style, and 39 whole, as now (`r19/q/q23marks.py`): 121 pages either way. Shown to Rob with module m53656's integration by parts three ways, before item 63, now, and with OpenStax's marks. Rob's call. Rob (2026-10-27): "Let's just document this and leave it alone": `cnx-input.md` says so (item 90).
- **A formula with an empty line in a `markdown` target** (2026-10-23, found for item 55). Pandoc's Markdown writer writes the empty line texmath writes for a one-column `mtable`'s empty last row inside the formula's dollar signs, where the Markdown reader ends the paragraph, so the formula reads back as raw TeX and text (`$$|_{`, a `matrix` as raw TeX, `}^{`, another, and `}$$`), not a formula, in a display or in text (PANDOC-NOTES; measured with 3.12.1 on an evaluation bar's limits). An `asciidoc` target's passthrough block reads back whole. Of the four books measured for item 55, three have such formulas, 39 of them. `markdown-source.lua` could write the empty row as `pdf-target.lua` now does, an empty group; done (item 59), as Rob asked, and the `asciidoc` target's formula in text, written on one line, with it.
